# Sources

Everything this repo's numbers rest on, and what we know exists but have not read. Add to it
whenever you read something. A figure marked `documented` anywhere in the repo should trace to an
entry here.

Keys used in the hardware records: **primary** is a NASA or manufacturer document read directly;
**secondary** is anything else.

## Project Mercury

### Read

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *The Mercury-Redstone Project*, NASA TM X-53107, MSFC, 1964 | [PDF (Colorado State mirror)](https://www.engr.colostate.edu/~marchese/rockets15/projectmercury.pdf) | The booster: section lengths (Figure 4-1), weights, skin gauges, tank volumes, control limits. The pad: service structure, white room, mobile aerial tower, countdown (sections 7.2 to 7.5). Flight results (section 8, Table 8-1) | Figure 4-1 is not to scale. Section 8.3.1's prose has wrong dates and times; use its tables. Figure 7-6's "600 ft" is a distance, not a scale bar |
| *Postlaunch Report for Mercury-Redstone No. 3*, NASA Space Task Group, 16 June 1961 | [PDF](https://tothemoon.im-ldi.com/download_document?file_name=mercury_redstone_3_postlaunch_report.pdf&project=MERCURY) | **The authority for MR-3**: event times, trajectory, loads, capsule masses, centre of gravity and inertias, landing bag, hatch | |
| *Results of the First U.S. Manned Suborbital Space Flight*, NASA / NIH / NAS, 6 June 1961 | [NTRS 19720065957](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19720065957/downloads/19720065957.pdf) | Flight summary, spacecraft description, the beryllium shield | |
| Hammack and Heberlig, *The Mercury-Redstone Program*, ARS paper, October 1961 | [NTRS 20150018552](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/20150018552/downloads/20150018552.pdf) | Shingle and pressure-vessel gauges, thruster ratings, cabin pressure, parachutes. Figures 2, 17 and 18: dimensioned drawings of the capsule and the whole vehicle (131.57 and 311.55 in; fin span 152 in) | **Pages are cited as printed on them** (the scan's page n + 1 is the page numbered n; earlier citations that counted otherwise were put right in November 2026). Figure 17's total (996.07 in) is 4.5 in short of Figure 18's 83.38 ft |
| *Mercury Project Summary*, NASA SP-45, 1963 | [NTRS 19630012071](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19630012071/downloads/19630012071.pdf) | Table 3-III: nominal ratings of every solid motor. Flight durations | Nominal figures; the manufacturer's differ slightly |
| McDonnell Aircraft, *Project Mercury Familiarization Manual*, SEDR 104, rev. May 1962 | [PDF](https://sourceforge.net/projects/mscorbaddon/files/MercuryFamiliarizationManual20May1962.pdf/download) | Escape, jettison, posigrade and retro motors; the tower; antenna fairing; recovery compartment; suit pressure. **Figure 1-3 (page 1-5) is the stations diagram**: every capsule and tower station | The orbital-capsule edition: not No. 7's hatch or windows |
| Murphy, *Flight Test Aerodynamic Heating Data for the Afterbody of the Project Mercury Spacecraft*, NASA CR-649, 1967 | [NTRS 19670020040](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19670020040/downloads/19670020040.pdf) | The capsule's stated geometry: 74.5 in, 80 in, 20°, 32 by 20 in cylinder, canister, shingle gauges | Gives the canister as 24 in tapering to 20 in, which looks narrow against photographs |
| Swenson, Grimwood and Alexander, *This New Ocean*, NASA SP-4201, 1966 | [PDF, whole book](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/02/sp-4201.pdf) | Hatch history, the 10 in ports, launch-morning times | Its prose gives 15 min 22 s; its table has 15:28 and 5,134 mph, which the telemetry does not support |
| NASM object record A19620021000, *Capsule, Mercury, MR-3* | [airandspace.si.edu](https://airandspace.si.edu/collection-objects/capsule-mercury-mr-3/nasm_A19620021000) | Where the spacecraft is now; its size as displayed | Repeats 15:28 and 5,180 mph |
| NASM, photographs of the flown capsule's interior and exterior, 2021: NASM2021-00147 (the panel, left console, fuse panel and upper port from the couch), -00153 (through the hatch opening: couch, chest parachute, hand controller, the back of the panel), -00160, -00161, -00162, -00165, -00168 and A19620021000_DSH01 (outside, read at 3000 px: both ports, the hatch opening, the periscope's door and objective, the shingles, the roll jets' plates, the paint) | on the object record above | **What the cabin's faces, colours and places are read from** (spacecraft.md, "Cabin"), and since November 2026 where the hatch, ports, periscope and roll jets are round the cone and which way the shingles' beads run (spacecraft.md, "Round the cone") | The capsule as kept, not as flown: the hatch and the humidity gauge are gone, the paint is flaking, labels may be later. -00147 is a fisheye. "Usage conditions apply": looked at, not copied into the repo |
| *Postlaunch Report for Mercury-Redstone No. 3* (above), Figures 3.1-1, 3.1-2 and 10.1-1, and the pilot's debriefing | as above | **Capsule 7's own panel** (3.1-2: main panel, left and right consoles, every legend), what differed from later capsules (3.1-1: upper and lower windows, the latch hatch's handles), every switch as found after the flight (10.1-1: the fuse panel's list, the tower-jettison ring pulled, one O2 emergency flow light, a cabin humidity gauge); cabin lights "on full white light"; "about 35 ft/sec" on the descent indicator | The figures are layouts and perspectives: nothing inside the cabin is dimensioned. It cites STG Working Paper 148 for the systems, which has not been found |
| *Results of the First U.S. Manned Suborbital Space Flight* (above), pp. 19 to 24 and Figures 5 and 7 | as above | The panel again (Figure 7, with two gauges in the second row); green and red sequence lights; the three-axis hand controller in the right hand and the abort handle in the left; two multi-pane portholes; the three cameras and where each was; the environmental system "below the astronaut's couch" | A coarse scan: small legends are marginal |
| McDonnell, *Project Mercury Capsule Flight Operations Manual*, SEDR 109-7 (capsule 7), 15 August 1960 | [NTRS 20160013609](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/20160013609) | Capsule 7's checklists, naming every control; Figures 1-1 to 1-3: consoles, main panel, fuse panel | Nine months before the flight: it has a LAUNCH OXY light, a CO2 gauge and water lights that did not fly. Poor OCR: the fuse panel's figure could not be read for its layout |
| SEDR 104 (above), sections 1-6, 2-3, 2-7, 10-19, 12 and Figures 1-6, 12-2 to 12-4 | as above | The periscope in full (objective "approximately 180 degrees", two powers, four filters, the display's reticles, Figure 12-3's 14.5 degrees); the couch's make; "channeled frames" lining the wall; four cabin floodlights and where; where equipment sat | **The orbital capsule's**, not No. 7's: used for the cabin only where spacecraft.md says so |
| Hammack and Heberlig (above), p. 5 | as above | The periscope's "8-inch circular display"; 1,900 and 80 nautical miles of ground at its two powers | Its panel figure is MR-4's |
| SEDR 104 of 1962 (above), 2-3, 12-2, 12-5, 12-7, 12-16, 12-18, 12-19, Figures 12-1 (two sheets), 12-2, 12-4, 10-9 and 10-12; SEDR 104 of December 1959 (above), Tables 10-2 and 10-3; SEDR 109-7 (above), 1-9, 1-13, 1-20, 2-18, 2-19, 2-24; *Postlaunch Report for Mercury-Redstone No. 3* (above), pp. 47, 73 to 76, 89 and section 10.7.2; *Results of the First* (above), pp. 10 and 111; *Results of the Second* (below), pp. 6, 43 to 45 and Figure 2-3 | as above | **The periscope as a mechanism**: only the objective cartridge moves, on a motor and gear box with roller guides; "a connecting link between the objective cartridge and the periscope door allows the door to move in unison"; Figure 12-1 draws the link and the door as a pan with one toothed long edge; "PERISCOPE EXT. (5 SEC)"; a 60 W motor; the programme (retracted at umbilical disconnect, out at separation, in thirty seconds after the retropack, out again for the descent; capsule 7 wired to put it out at 21,000 ft); by hand, a ratchet handle on the upper housing through a clutch, with the PERISCOPE switch-fuse off, and what the fuse restored then does; the RETRACT pilot light; No. 11's RETRACT SCOPE telelight and AUTO, MAN switch; "if the periscope does not completely retract, the periscope door will not close causing the cabin to overheat during re-entry" | No travel in inches, no hinge, latch or seal, and no drawing of the linkage with a dimension on it: the model's linkage is ours (spacecraft.md, "The periscope's door"). What the display shows drawn in is not stated. Read November 2026 |
| McDonnell Aircraft, *Project Mercury Familiarization Manual*, SEDR 104, 15 December 1959 | [archive.org, nasa_techdoc_19790077942](https://archive.org/details/nasa_techdoc_19790077942) | **The earlier manual, which covers the porthole capsules** (2 to 8). 10-44 and 10-47 (p. 10-23) and Figure 10-7: "Two four watt fluorescent floodlights provide interior lighting for the capsule", "self-contained", white, one on each hand, on the 115 V a-c fans bus through a switch that turns them OFF or ON. 2-24 (pp. 2-23 to 2-26): the ports of capsules 2 to 4, four panes sealed in a titanium frame attached to the cabin wall and an outer pane, curved to the cone, in a titanium frame attached to the outer skin; adjustable shades | Capsule 7 is not named in either passage: the lighting is "capsules 2 thru 8", the ports are capsules 2 to 4. Figure 10-10 is missing from the scan; no drawing of a fitting or of a port's frame is dimensioned. Read November 2026 |
| *Postlaunch Report for Mercury-Redstone No. 3* (above), section 7.3 (p. 65) and Figures 3.1-2, 10.1-1; *Results of the First U.S. Manned Suborbital Space Flight* (above), p. 16; *Results of the Second U.S. Manned Suborbital Space Flight* (below), Figure 2-3 and p. 45; SEDR 104 of 1962 (above), 10-18 to 10-21, 12-5, 12-23 | as above | **The cabin lights and their switches**: No. 7's CABIN LITES, OFF above and ON below, at the outboard top of the left console, found ON; "his eyes were not dark-adapted since the cabin lights were on full white light"; No. 7's ports "of multipane construction" in "heat-resistant glass"; No. 11's CABIN LIGHTS, BOTH, L.H. ONLY, OFF, and "cabin lights are both"; the orbital capsule's floodlights "to the right and left and above the Astronaut", its instruments "lighted externally by cabin floodlights", the periscope's reticle lit by a lamp of its own | The printed page and the page of the scan differ: 7.3's sentence is printed p. 65 and is page 76 of the scan, and "multipane" is printed p. 16 and page 19 of the scan. Older citations of these two documents in this repository may give the scan's page. No. 7 had no dimmer and no red light in anything read |
| NASA SP-45 (above), p. 282 | as above | "The field of view through the periscope was 172 degrees", "oriented to the earth's nadir when the spacecraft was at a pitch attitude of -14 degrees" | The orbital capsules |
| *This New Ocean* (above), pp. 355 and 367 | as above | The periscope "two feet in front of him"; "the two 10-inch side ports"; the latch mechanism's 69 lb | A history, not a drawing: secondary |
| NASA photographs S61-03257 (an early Mercury cabin in colour, capsule not named), S61-03645 and S88-31374 (Shepard going aboard and in capsule 7) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov) | The cabin's colours when new; the hatch's sill; three grab handles on the hatch | Captions are loose; which face of the hatch the handles are on cannot be told |
| NASA, *Project Mercury pressure suits* fact sheet (MA-8) | [PDF](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/static/history/40thmerc7/pressuresuits.pdf) | The suit, described 18 months after MR-3 | Later configuration than Shepard flew |
| collectSPACE, *Shepard's Freedom 7 spacesuit* | [collectspace.com](https://www.collectspace.com/news/news-050521a-shepard-freedom-7-spacesuit-60th-smithsonian.html) | The flown suit as conserved | |
| Smithsonian NASM, *Pressure Suit, Mercury, Shepard, MR-3, Flown*, A19770563000 | [airandspace.si.edu](https://airandspace.si.edu/collection-objects/pressure-suit-mercury-shepard-mr-3-flown/nasm_A19770563000) | The flown suit's photographs: the NASA insignia and "A. B. SHEPARD" name tag on the right chest, which way the zipper runs; neck ring 9 1/2 in | Shown as conserved, without the parachute harness. Only the insignia, tag and zipper were checked against the model; the 3D scan was not opened |
| NASA photographs of 5 May 1961: S61-02727, S61-02775, S61-02792, GPN-2000-001023 | [Wikimedia Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Alan_Shepard_pouso.jpg) and siblings | What the suit carried on the day: the insignia and name tag (S61-02727, colour, on the carrier's deck), the harness over it (S61-02792) | Commons dates some of the suit-up frames to April's rehearsals; the four named here are captioned 5 May |
| Wikipedia: Mercury-Redstone 3, Mercury-Redstone Launch Vehicle, Navy Mark IV, Cape Canaveral Launch Complex 5 and 6 | [MR-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mercury-Redstone_3) and siblings | Pad coordinates, launch counts, the suit, pointers to primaries | MR-3's timeline table is unreliable (separation at 02:24, apogee at 05:00) |
| Stephens, NASA TM X-493, 1961 | [NTRS 19650003098](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19650003098/downloads/19650003098.pdf) | Big Joe boilerplate only | Not the production spacecraft |
| McCutcheon, *The Redstone Engine*, AEHS | [enginehistory.org](https://enginehistory.org/Museums/USSRC/USSRC_Redstone.shtml) | A-7 lineage | Its A-7 performance line conflicts with TM X-53107 |
| National Historic Landmark nomination, Cape Canaveral Air Force Station (NRHP 84003872), item 7 pp. 3 to 5 | [NPGallery PDF](https://npgallery.nps.gov/NRHP/GetAsset/NHLS/84003872_text) | LC-5/6's history; the Redstone service tower quoted from the Corps of Engineers' *History of the Canaveral District 1950-1971*: A-frame mast, cantilevered platforms encircling the missile, "towered 140 feet", self-propelled on rails, 15-ton hammerhead crane; the arrow-shaped blockhouse | Secondary, quoting a primary. Its "painted blue" is the 1980s museum scheme, not 1961; it says the 5/6 and 26B structures are "identical", which Noble contradicts |
| Cape Canaveral Space Force Museum, *Cape Canaveral – A Noble Endeavor*, rev 1.5.2 | [PDF](https://ccspacemuseum.org/wp-content/uploads/displays/LC26gantry/Cape_Canaveral_Noble_Endeavor_v1.5.2.pdf) | The Noble Co. structures: LC-4's moved to 5/6 in 1955 and dedicated to pad 5 for Mercury; pad 6's tyre-mounted one (1959); LC-26's mast about 23 ft shorter; elevators each side, office at the base | Secondary; family photographs. Disagrees with the museum's gantry page on the 5/6 structure's fate |
| Cape Canaveral Space Force Museum, *Launch Complex 26 Gantry*, and *Launch Complex 5/6* pages | [LC-26 gantry](https://ccspacemuseum.org/artifacts/launch-complex-26-gantry/), [LC-5/6](https://ccspacemuseum.org/facilities/launch-complex-5-6/), [pads](https://ccspacemuseum.org/artifacts/launch-complex-5-6-launch-pads/) | The surviving twin: "111 feet 9 inches to the top rail", four motorised rail trucks, five platforms (four adjustable), mast folds down; a photograph of its Corps of Engineers blueprint; dated photographs of MR-1A, MR-3 and MR-4 at pad 5; the launch ring and "pad scale" plate kept at pad 6 | Secondary. The blueprint photograph (900 px) is too small to read its dimensions: ask the museum for a scan |
| Hammack and Heberlig (above), pp. 20 to 22 and Figures 22 and 23 (NASA S-61-1930, S-61-1931) | as above | The countdown's structure and cherry-picker moves; the white room "separated down the middle when the service structure was opened"; the structure closed round the vehicle, and opened and moved back | |
| *Results of the First U.S. Manned Suborbital Space Flight* (above), p. 77 and Figure 1(a) | as above | MR-3's morning: gantry ascended 05:15, removed 06:37; "HOLDING: GANTRY IN" and "GANTRY AWAY" during the long hold | |
| NASA SP-45 (above), Figure 4-9 | as above | MR-3 with the cherry picker at the hatch and the structure withdrawn | |
| OpenStreetMap, Cape Canaveral LC-5/6 (LC-5 relation 16884747, LC-6 way 195709356, blockhouse way 195709358, rails, "Mercury-Redstone Launch Pedestal" way 1219678282) | [openstreetmap.org](https://www.openstreetmap.org/relation/16884747) | Measured layout: the hardstand 195 to 202 ft square, rails along 25/205 deg true, pad 6 520 ft away at 025 deg, the blockhouse 351 ft from pad 5 | Traced from modern imagery: a few feet of error; some features (deluge monitors, revetments) may be later than 1961 |
| NASA photographs S61-03158, 6100884, S61-01927, S61-01928, S61-02407, S61-02408, S61-01908 (MR-3); S63-02651 (**MR-1**, 21 November 1960: see the note on it below); S63-22731, S61-01942 (MR-2) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/) | The pad as built and on launch day: structure, colours, rails, launcher, masts, cherry picker, ground equipment. S61-02407 (ignition), S61-01927 and S61-02408, read again at full size, give what stood behind the launcher: two open-topped white blast screens, an orange tank behind the lower, a long white tank on a trailer behind the taller with three floodlights and a switch box on it, a chevron-painted drum, sandbags on the grass, and the slab's far edge in line with the screens' feet. 6100884 and S61-01928, along the rails, give the slab's edge on the structure's side | Captions' dates are cataloguing dates; S61-03158's moment is not given. Oblique views with no scale but the booster: sizes and places of the ground equipment are estimates |

| NASA photographs of Freedom 7 in 1961: on the pad (the colour "egress test at Pad 5", 21 April; GPN-2000-001006, -001009; the launch, Commons 55249616037) and on the deck of the *Lake Champlain* after recovery, 5 May ("Astronaut Alan Shepard inspects his capsule on U.S. Champlain", MSFC-6101191, MSFC-6417078, jsc2007e046478, Commons 18495284395) | Wikimedia Commons | **The paint as flown** (spacecraft.md, "Paint"): UNITED STATES in two lines beside the periscope's door, FREEDOM over 7 low beside the hatch, no flag on the sides seen; the umbilical's arm beside UNITED STATES on the pad | Photographs: every place on them is estimated, and which side of the capsule each shows is read off the features it shares with the capsule as kept |
| NASA photographs GPN-2000-001008, GPN-2000-001006, MSFC-6414825, 61-MR4-65, and the colour "egress test at Pad 5" of 21 April 1961 | Wikimedia Commons | The mobile aerial tower ("cherry picker") and its truck, the white room's colour, the pad at liftoff | Photographs: forms and colours, no dimensions |
| TM X-53107 (above): 4.4 and Figure 4-8 ("Mechanics of Tilting Program": the pitch gyro, "Plane in which tilting occurs", "Direction of flight"); 7.1.3, L-25 ("Booster arrival and erection on launcher. Cable masts erected, vertical alignment, and positioned to flight azimuth"); 7.2.3.2, T−625 ("Check azimuth alignment of LEV-3") and T−130 ("Perform launch azimuth check"); 7.4.3 (the azimuths: 105° to MR-3, 100° for MR-4); Figure 4-25 (fins I and II over the launcher); Figure 7-6 (the mobile tower from T−55, drawn about 64 ft from the launcher and some 10 ft to the structure's side of square) | as above | **How the vehicle stood on the pad**: turned on the launcher until its pitch plane lay on the azimuth, since it did not roll in flight. lc-5.md, "Which way round the vehicle stood"; `launcherTurn` in places/cape-canaveral/lc-5.parts.ts; `site.aim` | It says the vehicle was positioned to the azimuth, not which of its sides lay down range: that is from the capsule's own axes (the periscope toward the Earth after a turnaround in yaw: *Results*). Section 3 and 8.3.2 have MR-3's azimuth as 102°, against 7.4.3's 105° and the Postlaunch Report's 105.23° heading |
| Fort Hays State University, *Project Mercury* collection, item 306 (F16_017, print 220: "U.S. Air Force photo ... LOD 61-6084, 7-3-61, CCMTA, NASA, PAD, Pad 5 looking south"), the full scan read in November 2026 for the vehicle | [scholars.fhsu.edu/mercury/306](https://scholars.fhsu.edu/mercury/306) | **MR-4's booster against the rails, on 3 July 1961**: taken from pad 6's gantry straight down the track with the structure round the vehicle, it shows the booster's UNITED STATES 19° to 24° right of the camera's line, so facing about 004° true; with the lettering on a fin's meridian 222° round from the hatch that turns the vehicle 67° ± 6° from square (lc-5.md) | One view; the booster's right limb is behind a pole, so the body's middle is good to a few pixels in 67. Its back (item 307) gives the date and the caption |
| Fort Hays State University, *Project Mercury* collection, items 308, 310, 312, 314 ("Capsule Checkout on Pad 5 - Level 3", prints 219 to 216, 30 June 1961), read in November 2026 for the hatch against the white room | [scholars.fhsu.edu/mercury](https://scholars.fhsu.edu/mercury/) | The white room with capsule 11's hatch open: a white work table at the hatch's sill, men on headsets, glazed walls a few feet behind them, clipboards on a panelled wall | They show the room close to and nothing that fixes a wall against the rails, so they do not give the hatch's bearing. The table is not modelled: it is not in the photographs of the pilots' insertion |
| Fort Hays State University, *Project Mercury* collection (USAF/RCA photographs made for NASA at the Cape), items F16_015, F16_016, F16_017 (prints 221, 222, 220) and F19_008, all July 1961 | [scholars.fhsu.edu/mercury](https://scholars.fhsu.edu/mercury/) (items 302, 304, 306, 407) | The only views found of the ground round pad 5 in 1961: the blockhouse and ready rooms from pad 6's gantry (roof equipment, windows, shutters, breezeway, trench and walk, parked cars, banded posts, huts, the road behind); pad 5 down the track (the turnout, the concrete road with NO SMOKING, cable racks, the shed, huts and trailers on the pad, timber floodlight poles); pad 5 from the blockhouse roof (the telemetry helix); pad 6's square-topped gantry, the racks and the scaffold stair from the ground | Eleven weeks after MR-3, during MR-4's preparation: movable things may differ. Nothing in them is dimensioned |
| Cape Canaveral Space Force Museum, *Launch Complex 26 Blockhouse* | [ccspacemuseum.org](https://ccspacemuseum.org/artifacts/launch-complex-26-blockhouse/) | The twin blockhouse's construction: walls two feet thick; the domed roof "approximately five-feet thick along the edges to nearly eight-feet directly overhead"; windows of "three segments of glass, each comprised of 15 layers of one-quarter inch glass", about a foot thick; a double set of armour-plate blast doors; "400 feet from the launch pads" | Secondary, and Complex 26's, not 5/6's: the NHL nomination calls the two identical. Its 400 ft is not 5/6's distance (305 ft, from OpenStreetMap) |
| Cape Canaveral Space Force Museum, dated photographs on its *Launch Complex 5/6* page: "Redstone in square top gantry, 8 March 1961, Pad 6"; "Launch Complex 5/6, April 1965" | [LC-5/6](https://ccspacemuseum.org/facilities/launch-complex-5-6/) | That pad 6 had its own portal gantry in 1961 and was launching Redstones (18 from pad 6, the last 27 June 1961) | Small images |

Whole metres a second and tens of degrees |

### Mercury-Redstone 4, *Liberty Bell 7*

What capsule No. 11 and its flight rest on (`missions/usa/mercury/flights/mr-4-liberty-bell-7/`), beside the
sources above that the two Redstone flights share.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Results of the Second U.S. Manned Suborbital Space Flight, July 21, 1961*, NASA Manned Spacecraft Center, 1961 | [NTRS 19640056774](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19640056774/downloads/19640056774.pdf) | **The authority for what differed in No. 11**: Hammack's paper (pp. 3 to 7) on the window (field of view, panes), the explosive hatch (70 bolts, fuse, 23 lb against 69, plunger forces), the clamp-ring covers (Figure 2-2), the panel (Figure 2-3) and the rate command system; the count, the event times and Table 2-I; Grissom's own report (pp. 47 to 56): the window, Venus, the tower leaving, the knife on the door, the cover and pin off the hatch's detonator | **Pages are cited as printed** (the scan's page n + 5). Figure 2-1 is a perspective sketch, not dimensioned. Figure 2-3 is too coarse here to read: use Hammack and Heberlig's copy |
| Hammack and Heberlig, *The Mercury-Redstone Program* (above), Figure 6 and pp. 6, 24 and 27 | as above | **Capsule 11's panel**: "Main instrument panel and consoles for MR-4 spacecraft", legible at 400 px/in: every switch and light's legend on the main panel and the left console; the heat shield for both ballistic flights; the hatch again; "no duplication of the premature actuation can be made" | A flattened layout, not dimensioned. The legends of three round instruments and of one with two needles cannot be read. No fuse panel is drawn |
| *Mercury Project Summary*, NASA SP-45 (above), Figure 19-1 (p. 328) | as above | **The window, dimensioned**: outer pane 21.00 by 11.254 and 7.511 in; inner panes 12.950 by 9.490 and 7.300 in with 1 3/4 in corners; panes 0.350, 0.170, 0.340 and 0.340 in; the skin at 20° and the inner panes at 26° to the axis; the field of view | The orbital spacecraft's window (the transmission curves are MA-9's). Its panes' thicknesses are the ones the MR-4 report gives, which is the ground for using its sizes for No. 11. The section is not dimensioned for depth |
| SEDR 104 (above), 2-4, 2-5, Figures 2-2 and 2-3, and Figure 13-11 | as above | The explosive hatch: two hatch shingles fastened only to the hatch, the initiator "in the upper aft corner", its plunger, cap and safety pin, the outside release, the knife and waste container on the hatch, retention springs; the window's two assemblies, the outer one curved to the cone; thermocouple stations on the hatch (Z123.0 to Z147.25) | The orbital capsule (and Figure 13-11 is capsule 9's). Which corner "upper aft" is has to be read off the figures |
| *This New Ocean* (above), pp. 365 to 377 | as above | The hatch's history and the lanyard behind "a small panel"; the window's maker and panes; the panel rearranged "to improve the eye-scan pattern"; the couch's padding; Grissom's suit (wrist bearings, the chest mirror, the new harness and microphones); the survival pack "near the astronaut's left arm"; the 69 bolts; the recovery, the open suit inlet, "about 2800 fathoms" | A history. Pages are as printed (the scan's page n + 14) |
| TM X-53107 (above), 4.2.1, 7.4.3 and 8.3.2 | as above | Booster MR-8: 102 lb more damping compound; the azimuth of 100°; oxygen loaded from T-180; the three holds | Its two accounts of MR-3's azimuth (102° and 105°) do not agree |
| NASA photographs of capsule 11 on the pad, July 1961: 61-MR4-53, 61-MR4-76, 61-MR4-79, S61-02881, S61-02882, S61-02888, S61-03698, S61-03705, S61-03740, S61-03744, MSFC-6116423, MSFC-6116427; and at sea, 21 July: S61-02817, S61-02820, S61-02824, S61-02826, 61-MR4-82 | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/); Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Liberty Bell 7* | **Where everything on the outside is**: the window in its plain plate with its rim and the foil behind; the hatch's sill beside the window; LIBERTY BELL 7 in three lines under the window and the crack beside it (61-MR4-53, 6116423, S61-03740); the periscope's door open with the lens and the umbilical under it, and UNITED STATES beside it (S61-03705); the flag on the door's other side (S61-02824); the shingles' beads running round the cone; Grissom's chest mirror and name tag (S61-03698) | Close, oblique views with no scale but the window's own panes: every place read off them is an estimate. No view found of the side between 165° and 225° |
| SEDR 104 (above), Figure 1-3, in the editions of December 1959 (p. 1-5 there, Z161.82 for the cone's joint with the cylinder) and November 1961 (Z161.71), and Figure 13-11 | as above | **Where the stations begin, and what the trapezoid on the cone is.** Z104.50 is the plane of the greatest diameter in both editions (and Figure 13-11's "heat shield attach point" and "adapter retaining ring"), so a station here is McDonnell's Z less 104.50, with no change of sign. The trapezoid Figure 1-3 draws on the cone, with leaders to Z123.09 and Z146.126 (stations 18.59 and 41.63), is **the hatch**: the same drawing is in the 1959 edition, before any capsule had the window. Figure 13-11 (capsule 9) lists the window's thermocouples, items 20 to 22, with no station | **No station for the window in either, nor in SP-45, the MR-4 Results, TN D-3814 or TN D-4807.** The hatch's stations here do not agree with Figure 13-11's thermocouple on the hatch at Z147.25, nor with the photographs (Liberty Bell 7's spacecraft.md, "The hatch"); and the cone's joint at Z161.71 is 57.21, where the model derives 58.38 (mercury-capsule.md): both left open |

| Photographs the shingle under the window and the paint were read again on, November 2026. No. 13: GPN-2006-000027 and its colour twin, S62-00380, the capsule as kept (Commons: *Mercury, MA-6, Friendship 7 - Seitenansicht*, *Friendship 7 National Air and Space Museum*), and President Kennedy at the capsule (Commons: *JFK inspects Mercury capsule, 23 February 1962*). No. 18: the emblem square on (Commons: *Aurora 7 insignia proper*), S62-03907, 62-MA7-73, TN D-3814 Figure 25, 62PC-52. No. 20: S63-07701, S63-07521, S63-06259, S63-09676-E, KSC-63-MA9-174, GPN-2000-001335 | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/); Wikimedia Commons | **Where each capsule's name or emblem, its UNITED STATES, flags and patches stand against the window**, each photograph's camera fitted to the cone (its joint with the cylinder, its foot, its limbs) or to hardware so placed, or the beads used as a ruler. What came of it: on every capsule the lettering is level with the window and the emblem hangs low on its shingle; No. 11's crack runs to station 54.5; No. 11 carried a second UNITED STATES across its hatch (not modelled); the shingle under the window has ten beads to a stack on Nos. 11, 13, 16 and 18 | Estimated, each to between 0.3 and 1.2 in (each flight's spacecraft.md gives its own). The far side of every capsule was not read. No time of day for the structure's rollback |
| TM X-53107 (above), 7.2.3.2, pp. 7-11 to 7-33 | as above | **MR-4's own countdown**, item by item: the structure to the edge of the pad at T-316 and back at T-265, platform 1 at T-200, LOX from T-180 to T-145, the LOX trailers removed at T-135, insertion at T-123, the hatch at T-90, the safety shower reconnected "on west side" at T-265 and disconnected and stowed at T-85, "Move spare parts trailer" and "Move transfer van to launch position" at T-80, platforms 4 and 3 at T-65 and T-60, the structure at T-55, the cherry picker positioned after it, the M-113 on station at T-50 | A plan, not a log: what was done when on the day is this plus the holds |

Black and white; no dimensions. Earlier notes here cite some of the same prints by their file names (F16_015 to F19_008) |

NASA S61-02407 (MR-3 as the engine lights: the capsule's mast down, the booster's falling) and S63-02651. TM X-53107 4.2.3 (the cooling package "mounted on a cable mast"), L-25 ("Cable masts erected"), T-540, T-530, T-390, T-380, T-325, T-45, T-35 s | [scholars.fhsu.edu/mercury](https://scholars.fhsu.edu/mercury/); [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/S61-02407) | **The masts' places round the vehicle** (triangulated from 466, 468 and 333: `places/cape-canaveral/lc5/site.py` `MASTS`, lc-5.md "The two cable masts"); that they were hinged on the launcher's table and fell straight out from the vehicle; the booster's cooling package, its hoses and its steady; the capsule's release head and the umbilical's arm to the periscope's door. Also what is in lc-5.md, "Which way round the vehicle stood": the same prints put the truck and the structure on the other side of the vehicle from the model's, by about 60 degrees | No drawing of either mast was found: every size is scaled against the booster's 70 in body. The print backs of 241, 118, 126 and 134 (their dates and captions) were not read. **S63-02651 is not MR-4.** NASA's archive (the Johnson Space Center collection, photo ID S63-02651) has it as the launch of Mercury-Redstone 4, and this repository cited it so until November 2026. Its booster has UNITED STATES in full down a white body and one row of chequers, where MR-3 and MR-4 had the long chequered section and UNITED over STATES. Wikimedia Commons holds it as *Mercury-Redstone 1 launch attempt S63-02651.jpg*, 21 November 1960, and says why: the booster's markings are MR-1's and the cloud is the one in the photograph of MR-1's escape rocket going (Commons 10073418). That identification is Commons' (secondary); that it is not MR-3 or MR-4 is plain in the picture. NASA's own record was not opened again. It shows the launcher, the two masts (the booster's with a yellow package at its head, the capsule's lying on the pad), the blast screens with an orange tank and the chevron drum, from the same place as S61-02407: what was read off it (the launcher's height and width, the drum's roundness, that the screens stood) holds for November 1960, on the same launcher, and the record now says so where it had "July" |
| NOAA NCEI Integrated Surface Database (above), stations 74794012868 and 74795012867, 21 July 1961 | [noaa-global-hourly-pds](https://noaa-global-hourly-pds.s3.amazonaws.com/1961/74794012868.csv) | **The sky MR-4 left under**: at 12:00 UTC the Skid Strip had 8/8 total cover with no ceiling: 1/8 at 510 m (type 09), 2/8 altocumulus at 5,400 m, 8/8 cirrostratus (GA1 `01,1,+00510,9,09`, GA2 `02,1,+05400,9,03`, GA3 `08,1,+22000,9,02`), wind 200° at 2.1 m/s, 25.5 °C; at 11:00 a ceiling at 3,000 m; Patrick at 12:00 5/8 altocumulus at 4,500 m | Decoded by us. The high layer's 22,000 m is the record's limit, not a measured base. Eight oktas with no ceiling is a thin layer in the airways code of the day. **Drawn**: the flight's `weather.ts` |
| *Results of the Second U.S. Manned Suborbital Space Flight* (above), pp. 37 and 52 | as above | **The cloud as the pilot saw it from 190 km**: "High cirrus blotted out everything except an area from about Daytona Beach back inland to Orlando and Lakeland to Lake Okeechobee and down to the tip of Florida. Beyond this the Gulf of Mexico was visible" (p. 37, the debriefing); the Cape and the Banana River picked out; "I never did see Cuba"; the Gulf coast between Apalachicola and Mobile, "the cloud coverage was quite extensive", land and water "both a hazy blue" (p. 52) | Places named, not bounds or covers: the bounds of each stretch and the cover each word means are ours (the flight's `weather.ts`: `beyond`) |
| *Results of the Second U.S. Manned Suborbital Space Flight* (above), pp. 6 and 7 (the sequence of events, Table 2-I), 35 and 36 (attitude control, Figure 6-3), 40 to 46 (the air-ground transcript), 47 to 56 (the pilot's report) | as above | **MR-4 flown** (flight.ts `trajectory`, `checks`): cutoff at 02:23 with the tower, separation ten seconds later, manual control from 03:05, the retro sequence by the timer at 04:46 "30 seconds prior to … apogee", retrofire by the pilot at 05:10 ("there's 1 firing" at 05:11.5, "There went 3" at 05:19), the retro attitude held "very close to … −34° in pitch", rate command from 05:43, retropack at 06:07, the drogue at 09:41, the main at 10:14, landing at 15:37; range 262.5 and height 102.8 nmi, 605.5 lb/sq ft, 6.3 and 11.1 g, 6,618 and 7,580 ft/s, weightless 5:00. After landing: "the capsule is floating, slowly coming vertical" at 16:35, the last words at 26:09 ("then I'll power down and blow the hatch"), and nothing after "as a result of the emergency egress"; the pilot "in the water for about 3 to 4 minutes" (p. 7) | The rate command system's ±3 deg/s (pp. 3 and 36; the pilot, p. 52) is its deadband. | *This New Ocean* (above), pp. 370 to 375 | as above | The flight and its end as the history tells it: the main at 12,300 ft, "about 1000 feet higher than the design nominal altitude"; the pin out of the detonator and "a dull thud"; the hatch skipping over the waves; the flooded spacecraft "weighed over 5000 pounds, a thousand pounds beyond the helicopter's lifting capacity"; 2,800 fathoms; Grissom in the water "four or five minutes" | A history; *Results* says three or four |

| *This New Ocean* (above), pp. 311, 356 and 372 to 375 | as above | The landing bag: "the heatshield and porous bag were to drop down about four feet", "a sort of sea anchor" (p. 311), "its four-foot impact skirt" (p. 356); the capsule "rolling badly with the swells" (p. 372); the copilot "preparing to cut the capsule's antenna whip", the hatch "strike the water at a distance of about five feet from the hatch, and then go skipping over the waves", the antenna clipped, the hook threaded, "sank out of sight, but the pickup pole twanged as the attached cable went taut" (p. 373); the chip light; "struggled valiantly to raise the spacecraft high enough to drain the water from the impact bag", "almost clear of the water", "over 5000 pounds, a thousand pounds beyond the helicopter's lifting capacity", "allowing the spacecraft to sink swiftly" (p. 375) | A history: the 4,000 lbf capacity is derived from its two figures, and "over 5000" is a bound. *Results of the First United States Manned Orbital Space Flight* (below), pp. 28 and 29; *Results of the Second U.S. Manned Suborbital Space Flight* (above), pp. 6, 39 and 44; *Postlaunch Report for Mercury-Redstone No. 3* (above), 5.6.2, 9.3 and 10.1.1; "Twelve seconds after main chute deployment the landing impact bag starts to inflate" (1959, 9-2; the 1962 printing's 9-2 has the same twelve-second relays "releasing the heat shield"); "the torus bag at base of impact bag will be inflated to 10 psi" (1959, 9-3); Known and not read: the *Postlaunch Memorandum Report for Mercury-Redstone No. 4* (NASA STG, 1961: capsule
weights, centre of gravity, event times to the tenth, the hatch investigation); the MR-4 press kit; McDonnell's
flight operations manual for capsule 11 (SEDR 109-11), which would give the panel dimensioned and the fuse panel;
the Cosmosphere's restoration record and photographs of the cabin as found.

### The recovery helicopter: the Marines' HUS-1

What the Sikorsky HUS-1 (S-58) that recovered the Redstone flights rests on (`missions/usa/mercury/vehicles/hus-1.md`,
`hus_1.py`). Read November 2026.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |

Read as OCR text. The 1960-61 edition is on archive.org for lending only and was not read |
| Sikorsky Aircraft, *Descriptive Arrangement* of the S-58: a three-view with its dimensions | reproduced in Bryan Swopes, *This Day in Aviation*, "8 March 1954" ([thisdayinaviation.com](https://www.thisdayinaviation.com/8-march-1954/)), credited "(Sikorsky)" | **The model's shape**: rotor 56.0 ft, tail rotor 9.5 ft and its lowest tip 77 in up, track 12.0, fuselage 46.6, height 14.3, 65.7 ft with the rotors turning, 56.3 and 37.0 stowed, blade area 35 sq ft, NACA 0012, 11.3 : 1; and, read off the views, the body's outline, the gear, the pylon, the stabiliser, the door (dashed) and the windows | A 530-pixel reproduction of a drawing whose date and edition are not given: positions read off it are good to about 2 in. Known and not read: the Navy's *Standard Aircraft Characteristics* chart for the HUS-1 (the HyperWar depository lists
it "not yet scanned"); the HSS-1 and HUS-1 flight manual and the *NATOPS Flight Manual, Navy Models UH-34D, G and J*
(1966), which would give the rescue hoist's and the cargo hook's rated loads, the hovering ceilings at the HUS-1's
own weights, and the door and hoist dimensioned; HMR(L)-262's command chronology for July 1961.

What spacecraft No. 13 and Atlas 109-D rest on (`flights/ma-6-friendship-7/`, `vehicles/atlas-lv3b.md`,
`places/cape-canaveral/lc-14.ts`), beyond the sources above that the Mercury flights share.

Known and not read: the *Postlaunch Memorandum Report for Mercury-Atlas No. 6* (NASA MSC, March 1962: weights and
centres of gravity, event times to the tenth, the launch vehicle's own performance; a copy is in the National
Archives' catalogue; not on NTRS, searched November 2026); a National Archives (Fort Worth) memorandum on why the capsule
landed "39 nautical miles from the planned impact point" (SNAC); Convair's flight evaluation report for Atlas 109-D and any Convair drawing or station list of
the Atlas D (the booster's stations, weights, thrust as flown, the pitch programme); McDonnell's flight operations
manual for capsule 13; the hourly weather observations at the Cape for 20 February 1962 (NOAA's Integrated Surface
Database, as read for MR-4); photographs of No. 13's panel as it is kept, read against the model; Glenn's height
from a primary source.

What the complex's model rests on (`places/cape-canaveral/lc-14.md`, `lc14/`), beyond HAER No. FL-8-7, the NHL
nomination and OpenStreetMap's stand, ramp and tracks listed under Mercury-Atlas 6 above.

### Mercury-Atlas 7, *Aurora 7*

What spacecraft No. 18 and Atlas 107-D rest on (`flights/ma-7-aurora-7/`, `vehicles/mercury_cabin_18.py`,
`vehicles/atlas-lv3b.md`), beyond the sources above that the Mercury flights share.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Second United States Manned Three-Pass Orbital Mission (Mercury-Atlas 7, Spacecraft 18): Description and Performance Analysis*, ed. John H. Boynton, NASA TN D-3814, February 1967 | [NTRS 19670008816](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19670008816) | **The authority for the flight and for what No. 18 was**: the postlaunch report as later published. Liftoff 07:45:16.57 EST (p. 3); **the twenty-one differences from spacecraft 13 (pp. 5 and 6) and Table IV, each change with its date (pp. 31 and 32)**; weights, centres of gravity and moments of inertia for five phases (p. 7); **Atlas 107-D against 109-D (pp. 7 and 8)**; the count, the holds and the weather (pp. 16 to 18); what the pad's cameras saw, the periscope drawing in among it (p. 18); the electrical and sequential changes, the emergency drogue switch wired to the periscope (pp. 59 to 61); the heat shield (p. 65); **the white paint patch (p. 66) and Figure 25**; the parachutes and barostat (pp. 66 and 67); **the balloon (pp. 70 to 72, Figure 29) and the zero-gravity experiment (p. 73, Figure 30)**; the pilot's report (pp. 89 to 97); the launch vehicle's performance (pp. 98 and 99); **Table XVI, every event planned and as flown (p. 122); Table XVII, the cutoff, the orbit, the loads, the landing (pp. 123 and 124)**; Table XX, the landing point (p. 128) | A printed page is the PDF's page less 14. Its figures are one-bit scans: Figure 25 gives places, not tones. It says the balloon was in the recovery compartment (pp. 5 and 32) and in the antenna canister (p. 71). Its table and its text differ on when the retrorockets lit (pp. 61 and 122) |
| *Results of the Second United States Manned Orbital Space Flight, May 24, 1962*, NASA SP-6, Manned Spacecraft Center, 1962 | [NTRS 19620004691](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19620004691) | The same flight as first published: the spacecraft's systems and what was changed since MA-6 (pp. 1 to 14: the barostat, the "maneuver" switch, the thrusters and Figure 1-4, the Earth-path indicator and panel camera deleted, the experiments, Atlas 107-D); the solid motors' ratings and the retrorockets' 3 per cent (p. 3); **the air-ground transcript (pp. 77 to 107)**: "Periscope is out" at 00:05:27 (p. 78), "The scope did come in" at 04:36:14.5 (p. 106), the drogue by hand at 04:50:51 and the main at "Mark 10" (p. 107) | A printed page is the PDF's page less 6. No figure of the panel. Prints 950 miles for the Super SARAH beacon's range where TN D-3814 has 250 |
| *This New Ocean* (above), chapter 13, pp. 443 to 458 | as above | The experiments and who asked for them (pp. 443 to 445); what was taken out of No. 18, the couch's new liner, the life vest "beneath the parabolic mirror" (p. 445); the name and why (p. 446); the staging time (p. 446); the morning to the minute, "all 70 bolts", the mirror on his chest (pp. 446 and 447); the flight told in order; the landing, the raft and the recovery (pp. 455 to 458) | Secondary. Gives the balloon package as two pounds and three 15-minute holds, where the report has about 5 lb and holds of 15, 15, 10 and 5 minutes |
| *Mercury Project Summary*, NASA SP-45 (above), pp. 12 and 13 | as above | Spacecraft 18 against 13 in a paragraph; **the insulation bulkhead: cut out of Atlas 109-D on the pad, retained in 107-D, removed at the factory from 113-D** | |
| NASA photographs of spacecraft No. 18 before the flight: S62-03907 (Carpenter looking in at the hatch: the window, the emblem, the last letters of UNITED STATES); 62-MA7-73 (Cecelia Bibby painting the emblem, Carpenter beside her: the flag masked for painting, the seam, "CAUTION THERMOCOUPLE", J37); S62-04018 and S62-02846 (the insertion) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/S62-03907); Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Aurora 7* and *Category:Mercury-Atlas 7* | **The emblem's place** on the shingle under the window and against the hatch; **two stacks of beads on that shingle**; UNITED STATES to the left of the window with its flag below the seam; the window and hatch where No. 11's are | All taken close and at an angle: each scales differently across the frame |
| The Aurora 7 emblem photographed square on, in colour (Commons: *Aurora 7 insignia proper.jpg*, credited to NASA; 442 px) | Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Aurora 7* | **The emblem's layout and colours**: a blue 7 with a pale edge, cream, orange and pale blue rings of rays, white AURORA across its foot and onto the hatch's border | Small, undated and without a NASA number. How many rings there are and their order is not certain in it. Colours are a print's |
| NASA photographs of the vehicle: S62-05531 (on the stand as the engines start, in colour), 62PC-52, S62-02849, S62-05529 and GPN-2000-001400 (leaving it) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/s62-05531); Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Mercury-Atlas 7* | **"107 D" on the fuel tank** either side of the fairing above a vernier; UNITED STATES down the oxygen tank before the frost; the second UNITED STATES and its flag on the capsule, round to the right of the hatch (62PC-52); the red clamp ring; the tower's red | From hundreds of feet: the capsule is some 150 pixels across at best |
| Convair/General Dynamics Astronautics Atlas negative collection, 14_006114, "Atlas 107D Being Transported to Hangar J", 8 March 1962 (San Diego Air and Space Museum; on Commons) | Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Mercury-Atlas 7* | The date the vehicle reached the Cape, which *This New Ocean* also gives | 600 px |
| Museum of Science and Industry, Chicago: the capsule as kept (Commons: *Aurora 7 the Museum of Science and Industry in Chicago.JPG*) | Wikimedia Commons | Where it is | Not read against the model |
| NASA TN D-3814 (above), pp. 16 to 18, 77, 96 and 111; NASA SP-6 (above), Tables 5-II and 5-III (pp. 44 and 46) and the transcript, p. 77 | as above | **The morning at the pad** (`pad.ts`): the count's points (insertion T-135, the hatch from T-94 to T-74, the shingles by T-64, the service tower's first motion at T-47 and stowed at T-33, liquid oxygen from T-32, 45 minutes held at T-11), up the gantry at 4:36 and aboard at 4:43 (Table XI); **the weather at launch** (p. 17); the neck dam stowed (pp. 77 and 96); the pilot's weight that morning, 154 lb (SP-6, Table 5-III); "Clear blue sky" at 32 seconds (SP-6, p. 77) | No time by the clock is given for any point of the count: they are put by the clock from the liftoff and the holds, all at T-11 (derived). Table XI has 4:36 for the gantry and the history 4:35; SP-6's table has 3:40 into the van and the history 3:45 |
| NOAA NCEI Integrated Surface Database, 1962: stations 74794012868 (Cape Canaveral, the Skid Strip) and 74795012867 (Patrick AFB), 24 May | [noaa-global-hourly-pds](https://noaa-global-hourly-pds.s3.amazonaws.com/1962/74794012868.csv) (fetched 16 November 2026) | **The weather Aurora 7 left under.** The Skid Strip at 13:00 UTC, fifteen minutes after liftoff: wind 250° at 4.1 m/s, ceiling 210 m (CIG `00210`), visibility 1,600 m, 25.0 °C, dew point 22.8 °C, `GF1 07` with no layer coded, present weather 11 (patches of shallow fog). At 12:00: 230° at 2.6 m/s, no ceiling, 1,200 m, GA1 `01,1,+99999,9,99`, GA2 `06,1,+22000,9,00` (six oktas of cirrus), fog. At 14:00: 4,800 m, no ceiling; at 15:00 8,000 m, GA1 `05,1,+22000,9,00` (five oktas of cirrus). Patrick: 2,000 m in fog (present weather 45) at 11:00, 8,000 m and `GF1 02` at 13:00, no ceiling all morning | Decoded by us (codes per the NCEI format document). The report's figures at launch (TN D-3814, p. 17) are the station's of 13:00 to the figure but for the wind's bearing (240° there). `GF1` takes the airways classes here, not a count of oktas. The cirrus's 22,000 m is the record's limit, not a base. **Drawn**: the flight's `weather.ts` |
| NASA photograph 62PC-52 (the liftoff, from the ramp, in the fog) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/62PC-52) | **Complex 14 that morning**: the tapered tower with the bascule upright and its cage at the top, the ramp's rail, two floodlight poles of three lamps, the signal; the sky grey with fog | A single view along the ramp |
| NASA TN D-3814 (above), pp. 7, 67, 87, 95, 99 and 101, Tables XVI, XVII and XX; NASA SP-6 (above), Tables 3-I and 3-II and p. 21; NASA SP-45 (above), Table 8-III; *This New Ocean* (NASA SP-4201), pp. 453 and 454 | as above | **The flight as flown** (`orbital.ts`, mission.md): every event planned and flown, the cutoff and the orbit, the loads (Tables XVI and XVII); the five weights (p. 7); 107-D's staging at 130.1 s (p. 7); **the retrofire as the tracking gave it: 36.5° in pitch, 27° in yaw, the impulse 3 per cent under the group's 38,943 lb s, and the landing 250 nautical miles long and 15 north of the track** (p. 101); "approximately 3 seconds late" by hand (p. 87); the drogue by hand at 25,450 ft and the main at 8,950 ft (p. 67); "right on 30 feet per second" (p. 95); the guidance 2 ft/s high (p. 99); the pick-up point (Table 8-III); "canted at retrofire about 25 degrees to the right", and what each error was worth: 175, 15 and 60 miles (*This New Ocean*, p. 454) | The capsule's weight at retrofire is not printed ("a calculated spacecraft pre-retrograde weight"). SP-6 has retrorocket No. 3 at 04:33:20.5 where Table XVI has 20.3, and the retrofire "at approximately 04:33:09" (p. 21). Which way the yaw lay is said only by *This New Ocean*; the landing north of the track agrees with it |

Known and not read: the *Postlaunch Memorandum Report for Mercury-Atlas No. 7* as first issued (NASA MSC, June 1962:
TN D-3814 is its edited publication, without the full time histories it mentions); McDonnell's flight operations manual
for capsule 18 (SEDR 109-18), which would give the panel as built, with the "maneuver" switch and the steam-vent
indicator placed; Convair's flight evaluation report for Atlas 107-D; any drawing of the balloon experiment's
installation (Langley Research Center) or of the insulation bulkhead; colour photographs of the emblem with a NASA
number and date, and of the capsule between 60° and 180°; the report's plot of the launch area's winds to 60,000 ft
(TN D-3814, Figure 8), not read off; the Cape's rawinsonde of that morning; Carpenter's height from a primary source; Carpenter and Stoever, *For Spacious Skies* (2002), for the emblem's
making.

### Mercury-Atlas 8, *Sigma 7*

What spacecraft No. 16 and Atlas 113-D rest on (`flights/ma-8-sigma-7/`, `vehicles/mercury_cabin_16.py`,
`vehicles/mercury_orbital.py`, `vehicles/atlas-lv3b.md`), beyond the sources above that the Mercury flights share.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *First U.S. Manned Six-Pass Orbital Mission (Mercury-Atlas 8, Spacecraft 16): Description and Performance Analysis*, ed. John H. Boynton, NASA TN D-4807, September 1968 | [NTRS 19680025550](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19680025550) | **The authority for the flight and for what No. 16 was**: the postlaunch report as later published. **The eighty-one changes from spacecraft 18, by system** (pp. 7 to 12), dated in Table IV (pp. 38 to 40); **what Atlas 113-D had that 107-D had not** (pp. 12 and 13: no insulation bulkhead, baffled injectors and Figure 7, hypergolic igniters and Figure 8, no hold-down, the staging backup, the range safety receivers); weights and inertias for five phases (Table I, p. 14); the count (pp. 25 and 26); **the weather at launch and at recovery** (pp. 26 and 27, Figure 9: the winds aloft); the pad's cameras: umbilical, periscope, door (p. 28); recovery aids, the SOFAR bomb's fix (p. 35); the control system and its new switch (pp. 53 to 56); **the dipole antenna** (p. 61, Figures 21 and 22); the heat shield, the afterbody's temperatures and **the white paint patch** (pp. 67 and 68); parachutes, hatch, landing bag (pp. 69 and 70); **the hand-held camera** (p. 73, Figures 28 and 29) and **the ablation panels** (pp. 75 to 77, Figure 33); where the equipment was stowed (pp. 88 and 89); the yaw exercises with window and periscope (pp. 91 and 92); drifting flight (p. 92); the pilot's report (pp. 96 to 103); the launch vehicle, the roll at liftoff, the fuel taken off (pp. 104 to 106); **Tables XXII and XXIII (pp. 134 and 135): every event planned and as flown, the cutoff, the orbit, the loads, the landing point** | A printed page is the PDF's page less 16. Its figures are scanned in two tones: the photographs are nearly unreadable. Page 3 gives the range zero as 07:45:16.00, which is MA-7's. The table of work (p. 38) calls the high-thruster cutout a "low-thrust-only select switch". Its two accounts of which shingles were instrumented disagree (pp. 75 and 76). The staging backup is said to have been changed "from T + 136 to T + 132.3 seconds" from MA-7's vehicle (p. 12), for which TN D-3814 (p. 7) gives 132.2 |
| *Results of the Third United States Manned Orbital Space Flight, October 3, 1962*, NASA SP-12, Manned Spacecraft Center, December 1962 | [NTRS 19630002114](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19630002114) | The same flight as first published, with photographs that can be read: the helmet's new microphone (Figure 1-4), **the dipole antenna on the striped retropack (Figure 1-5)**, the filter mosaic, **the ten cylinder shingles laid out, nine with their panels (Figure 1-9)**; the launch vehicle's changes (pp. 10 and 11); the suit temperature and the thought of ending the flight after one orbit (p. 15); the fuel after retrofire (p. 18); the leg restraints (p. 6); **the air-ground transcript (pp. 57 to 119)**: the periscope by day and night, "that blasted periscope" (p. 68), the dipole (pp. 64 and 79), "The scope is coming in" and its confirmation at 08:53:40 (p. 116), the descent, "I'm going to retract the scope manually" (p. 119) | A printed page is the PDF's page less 6. Two columns: the text layer runs them together |
| *This New Ocean* (above), chapter 14, pp. 461 to 490 | as above | The choice of No. 16 over No. 19 and its preparation (p. 465); the name (pp. 470 and 471); the morning to the minute, the ignition key, the steak sandwich, "every bolt sank neatly in its threads" (pp. 471 and 472); the roll at liftoff "only 20 percent short of an abort condition" (p. 473); the flight told in order; the periscope "excess baggage" (p. 475); the landing 4.5 miles from the carrier (p. 483); the heat shield afterwards (p. 484); the periscope and the Hasselblad among what was weighed for MA-9 (p. 490) | A printed page is the scan's page less 5 in this chapter. Its speeds and heights are in statute miles |
| *Mercury Project Summary*, NASA SP-45 (above), p. 223 | as above | **The hand-held cameras flight by flight**: a 35 mm Ansco Autoset on MA-6, a 35 mm Robot Recorder on MA-7, a 70 mm Hasselblad on MA-8 and MA-9, with the film and exposure of each | |
| NASA photographs of spacecraft No. 16 before the flight: GPN-2000-001441 (in Hangar S, hung from a sling over the retropack: **the window and hatch under their covers, the second UNITED STATES and its flag, the fittings marked J37 and J20 at the foot of the shingle under the window, the retropack's stripes square on**); S62-06002 (Schirra going aboard: **the emblem under the window**, the suit without a chest mirror); S62-08895 (suiting up) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/s62-06002); Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Sigma 7* | Where the paint is; what the pilot wore | GPN-2000-001441 is before the emblem was painted. S62-06002 is close, oblique and soft: the emblem's upper half only |
| The shingle that carried the emblem, as it is kept (Commons: *Flown Superalloy Shingle with Sigma 7 logo, from the MA-8 Mercury Spacecraft piloted by Wally Shirra (50504120447).jpg*; 1000 px) | Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Mercury-Atlas 8* | **The emblem's layout, square on, and its colours as they are now**: a red sigma edged in cream, a yellow 7 edged in white in its mouth; two stacks of eleven beads; the plain foot with two round fittings, J37 and J20 | Scorched by the entry and flaked: the sigma's lower half is mostly gone. No scale. Whose photograph it is and where the shingle is kept are not stated beyond its caption |
| NASA photographs of the flight: KSC-62PC-0078 and S62-06006 (the liftoff, in colour: **"11 3D" on the fuel tank either side of the fairing above a vernier**, the second UNITED STATES and its flag, a pale panel on the cylinder above it, a white panel and a round badge on the adapter); S62-06175 and S62-06144 (the capsule lowered to the carrier's deck: the window, the seams, **the first UNITED STATES to the left of the window, a pale rectangle above it**); S62-06108 and S62-06148 (Schirra leaving by the hatch); S66-53205 (the capsule shown at Houston in 1966) and KSC-20161107-PH_GEB02_0276 (as it is shown now) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/KSC-62PC-0078) | The vehicle's number; where the lettering stood; that the periscope was aboard | After the entry the paint is dark and the panels charred: the recovery photographs show tones, not colours |
| NASA TN D-4807 (above), pp. 12, 25 to 28 and Table X; NASA SP-12 (above), Table 3-IV (p. 25) and the transcript | as above | **The morning at the pad** (`pad.ts`): to the pad at 4:07, up the gantry at 4:37 and aboard at 4:41, T-140 (Table X); the hatch from T-108 to T-96, the shingles by T-88, the service tower's "removal started" at T-64, one hold of 15 minutes at T-45, liquid oxygen from T-38 (pp. 25 and 26); **the weather at launch, observed at 7:17** (pp. 26 and 27); two fixed cameras saw the umbilical go, the periscope draw in and its door shut (p. 28); no hold-down delay on 113-D (p. 12); the pilot's weight that morning, 176¾ lb (SP-12, Table 3-IV); his fingertip lights in the transcript | No time by the clock is given for any point of the count, nor for the structure being stowed: they are put by the clock from the liftoff and the one hold (derived), the stowing at Aurora 7's fourteen minutes (assumed). SP-12's table is a poor scan ("176%"); TN D-4807's has 176.75 |
| NOAA NCEI Integrated Surface Database, 1962: stations 74794012868 and 74795012867, 3 October | [noaa-global-hourly-pds](https://noaa-global-hourly-pds.s3.amazonaws.com/1962/74794012868.csv) (fetched 16 November 2026) | **The weather Sigma 7 left under**, beside the report's: the Skid Strip at 12:00 UTC, fifteen minutes before liftoff: wind 140° at 2.1 m/s, no ceiling, visibility 16 km (its limit), 23.9 °C, dew point 22.8 °C, GA1 `01,1,+00660,9,08` (one okta of cumulus at 660 m), GA2 `04,1,+22000,9,02` (four of cirrostratus), `GF1 02`. At 15:00: two oktas of cumulus at 780 m and two of cirrus. Patrick at 12:00: one okta of cirrostratus and a layer at 1,800 m of no coded cover | Decoded by us. The cirrus's 22,000 m is the record's limit, not a base. **Drawn**: the flight's `weather.ts`, which takes the report's figures first |
| NASA photographs of the morning: S62-06002 (Schirra at the hatch, Cooper behind him); S62-07872 (the liftoff, from the ramp, in colour) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/s62-06002) | A webbing strap with a buckle across the suit's chest; **Complex 14 that morning**: the tower, its bascule upright, the ramp's rail, floodlights, the signal; and by the ramp's foot two street lamps on curved arms, a crane's orange boom and trailers; a blue sky streaked with cirrus, cumulus on the horizon | The wrists are not clear in S62-06002 |
| NASA TN D-4807 (above), Tables I, XXII and XXIII and pp. 69, 101, 103, 105, 106 and 108; NASA SP-12 (above), Tables 2-I and 2-II; NASA SP-45 (above), Table 8-III | as above | **The flight as flown** (`orbital.ts`, mission.md): every event planned and flown, the cutoff and the orbit, the loads (Tables XXII and XXIII; SP-12's Table 2-I gives the cutoff's path, −0.0062°, and the perigee in nautical miles); the five weights (Table I); **the retrofire as the report's own integration flew it: 38,975 lb s at −34° pitch, no roll or yaw, over 2,994 lb, which came down within a mile of the retrieval point** (p. 108); Schirra's backup button and the half degree (p. 101); the drogue by hand at 39,400 ft and the main by its barostat at 10,600 (p. 69); the guidance's 15 ft/s high and 0.0084° low (p. 105); the lofted climb, BECO 2 s early and SECO 10 s late (p. 106); the pick-up point (Table 8-III) | Table XXIII's scan drops the decimal points and the signs of the path angles; SP-12's table settles them. The Gilmore weighing system's 324,650 lb at the oxygen tank's securing (p. 105) is more than the type's whole stack: what it weighed with is not said, and it is not used |

Known and not read: the *Postlaunch Memorandum Report for Mercury-Atlas No. 8* as first issued (NASA MSC, October
1962: TN D-4807 is its edited publication); McDonnell's flight operations manual for capsule 16 (SEDR 109-16), which
would give the panel as built, with the thrust-select, attitude-select and antenna switches and the new gauges placed;
Convair's flight evaluation report for Atlas 113-D; the ablation experiment's own report, which would name the eight
materials and say which shingle carried which; any drawing of the dipole antenna's installation, or of the shingles;
a photograph of the emblem before the flight in colour and square on (one of Cecelia Bibby painting it with Schirra
is on Commons, *Cece Bibby with Wally Schirra - Sigma 7 logo on Mercury spacecraft.jpg*, and could not be fetched),
of the capsule between 60° and 180°, and of the white patch and the three-colour triangle close to; what the white
panel and round badge on the adapter were; the report's plot of the launch site's winds to 60,000 ft (TN D-4807,
Figure 9), not read off; when the service structure was stowed that morning; Schirra's height from a primary source; Schirra, *Schirra's Space* (1988), for the emblem and the camera.

### Mercury-Atlas 9, *Faith 7*

What spacecraft No. 20 and Atlas 130-D rest on (`flights/ma-9-faith-7/`, `vehicles/mercury_cabin_20.py`,
`vehicles/atlas-lv3b.md`), beyond the sources above that the Mercury flights share.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Mercury Project Summary Including Results of the Fourth Manned Orbital Flight, May 15 and 16, 1963*, NASA SP-45 (above) | as above | **The programme's summary and this flight's report in one.** The flight in a paragraph: 87.2 by 144.2 nautical miles, the 0.05 g light on the 19th pass, the manual retrofire (pp. 13 and 14); Table 3-I, every flight's duration and outcome (p. 51); **Table 3-II, "Summary of Modifications to MA-9 Spacecraft": twenty changes, each with its reason and its weight (p. 52)**; the rate control system's removal, the new thrust chambers, the auxiliary fuel tank and Figure 3-11 (pp. 54 to 56); **the television system and Figure 3-12** (p. 56); the transmitters deleted, the HF dipole tested again (pp. 56 and 57); **Atlas 130-D: the booster engines' alignment offset (pp. 96 and 97), its acceptance, the count, liftoff at 8:04:13 a.m. e.s.t., every powered-flight time (pp. 102 and 103)**; the landing point (pp. 135 and 151); **the flashing beacon, Figures 12-2 and 12-5 (pp. 218 to 220), the Geiger tubes and Figure 12-14 (p. 226), the balloon and Figure 12-16 (p. 227)**; why the periscope went (pp. 282 to 285); **Figure 17-7(b), the MA-9 instrument panels (p. 291)**; the pilot's report (pp. 349 to 358); the air-ground transcript (pp. 385 to 441) | A printed page is the PDF's page less 9. It has no description of spacecraft 20 as a whole, no weights and no table of events planned and flown. Figure 17-7(b) is four inches across with most legends illegible; Figures 12-5 and 12-14 are sketches. It prints the Atlas's acceptance as "March 15, 1962" |
| *Mercury-Atlas 9 Press Kit*, NASA, May 1963 | [NTRS 19630007867](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19630007867) | The experiments as planned: the beacon "mounted in a cylindrical canister on the retropack" and its 92° to the flight path (pp. 9 and 10); the Geiger counters (p. 15); the balloon's "three-inch-diameter cylindrical container located in the antenna canister under the destabilizing flap" and its orange Mylar (pp. 16 to 19); **the television camera "on a bracket to the right of the pilot's hand controller", "normally directed at the pilot" (pp. 20 to 22)**; the spacecraft: fuel 66 lb in three tanks (p. 30), the batteries (p. 31), humidity among the environmental displays (p. 32), **the spring-loaded umbilical door and its lights (p. 33)**, the pilot's desk (p. 34), the second oxygen bottle (p. 35), what was removed (p. 36); Atlas 130-D at the Cape on 18 March 1963 and erected on the 22nd, "baffle injection engines", "a hypergolic start" (pp. 44 to 46) | Written before the flight: plans, not results. Round figures. Its page numbers are its own; the PDF's are two more |
| *The Triumph of Astronaut L. Gordon Cooper, Jr. and the Faith 7, May 15-16, 1963*, NASA Manned Spacecraft Center, 1963 | [NTRS 19630008975](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19630008975) | **Cooper's height and weight (p. 1)**; the powered flight by the second, the tower gone at 154 s (p. 11); the experiments as flown, the beacon out at 3 h 25 min (pp. 14 to 19); the television's weight (p. 20); **the white paint patches: "three test panels were 6-inch squares baked onto a single test shingle located at the small end of the conical section" (p. 21)**; **the suit's changes: boots integrated, the life vest moved to the lower left leg, new shoulders and glove wrists (p. 22)** | A picture book for the public, two columns of captions; a printed page is the PDF's less 2 |
| *This New Ocean* (above), chapter 14, pp. 489 to 503 | as above | Spacecraft No. 20 chosen, 22 orbits decided on 9 November 1962 (p. 489); Atlas 130-D rolled out on 30 January 1963, failed inspection, accepted on 15 March; **"all 183 of the changes"; what was added and what the periscope, the rate control system and the transmitters weighed; the couch nearly replaced by a hammock; 3,026.3 lb in orbit (p. 490)**; the booster engines' offset, the mating on 22 April (p. 491); the name (p. 492); 14 May (pp. 492 to 494); the flight orbit by orbit, period 88 min 45 s, inclination 32.55° (pp. 494 to 501) | Calls the television a "monitor". Its table of flights gives 34:03:30 |
| NASA TN D-4807 (above, under Mercury-Atlas 8), pp. 61 and 73 | as above | What No. 20 carried over from No. 16 and this flight's own sources only name: the HF dipole antenna on the retropack (two tapes of 14 ft) and the Hasselblad 500C | Of spacecraft 16: that No. 20's were the same articles in the same places is assumed |
| NASA photographs of spacecraft No. 20 before the flight: **S63-06259 (14 May) and S63-09676-E (15 May, in colour): Cooper going aboard, from the side of the first UNITED STATES**; S63-07521 (15 May, in colour, close and square on: the last letters of UNITED STATES, the window under its orange cover, CAUTION THERMOCOUPLE stencilled at the seams, the open hatch); S63-01922 (Cooper suited beside the capsule in the hangar); S63-06129 (in the couch); S63-03960 and S63-03965 (with the instrument panel out of the capsule, 1 February 1963) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/s63-06259); Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Mercury-Atlas 9* and *Category:Faith 7* | **The three white squares in a column over UNITED; UNITED STATES low on the cone with its flag under it; the window's cover and, under it, the emblem: a white pointed field with FAITH and a 7 on it**; a small louvred panel beside STATES; the suit's wrist rings, thigh port and harness | The emblem is at the cone's limb in both, almost edge on: its shape cannot be told for certain. S63-03960 is of the panel three months before the flight, with its centre section not yet fitted |
| NASA photographs after the flight: GPN-2000-001335 (Cooper backing out of the hatch on the deck: the hatch's sill, UNITED STATES and the flag to its right, the seams); KSC-63-MA9-174 (the capsule in its collar, from above); **S63-07701 (on the deck, in colour, from the window's side: the window level with UNITED STATES, the hatch beside it, the emblem under it scorched pale, a round port above the window)** and GPN-2000-001439 (on the deck) | Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Recovery of Mercury-Atlas 9* | **The second UNITED STATES: beside the hatch, between the seams at its head and foot**; **KSC-63-MA9-174: the seam round the cone just above the first UNITED, the one tall row of shingles above it with the three squares on one shingle, a plain plate at the limb on the side of the pilot's feet**, a round fitting high on the cone; no paint from about 100° to 170° | After the entry: the white squares have gone grey. KSC-63-MA9-174 looks down the cone: stations scaled off it are good to an inch or two |
| The later commemorative insignia (Commons: *Faith 7 insignia.jpg*; 172 px) | Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Logo of Mercury-Atlas 9* | A white five-pointed star with FAITH over a 7: what the emblem is drawn as | Made after the flight (Commons says of a sibling file that such patches came "much after"); not a photograph of the capsule |
| NASA photographs of the vehicle: 63C-1417 (GPN-2000-000998: on the pad on 14 May, in colour), LOC-63C-1556, S63-07608 | Wikimedia Commons, *Category:Atlas 130D* | **"130 D" on the fuel tank** either side of the fairing above a vernier; UNITED STATES down the oxygen tank; the red clamp ring; the tower's red | From hundreds of feet |
| Space Center Houston: the capsule as kept (Commons, *Category:Faith 7 at Space Center Houston*) | Wikimedia Commons | Where it is; UNITED STATES and the flag beside the hatch, as in 1963 | Hung nose down in coloured light with the hatch off. Not read for anything a restorer could have changed |
| *The Triumph* (above), pp. 7 and 21; NASA SP-45 (above), pp. 102, 103 and 264 and Table 18-II (p. 301); the press kit (above), p. 47 | as above | **The morning at the pad** (`pad.ts`): woken 2:50; the van from Hangar S at 5:02 to "its parking place near the elevator at Pad 14"; Cooper out of it at 5:32, "accepted the best wishes of the pad crew and entered the elevator", aboard at 5:36 (*The Triumph*, p. 7); one hold of 4 minutes at T-11 min 30 s and liftoff at 8:04:13, with the normal 2 s of hold-down (SP-45, pp. 102 and 103); the 14th's two hours at T-60 for "the diesel generator used for moving the gantry" (p. 264); the planned count: aboard T-120, hatch T-105, a hold at T-65, "Service tower removed" T-50, the umbilical ejected at T-35 s (press kit, p. 47); **the suit's changes for this flight** (*The Triumph*, p. 21); the pilot's weight that morning, 147 lb (Table 18-II) | No point of the 15th's count is given by the clock: the structure's removal is put at the plan's T-50 (assumed), which with the plan's insertion at T-120 and a 30-minute hold at T-65 comes six minutes over the liftoff; the plan is not the day |

At 12:00: GA1 `01,1,+22000,9,00` (one okta of cirrus), 16 km. At 15:00: GA1 `02,1,+03000,9,03` (two oktas of altocumulus at 3,000 m). Patrick at 13:00: 230° at 3.1 m/s, 19 km, `GF1 02` | Decoded by us. **Drawn**: the flight's `weather.ts` |

| NASA SP-45 (above), pp. 13, 102, 103, 124, 151 and 357, Tables 3-I and 8-III, and the air-ground transcript, pp. 385, 386, 440 and 442; *This New Ocean* (NASA SP-4201), pp. 494, 496, 499 and 500; the press kit (above), "Events during powered flight" | as above | **The flight as flown** (`orbital.ts`, mission.md): BECO 132.5 s, staging 135.4, SECO 303.03, the roll and pitch programmes' ends, the burnout against the plan (pp. 102 and 103); the orbit, 87.2 by 144.2 nautical miles (p. 13), 88 min 45 s at 32.55° (*This New Ocean*, p. 496); **the retrofire by hand at 33:59:30 g.e.t.** (p. 151), at 34° by the reticle (*This New Ocean*, p. 500); the capsule's clock 16 s fast by the fifteenth orbit (p. 499) and the transcript's "fire" at 33:59:53; the pack, the 0.05 g the ground gave, the drogue and the main in the transcript; the drogue at 42,000 ft (the pilot, p. 357); "About 34 feet per second" (p. 442); "about 4.7 days" of orbit left (p. 124); the pick-up point (Table 8-III); the plan's cutoff at 528,400 ft and 25,700 ft/s and the tower at 154 s (the press kit) | No weights, no loads, no events planned and flown, no cutoff conditions: the *Postlaunch Memorandum Report* for MA-9 (three parts, NASA MSC, 1963) was not found. The period and the heights do not agree as a nodal period (mission.md, "The period") |

Known and not read: the *Postlaunch Memorandum Report for Mercury-Atlas No. 9 (MA-9)* (NASA MSC, June 1963), which
would give the differences from spacecraft 16 one by one, the weights, and the events planned and flown: a search of
NASA's report server did not find it, nor any Technical Note made from it as TN D-3814 and TN D-4807 were made from
the two flights' before; the Manned One-Day Mission's planning documents, among them the *MA-9/20 Mission
Directive* and the *Technical Information Summary* that *This New Ocean* cites; McDonnell's flight operations manual
for capsule 20 (SEDR 109-20), which would give the panel dimensioned and every legend; any photograph of the emblem
square on, of the capsule between 60° and 170° before the flight, or of its retropack; Langley's drawings of the
flashing beacon's canister and of the Geiger package; any record of the 15th's count by the clock, or of the weather
at the pad as the flight's own people observed it.

### The Cape

Where things stood on 5 May 1961, for the scenery beyond Complex 5/6 (`missions/usa/places/cape-canaveral/scenery.ts`;
`missions/usa/places/cape-canaveral/lc-5.md`, "Beyond the complex"), and what the land, its plants, the
sea and the air were like (`missions/usa/places/cape-canaveral/ground.ts`; `lc-5.md`, "The land").

| Source | Where | Good for | Caveats |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| OpenStreetMap, Cape Canaveral: the pad of each launch complex (LC-5 relation 16884747; LC-26 relations 16639149 and 16671991; LC-17 ways 1223354955 and 1223354950; LC-18 ways 1223263907 and 1222374142; LC-9 way 1057023594; LC-25 ways 1224886198 and 1348762839; LC-29 way 1225540370; LC-30 ways 1224834012 and 1224845401; LC-31 and 32 ways 1222374138 and 1222347130; LC-21 way 1219668680; LC-11 to 16 ways 195727088, 195727089, 1248266814, 195727091, 195727092, 195727093; LC-19 way 195727094; LC-20 way 1300095560; LC-34 way 62293415), Cape Canaveral Light (way 1219644136), the coastline (ways 22314664, 23117213, 22322002) and the Banana River's shore (ways 23117210, 22321610); the Indian River Lagoon (relation 1204544, the Banana River's two shores south of 28.42 and its west shore throughout, and the Indian River's east shore) | [openstreetmap.org](https://www.openstreetmap.org/#map=13/28.4700/-80.5600) | Every position used: each pad's centre to about 20 m, from which bearing and distance from pad 5 are computed | Present-day mapping of pads and ruins. The shore is today's, not 1961's. McDowell's pad list differs by about 150 m for Complex 25 |

| Cape Canaveral Space Force Museum, *Launch Complex 26 Gantry*, *Launch Complex 26 Blockhouse*, *Launch Complex 26 Launch Pads*, *Launch Complexes 3/4*, and the dated photographs on its *Launch Complex 26* page (Juno II at 26B, 23 January, 24 February and 27 April 1961; a tactical Jupiter, 22 April 1961); its photograph of the gantry's blueprint, "Missile Service Stand" | `https://ccspacemuseum.org/artifacts/launch-complex-26-gantry/`, `-blockhouse/`, `-launch-pads/`; `/facilities/launch-complex-26/`, `/launch-complexes-3-4/` | Complex 26 as built (`places/cape-canaveral/complexes.md`): one gantry for two pads, 111 ft 9 in to its top rail, five platforms, the top enclosed; its twin's base, 61 by 26 ft; the blockhouse 400 ft from the pads, walls 2 ft, a vaulted roof 5 to 8 ft thick, ports of 45 sheets of glass; a scale platform, a generator building, a fuel-truck revetment and two monitors behind 5 ft walls at each pad; the cable trenches. The aerial of 23 January 1961 gives the apron, the gantry's arrangement and which way its base lay | Secondary.
