### The Earth and the sky

What `src/world/` is built from (bodies, time, frames, ephemeris, stars) and checked against.

| Source | Where | Good for | Caveats |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Meeus, *Astronomical Algorithms*, 2nd ed., Willmann-Bell, 1998 | book | The methods: sidereal time (ch. 12), precession (ch. 21), nutation (ch. 22), the Sun (ch. 25, low accuracy), the Moon (ch. 47, cut to its 32 and 30 largest terms), the Moon's phase and bright limb (ch. 48), the Moon's librations and the Sun's place over it (ch. 53: `world/moon.ts`), planets' magnitudes (ch. 41), refraction (ch. 16); its worked examples are the unit tests | The Sun to 0.01°; the Moon as cut here to about 0.01°. Chapter 53's series for the physical librations were set down from memory and are held to his example 53.a (rho, sigma and tau to five places) and to Horizons |
| JPL Horizons, DE441 | [ssd.jpl.nasa.gov/horizons](https://ssd.jpl.nasa.gov/horizons/) | Reference values: airless apparent azimuth, elevation and range of the Sun, Moon and planets from LC-5, Baikonur, LC-39A, Greenwich and 0° N 0° E at five instants (queried November 2026); and, for the Moon from the Earth's centre at four instants from 1961 to 2024 (queried 13 November 2026), the points of the Moon under the Earth and under the Sun, the Sun's elongation and the lit share (tests/moon.test.mjs, tests/front.test.mjs) | Before 1962 its "UT" is UT1. The Moon's frame there is the mean-Earth one: `moonAxes` is within 0.03° of it |
| Park, Folkner, Williams and Boggs, *The JPL Planetary and Lunar Ephemerides DE440 and DE441*, AJ 161:105, 2021; the kernel de440s.bsp (DE440 cut to 1849-2150), read by NAIF's *SPK Required Reading* and *DAF Required Reading* (type 2 segments: Chebyshev positions, km, ICRF, TDB seconds from J2000) | [ssd.jpl.nasa.gov/ftp/eph/planets/bsp](https://ssd.jpl.nasa.gov/ftp/eph/planets/bsp/), [NAIF](https://naif.jpl.nasa.gov/pub/naif/toolkit_docs/C/req/spk.html) | The Sun, the Moon, the Earth-Moon barycentre and the planets' systems, 1957 to 2035, refitted (`pnpm ephemeris`, public/ephemeris/de440.bin) to 6 m and 0.08 mm/s for the Moon and 0.4 to 5 km for the rest, and the Earth-Moon mass ratio (81.30056822) | The paper is cited, not read: the kernel is the source. The planets are their systems' barycentres |
| JPL Horizons, DE441, vector tables (queried 16 November 2026): the Moon from the Earth's centre, ICRF, geometric, at Apollo 8's and Apollo 11's translunar injection and lunar orbit insertion and at Freedom 7's liftoff; the Sun at Apollo 11's liftoff and on 8 April 2024 | [ssd.jpl.nasa.gov/api/horizons.api](https://ssd-api.jpl.nasa.gov/doc/horizons.html) | What `stateOf` is held to (tests/ephemeris.test.mjs): 1 to 3 m and 0.01 mm/s for the Moon | Its observer range is astrometric (where the light left, in the solar system's frame): up to 40 km from the Moon's geometric distance, which the test allows for. Before 1962 its "UT" is UT1 and its TT is UT1 plus its own delta-T, 0.33 s from the leap-second table's in May 1961 |
| NASA Manned Spacecraft Center, *Apollo 11 Mission Report*, MSC-00171, November 1969: table 7-I (what each trajectory parameter is), table 7-II (the states at each event of the coast: geodetic latitude, longitude, altitude, space-fixed velocity, flight-path angle and heading) and table 7-III (the pericynthion each translunar manoeuvre resulted in), section 7.4 | [nasa.gov, A11_MissionReport.pdf](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/static/apollo50th/pdf/A11_MissionReport.pdf) | What a coast on the gravity terms is held to (tests/gravity.test.mjs): from the states after the separation manoeuvre and the first midcourse correction, the pericynthion within what the published digits allow | Read as text from the scan: a few figures came out garbled and were read off neighbouring columns and Apollo by the Numbers. The injection state's pericynthion (896.3 n mi) is not reproduced from its own state: 205 n mi nearer |
| Orloff, *Apollo by the Numbers*, NASA SP-2000-4029, 2000: each flight's ascent, Earth orbit, translunar and lunar orbit tables, the timelines, and the table of translunar injection conditions (GET, altitude, speeds, geocentric and geodetic latitude, longitude, angles, inclination, eccentricity, C3) | [nasa.gov, sp-4029.pdf](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/04/sp-4029.pdf) | The ground elapsed times of Apollo 8's and 11's injection and lunar orbit insertion (tests/ephemeris.test.mjs); the injection state to more digits, which is self-consistent (its radius from C3 within 1 m of WGS 84's for its place) | Apollo 8's injection state has its place only in the injection table, and no pericynthion resulting from it was found to fly it against |

C01 is smoothed: the fortnightly tides (a millisecond) are not in it. Polar motion (under 15 m) is not used |

Its area agrees with the sea's in 1960: level 53.4 m, about 67,500 to 68,000 km², 1,090 km³ (Micklin, "Desiccation of the Aral Sea: a water management disaster in the Soviet Union", *Science* 241: 1170-1176, 1988), which began to fall in 1961. ### The light at night

What the night is lit by, and what the eye does with it (`viewer/daysky.ts`, `viewer/eye.ts`, `viewer/lamps.ts`; read
16 November 2026). Each figure's confidence is in the code beside it.

| Source | Where | Good for | Caveats |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Janiczek and DeYoung, *Computer programs for sun and moon illuminance with contingent tables and diagrams*, U.S. Naval Observatory Circular 171, 1987 (DTIC ADA182110), subroutine ATMOS and Appendix B | [archive.org, DTIC_ADA182110](https://archive.org/details/DTIC_ADA182110); its constants as the R package skylight carries them, [bluegreen-labs/skylight](https://github.com/bluegreen-labs/skylight) | **Twilight and the Sun's light on level ground** (`circular`, `sunLux`): IS = 133,775 M lux, M its spherical atmosphere's beam (e^-0.21 a thickness) and sky (0.0289 e^-0.042 a thickness) terms; 124,000 lux overhead, 757 at the horizon, 3.0 at -6°, 0.0044 at -12°. Its sky term on the Moon is how the Moon lights the sky (`moonLight`) | Documented. Fitted to Jones and Condit (1948) and Brown (1952) down to nautical twilight; under -12° it falls away to nothing, which is where the night sky takes over. It is given the apparent elevation (the circular refracts first). Its own night-sky constant is 0.0003 lux in the text and 0.0005 in its code; its cloud divisors (2, 3, 10) are not used. Its Moon's phase law (Lumme and Bowell) is not used: Krisciunas and Schaefer's is |
| U.S. National Park Service, Natural Sounds and Night Skies Division, *Metrics Guide to Night Skies*; Duriscoe, *Measuring anthropogenic sky glow using a natural sky brightness model*, PASP 125:1370, 2013, and his slides *Sky glow* (DOE, Denver 2016) | [nps.gov, NSQmetrics](https://www.nps.gov/subjects/nightskies/NSQmetrics.html), [energy.gov, duriscoe_sky-glow_denver2016.pdf](https://www.energy.gov/sites/prod/files/2016/12/f34/duriscoe_sky-glow_denver2016.pdf) | **The night's floor** (`NIGHT_LUX`, `NIGHT_FLOOR`): a natural sky's horizontal illuminance 0.8 millilux; its mean luminance 250 µcd/m², 172 at the zenith; its share as airglow 48 %, zodiacal light 28 %, the Galaxy 19 %, stars 5 % | The NPS page read; the 2013 paper cited through the slides, which do not say whether the shares are of luminance or of illuminance (taken as of illuminance). Solar minimum; at maximum the airglow is half a magnitude brighter. Leinert et al. 1998 and Roach and Gordon 1973, the reviews behind it, were not reached |
| Falchi et al., *The new world atlas of artificial night sky brightness*, Science Advances 2:e1600377, 2016 | [PMC4928945](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC4928945/) | A check on the floor: a natural sky background of 22.0 mag/arcsec², 174 µcd/m² | |
| Krisciunas and Schaefer, *A model of the brightness of moonlight*, PASP 103:1033, 1991, equations 8, 9 and 20 | [ADS](https://ui.adsabs.harvard.edu/abs/1991PASP..103.1033K) | **The Moon** (`FULL_MOON`, `moonPhase`): above the air 10^(-0.4 (3.84 + 0.026 \|α\| + 4e-9 α⁴)) foot-candles, 0.313 lux full, 0.029 at a quarter | Their equation 9 is Allen's (1976), not read. The surge of the last 7° about full (35 % more) and the distance (0.12 mag either way) are left out |
| Durand and Dorsey, *Interactive tone mapping*, Eurographics Workshop on Rendering, 2000: §3.2, §3.3, equations 1 and 2 | [graphics.cs.yale.edu](https://graphics.cs.yale.edu/sites/default/files/tonemap.pdf) | **The eye's light adaptation** (`LIGHT_SECONDS`): τ = 0.4 s for the rods, 0.1 s for the cones; the rods' threshold-versus-intensity of Ferwerda et al. (flat for log La ≤ -3.94: `EYE_LEAST`); Hunt's blue of a white in the dark, RGB (1.05, 0.97, 1.27), for the rods' look | Ferwerda, Pattanaik, Shirley and Greenberg, *A model of visual adaptation for realistic image synthesis*, SIGGRAPH 1996, is cited through this paper: its own text was not reached |
| Pattanaik, Tumblin, Yee and Greenberg, *Time-dependent visual adaptation for fast realistic image display*, SIGGRAPH 2000 | [ACM](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/344779.344810) (read in a third party's copy) | **The eye's dark adaptation** (`DARK_SECONDS`): pigment regeneration τ = 110 s for the cones, 400 s for the rods; neural adaptation in 80 to 150 ms; the pupil's 2 to 8 mm worth only a factor of ten | The rods' 400 s, and the half hour of a full dark adaptation (below), are not followed: the eye here comes back over the cones' time |
| Hecht, Haig and Chase, *The influence of light adaptation on subsequent dark adaptation of the eye*, J. Gen. Physiol. 20:831, 1937; Lamb and Pugh, *Phototransduction, dark adaptation, and rhodopsin regeneration*, IOVS 47:5138, 2006 | [PMC2141534](https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC2141534/), [IOVS](https://iovs.arvojournals.org/article.aspx?articleid=2163994) | What is not followed: the rods' break at 10 to 13 minutes, their recovery at 0.24 log units a minute, about six log units in all over half an hour | Background for `DARK_SECONDS` |
| Larson, Rushmeier and Piatko, *A visibility matching tone reproduction operator for high dynamic range scenes*, IEEE TVCG 3:291, 1997 | [IEEE](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/document/646233) | The rods' luminance from XYZ, V = Y (1.33 (1 + (Y + Z)/X) - 1.68) (stage/pipeline.ts `scotopic`) | Cited as Durand and Dorsey and the tone-mapping literature give it; not re-read |
| CIE 41 (1978), the mesopic range, about 0.01 to 3 cd/m², as Wikipedia's *Mesopic vision* gives it | [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mesopic_vision) | Where the rods give way to the cones (stage.ts `MESOPIC`) | Secondary. CIE 191 (2010) has 0.005 to 5 |
| CIE 146:2002, *CIE equations for disability glare* (with CIE 147), extending Stiles and Holladay's L = 10 E / θ² (E at the eye in lux, θ in degrees, 1° to 30°) | [cie.co.at](https://cie.co.at/publications/cie-collection-glare-2002) | The veil a lamp in view lays over what the eye adapts to (eye.ts `GLARE`) | Read as summaries (the CIE's page, TNO's abstract), not the report. Taken on the scene's own scale of the lamps, without the age term |
| GE Lamp Catalogue, 1960, pp. 52 and 55 (H25-DE mercury-vapour: warm-up 7 minutes); Westinghouse H33-1-GL/W (1969: 5 minutes), both as lamptech.co.uk quotes them | [lamptech.co.uk](https://www.lamptech.co.uk/) | How long a pad's floods take to come up (lamps.ts `DUTY.warm`, 300 s) | Secondary, a collector's site citing the catalogues. What lamps lit Complex 5 and Site 1 is not recorded (nothing found in English or Russian): the kind is an estimate, and so is when they were switched on and off |

### The flight as flown

What `flight.ts`'s `trajectory` and `checks` and `src/sim/` rest on.

| Source | Where | Good for | Caveats |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Postlaunch Report for Mercury-Redstone No. 3*, sections 3.1, 4.1, 4.2, 10.2 and the air-ground transcript | as above | **The checks**: event times; height, speed, climb angle and heading at separation, space-fixed and Earth-fixed; range, greatest height, dynamic pressures and loads; capsule weight in each configuration; separation and retrofire velocities; the retro attitude flown (20°); every hold in the count; the parachute's reefing as the pilot called it. Figures 4.2-3 to 4.2-8: time histories | The 8,107 km/h (7,388 ft/s) is space-fixed, at capsule separation; Earth-fixed it was 6,414 ft/s. Section 2.0 gives the .05 g relay as 07:38 where the table has 07:48.2 |
| NASA TM X-53107, Table 3-1, Figure 4-9, Table 8-1, Table 8-4 | as above | Tilt programme: begins 15.0 s on MR-3, arrested 131.7 s, the pulse programme drawn to 41°; MR-3's sea-level thrust (78,860 lb), specific impulse (214.8 s) and liftoff weight (66,098 lb); booster dry weight 8,195 lb | Figure 4-9 is the earlier flights' programme (begins 24.3 s); MR-3's own curve is not given. Section 8.3.1's prose has cutoff at 141.3 s and separation at 141.8 s, which the Postlaunch Report contradicts |
| Brown and Moseley, *Summary of Wind-Tunnel Investigations of the Static Longitudinal Stability Characteristics of the Production Mercury Configurations at Mach Numbers from 0.05 to 20*, NASA TM X-491, 1961 | [NTRS 19710069960](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19710069960/downloads/19710069960.pdf) | Drag coefficient at no angle of attack against Mach number: Figure 20 (capsule with escape tower), Figure 22 (re-entry, 1.6 above Mach 4); reference area the capsule's 74.5 in cross-section | Figures read to 0.02 or 0.03. Nothing on the capsule mounted on a Redstone |
| Aircraft Engine Historical Society, *The Redstone Engine* | [enginehistory.org](https://www.enginehistory.org/Museums/USSRC/USSRC_Redstone.shtml) | The A-7 rated 78,000 lbf at sea level and 89,000 lbf in vacuum; chamber pressure 318 psia | Secondary; its specific impulses (235 and 265 s) are the Hydyne engine's, not Mercury-Redstone's alcohol. Used only as a cross-check on the nozzle exit area derived from the flight's 6.3 g |
| *U.S. Standard Atmosphere, 1976*, NOAA-S/T 76-1562 | NOAA / NASA / USAF | The air (`src/sim/atmosphere.ts`): the defining layers to 86 km, Table I's densities above (pp. 68 to 73, every 10 km to 300 km and on to 1,000; read from the NTRS scan, 19770009539, November 2026) | A mean year at mid-latitude. The flight was planned on the annual Patrick AFB atmosphere, which we do not have |
| **NOT READ: quoted from memory, to verify.** Sutton and Graves, *A General Stagnation-Point Convective-Heating Equation for Arbitrary Gas Mixtures*, NASA TR R-376, 1971 | [NTRS 19720003329](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19720003329) (the link was not opened either) | **The heat flux** (`src/sim/heating.ts`): q = K sqrt(density / nose radius) speed³ at a blunt body's stagnation point, with K = 1.7415e-4 kg^0.5/m for air | The equation and the constant are written down from memory, as they are commonly quoted from the report; the report was not opened, and no worked example of its own is reproduced in the tests. Verify both, and the units, before anything depends on the flux. A cold-wall, hypersonic fit: below about Mach 3 it says only that the heating is small. The wall-enthalpy correction and everything about how the flux is shared over the surfaces are ours |
| **NOT READ: quoted from memory, to verify.** *CRC Handbook of Chemistry and Physics* (properties of the elements; thermal properties of metals) | no edition or page recorded | Beryllium: 1,848 kg/m³, 1,825 J/(kg K) at 25 °C, melts at 1,287 °C (the heat shield and the recovery compartment's shingles as lumps) | Remembered as the handbook's figures; the handbook was not opened. All three are `estimated` in `flight.ts`, and so is every mass per square metre worked from the density. Specific heat is held constant, though beryllium's is remembered to rise by half by 600 K |
| **NOT READ: quoted from memory, to verify.** The maker's data sheet for René 41 (General Electric; today Haynes International's *Haynes R-41 alloy*), or MIL-HDBK-5 | none recorded | René 41: 8.25 g/cm³, 0.108 Btu/(lb °F) (452 J/(kg K)) at room temperature, used to about 1,800 °F (1,255 K): the afterbody shingles | Remembered, not looked up: `estimated`. Hammack and Heberlig (p. 5) do say the shingles "will sustain temperatures in the 1,600° F to 1,800° F range", which is the limit's only support in anything read |
| **NOT READ: quoted from memory, to verify.** A handbook figure for 5052 aluminium (ASM *Metals Handbook*, MIL-HDBK-5 or the Aluminum Association's data) | none recorded | 5052 aluminium: 2.68 g/cm³, 880 J/(kg K) at room temperature, losing its strength from about 200 °C (475 K): the booster's aft skin | Remembered, not looked up: `estimated`. The emissivities in `heating` (0.5 beryllium, 0.85 René 41 and white paint; bare polished beryllium about 0.2, oxidised about 0.6) are judgements with no source at all |
| Hammack and Heberlig (above), pp. 4 to 9, 15 and 25, read again for an audit of the simulator's inputs (November 2026; text taken from the NTRS scan, p. 5 checked against the page image) | as above | p. 4: the escape rocket as 58,500 lbf. p. 5: Mercury-Redstone heating: peak stagnation-point rate "less than 5 Btu/ft²/sec", total heat input to the "approximately 1-inch-thick" shield 150 Btu/ft², "a 25° to 30° rise in temperature", afterbody 300 to 400 °F during exit; René 41 shingles 0.016 in, sustaining 1,600 to 1,800 °F, their black oxidised surface raising emissivity. p. 6: the couch tested to 25 g. pp. 7 and 8: the jets' ratings; "a theoretical specific impulse of about 160 pound-seconds"; 32 lb of peroxide for the automatic system and 23 lb for the manual. p. 9: a 6-foot conical-ribbon drogue at 21,000 ft; "a 63-foot, ring-sail chute with alternate international orange and white gores" at 10,000 ft. p. 15: at booster cutoff "the tower clamp ring was released and both the escape and pylon jettison rockets fired to remove the tower". p. 25: "descent velocities of 28 to 30 feet per second were always achieved" | The peroxide loads and the main parachute's gore colours are `documented` from these pages. The specific impulse is theoretical (the simulator's 150 s is a delivered figure taken under it, `estimated`); the 25 g is a test come through, not a limit (the 20 g limit stays `estimated`). The heating figures are for the Mercury-Redstone flights as a class and are set beside the model's in the flight's README; they are not `checks`, the comparison having been seen before any tolerance was set |
| Launch photographs, the cloud over the pad: S61-02407 and LOD-61-4732 (MR-3, on the stand at ignition, one elevated camera, the boom swinging); S61-02408, MSFC-6414825 and the colour *Mercury 3* (MR-3 a few metres up); MSFC-6100884 and S61-01928 (MR-3 along the rails); GPN-2000-000859, LOC-61C-883 and 61-MR3-74 (MR-3 10 to 20 m up, then two or three of its heights); *Mercury-Redstone 3 - liftoff* and S63-22731 (MR-2, from the ground); S61-03121 and MSFC-6414824 (MR-4) | Wikimedia Commons (by these names), [nasa.gov](https://www.nasa.gov/image-feature/liftoff-of-alan-shepards-freedom-7-mission), [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/) | **The cloud over the pad** (trail.ts `blast`): a knobbly white cloud hugging the stand at ignition, about 8 m across and 3 m high, then about 20 m across and 5 m high (MR-4's 35 m and 6 m), on every side; once the vehicle is a few metres up the jet splashes out flame-lit at the stand's foot and the cloud round it is gone; a translucent white-to-tan veil 20 to 50 m out and 5 to 10 m high, densest in the lowest 3 m; the apron clear but for wisps at about T+6 | Sizes read against the booster's 1.78 m and 25.4 m, in oblique views: estimates. Moments are inferred from the vehicle's height, not timed. Close views looking up (S61-02409, MR3-9, S61-01908) show white billows that may be the morning's cumulus, so are not used. No film was watched |
| *Mercury-Redstone Project*, TM X-53107 (above), table 5-1 and section 7 | as above | The start: "LOX and water lead start"; "Liftoff occurred when approximately 85 percent of rated thrust was attained"; no deluge anywhere in the pad's description | The time from ignition to liftoff is not given |
| NWS Melbourne, *Mercury launches: weather* | [weather.gov/smg/mercury](https://www.weather.gov/smg/mercury) | MR-3: scattered low cloud, the count held at T-15 for it | No surface wind: the wind that carries the cloud over the pad is the clouds' (missions/usa/places/cape-canaveral/ground.ts) at six tenths, estimated |
| **NOT READ: seen only as a search snippet.** Spaceline, *Redstone* fact sheet | [spaceline.org/?p=7106](https://www.spaceline.org/?p=7106) | "A cone-shaped flame deflector was located directly beneath the stand" | The page did not open. The model's deflector is four-sided (lc-5.md, from SP-4204): which it was is not settled here |
| Wyman, Sloan and Shirley, *Simple Analytic Approximations to the CIE XYZ Color Matching Functions*, Journal of Computer Graphics Techniques 2(2), 2013 | [jcgt.org](https://jcgt.org/published/0002/02/01/) | The colour of a black body at a temperature (`src/viewer/entry.ts`): Planck's law through the 1931 observer, to linear sRGB | Colour only. How bright a glow is drawn is a curve of ours |

### Aborts off the pad

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Blanchard and Raper, *Full-Scale Flight Test From Sea Level of an Abort-Escape System for a Project Mercury Capsule*, NASA TM X-422, October 1960 | [NTRS 19670022198](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19670022198/downloads/19670022198.pdf) | **The one pad abort whose inputs and results have all been read** (pp. 5 to 8 and Table I checked against the page images): the Grand Central motor on Langley's full-scale model; nozzles canted 19 degrees; the motor "canted in order to obtain a misalinement of the thrust vector of 0.78 inch"; weights, centres of gravity and inertias at take-off, burnout and alone (Table I); launched at 71 degrees of elevation; 1,950 ft, about 1,600 ft from take-off, 63.1 s, 595 ft/s, 22 g for 0.1 s, about 1 1/4 turns in pitch before the tower went, 28 ft/s onto the water. **Figure 9: the motor's thrust against time on the stand**, with its legend (burning time 0.601 s, action time 1.248 s, 56,795 lb-s between the 10 per cent points, 290.5 lb of propellant) | A boilerplate with its own parachutes and g-switched timers, not a production capsule. Figure 9 is "typical": one firing, read here to about 1,000 lbf. Figures 12 to 18 (the trajectory and accelerations against time) have not been read point by point |
| Baber, Carter and English, *Flight Test of a Little Joe Boosted Full-Scale Spacecraft Model and Escape System for Project Mercury*, NASA TM X-629 | [NTRS 19670022650](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19670022650/downloads/19670022650.pdf) | p. 7 (checked against the page image): with "current production Mercury components", "The thrust axis of the escape rocket was misalined about 1 inch to the side of the spacecraft-escape-system center of gravity"; the motor as 1-KS-52000, 52,000 lbf for about 0.78 s; the jettison motor 785 lbf for about 1.3 s | "About 1 inch", and of that flight's system: which way it was set on a Mercury-Redstone is not said |
| McDonnell, SEDR 109-7 (above), p. 2-2, "Launch abort" | as above | Capsule 7's sequence after an abort: retropack jettisoned, tower at maximum altitude, drogue 2 s after the tower, main 2 s later below 10,000 ft or at 10,000 ft, the normal sequence above 42,000 ft | Read from the OCR text, which is clean on this page, not checked against the page image; August 1960 |
| SEDR 104 (above), section 6-4 and the abort sequence in section 5 | as above | Nozzles canted 19 degrees; the thrust alignment mirror; "For off the pad escapes the rocket will obtain a maximum capsule altitude of approximately 2500 feet"; the retropack dropped on an abort, the tower let go by the maximum altitude sensor, two 2-second timers | The orbital capsule; no figure for the thrust line's offset. OCR text |
| *This New Ocean* (above), pp. 141 and 261 to 262 | as above | The pylon deliberately misaligned "about one inch"; the Beach Abort of 9 May 1960: "one minute and 16 seconds but covering half a mile in an arc 2465 feet high", from a "canted pad" with "its pylon toward the sea" | A history: no weight for spacecraft No. 1 that day, no launcher angle. Wikipedia's *Beach Abort* gives conflicting figures and is not used |
| Pearson, NASA TM X-547 and TM X-324 (wind-tunnel tests of modified escape configurations) | [NTRS 19630010659](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19630010659/downloads/19630010659.pdf), [19670022687](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19670022687/downloads/19670022687.pdf) | Opened only: TM X-547 mentions "a canted face on the escape rocket to increase the miss distance" | Not read for the escape configuration's stability, which the simulator's abort wants |

### Known, not yet read

| Source | Would settle |
| --- | --- |
| McDonnell's general-arrangement and outer-mould-line drawings of the Mercury capsule; the SEDR 109 issues for capsules 11, 13, 16, 18 and 20 (only SEDR 109-7, of August 1960, has been read) | A station for the window, which no document read gives; the hatch's stations; where the cone meets the cylinder |
| A weather chart, sounding or reanalysis for 5 May and 21 July 1961 (the Weather Bureau's Daily Weather Map; the 20th Century Reanalysis); Sassen and Cho, "Subvisual-thin cirrus lidar dataset for satellite verification and climatological research", *J. Appl. Meteor.* 31, 1992 | Everything in each day's weather map that is not a record's word (viewer/weather.ts); the classes of cirrus by optical depth that types.ts quotes at second hand (under 0.3 thin, to 3 the Sun's disc still shows) and the veil's depth is taken within |
| MSFC *Firing Site Weight Report* for MR-7 (cited by TM X-53107) | Propellant loads by mass: needed for the simulator |
| An Army article on the Redstone's ground equipment by Capt. Luke A. Vavra, Department of Materiel (about 1961; found only as a search result quoting it, on a page that hosts a copy) | Its account of the XM74 launcher, which "orients the missile vertically and aligns a selected reference fin toward the target": how the launcher's ring turned on its base, and which fin was the reference. The model has the table turning and the legs and deflector not (lc-5.md, "What turns on the launcher": estimated) |
| A drawing of the capsule's index on the Mercury-Redstone adapter, or the MR-3 Postlaunch Report's roll trace through the turnaround | The capsule's clocking on the booster to the degree: the model takes the periscope to lie on the pitch plane exactly (lc-5.md, "Which way round the vehicle stood") |
| MSFC MTP-AERO-61-36, the MR-3 booster flight evaluation | Engine performance, chamber pressure |
| Chrysler, *This Is Redstone*, or an Army Redstone technical manual | Fin geometry and span, nozzle exit diameter, A-7 flow rates |
| NASA STG Working Paper 148 | The capsule description the postlaunch report defers to |
| NASA Space Task Group Working Paper 148 (capsule 7's systems, cited by the MR-3 postlaunch report) | A dimensioned cabin: the panel's station and size, the ports' places, the couch, the latch hatch's mechanism, the cabin lights, the periscope's powers |
| An earlier SEDR 104 covering capsules 5 and 7 | No. 7's hatch and ports; whether its stations differ from the 1961 diagram |
| Corps of Engineers drawings of the LC-5/6 blockhouse (Facility 1207A) and ready-room building; any 1961 interior photograph of its firing rooms | The blockhouse's heights, roof profile, window sizes, room layout and consoles: all estimated today |
| Photographs of the blockhouse and pad 6 on 5 May 1961; the 1961 site plan of Complex 5/6 | Where the cars, the transfer van and pad 6's gantry stood that morning; fences, roads, the turnout's destination |
| A dated aerial photograph or Air Force map of Cape Canaveral, spring 1961 | The 1961 shore; the roads; which way each pad faced; what stood on Complexes 12, 15, 16, 18 and 36 that month; where Complex 26's gantry was; the cleared ground round Complex 5/6 and between it and Complex 26. Searched for and not found (November 2026): the USDA's 1958 photography of Brevard County (University of Florida, UF00071730, flown 23 April 1958) leaves the Cape out, as a military reservation; NASA's image library has no aerial of 5/6 from 1958 to 1962; USGS EarthExplorer's single frames were not searched (it needs a login); Spaceline's *Launch Complex 26* page lists "Launch Complexes 5/6 And 26 Circa 1958", but the site could not be reached |
| Dimensions of the Thor (LC-17), Titan I (LC-15 to 20), Polaris (LC-25, 29) and Pershing (LC-30) service structures | Their heights: all estimated in the scenery |
| Drawings or photographs of the LC-5/6 service structure's lift, ladders and stairs | How people really went up it: only the lift's existence is documented |
| HAER or Air Force historic-property survey of LC-5/6 (none found; tDAR holds a Florida site form for 5/6, 8BR00216, and *Aerial Images, Cape Canaveral (1949-1951)*, document 458772, neither readable without an account); HAER Level II for LC-1/2 and 3/4, DTIC ADA637019 | Measured dimensions of the pad, launcher and service structure |
| Corps of Engineers drawings of the Redstone "Missile Service Stand" (LC-4/5 and LC-26), at full resolution | The structure's base, platform plans and heights, rail gauge |
| Army Redstone ground equipment manuals (launcher, cable masts) | The launch ring's size, levelling and azimuth gear, the masts' form |
| *Emergency Handbook for Pad Area Rescue* (LOD, 1961; TM X-53107 7.4.4) | Where the cherry picker, M-113 and fire trucks stood, and the cab's programmed descent |
| The mobile aerial tower's maker's manual, or TM X-53107's source for 7.3.3.1 | The control station at the rear of the truck: how many levers, which worked what (today invented) |
| An Army technical manual for the 60-inch searchlight (Sperry) and its trailer | The searchlights' size, candela and beam: today a figure for the type from memory |
| Photographs of the pad being cleared on a Mercury-Redstone morning | Whether the trailer SP-No 1, the huts, the shower and the searchlights left before launch, by what, and when |
| MSFC MTP-M-S&M-P-61-16, *Mercury-Redstone MR-3 Flight Evaluation of Propulsion Unit and Associated Systems* (Riquelmy and King, 3 August 1961); MSFC MTP-LOD-MP-61-17.3, *Firing Site Weight Report ... MR-3* (Heezen, 22 May 1961); MSFC MPR-M-61-5, *Description of Mercury-Redstone Launch Vehicle for Flight No. MR-3* | Thrust and flow against time, thrust build-up and tail-off, the weights at liftoff and cutoff to the pound: the simulator's powered flight ends 4% fast on the two summary figures it has, and one per cent in thrust is four in speed |
| MSFC MTP-AERO reports on the Mercury-Redstone's aerodynamics and the MR-3 trajectory (the MR-BD ones are MTP-AERO-61-36 and -43); the MR-3 tilt programme as flown | Drag coefficient of the whole vehicle against Mach number; the tilt programme's own curve for the "flatter trajectory" |
| A surveyed position for pad 5's launcher: the Air Force Eastern Test Range's geodetic survey of the Cape's pads, or an NGS datasheet for a mark on the complex | Latitude, longitude and height of the site's origin: today read off OpenStreetMap (a few metres) with the height estimated |
| Colour launch-day photographs of MR-7 | Lettering colour, the roll pattern's stripe count, structure and vehicle colours |
| Smithsonian 3D scan of Shepard's suit, and the records for his helmet (A19770563001) and gloves | Boots, gloves, helmet fittings, the suit's girths |
| Smithsonian NASM, Grissom's flown suit (A20030178000, transferred from NASA in 2003) and Shepard's glove (A19770563002): known here only from a search's report | The wrist ring's size and the harness as kept; whether Shepard's glove's "ball-bearing lock mechanism" is the bearing his report speaks of |
| A primary record of Grissom's height: his Air Force or NASA medical file, or the 1959 selection data | His stature, which the crew model takes as the 5 ft 7 in commonly printed |
| The MR-4 Postlaunch Memorandum Report (NASA STG, August 1961), which *This New Ocean* cites for the suit | What was new about the parachute harness, and whether the chest parachute flew |
| A heat-shield or shingle temperature measured on MR-3: *Postlaunch Report for Mercury-Redstone No. 3* (the sections on the heat protection system and on temperatures), not read for it; NASA CR-649 for the afterbody's measurements on the orbital flights and how the flux is shared over the afterbody | A check for `src/sim/heating.ts`, which has none: `flight.ts`'s `heating` is held against nothing measured. Hammack and Heberlig's p. 5 gives round figures for the Mercury-Redstone flights (above), set beside the model's in the flight's README and not made checks |
| Sutton and Graves, NASA TR R-376 (listed above as quoted from memory) | The stagnation-point equation and its constant for air, 1.7415e-4 kg^0.5/m in SI: `SUTTON_GRAVES` in `src/sim/heating.ts` |
| A materials handbook opened at the page: the *CRC Handbook*, MIL-HDBK-5, the René 41 data sheet | Density, specific heat (and how it rises with temperature) and melting or working limit of beryllium, René 41 and 5052 aluminium; emissivity of oxidised René 41, of beryllium as finished, and of the booster's white paint: every material figure in `flight.ts`'s `heating` |

The postlaunch report's Figure 3.2-1 is the liftoff photograph the booster's paint is read from;
its Figure 3.1-4 is an axis sketch with no dimensions.

### The Earth's surface from orbit

What the globe's land looks like and how high it stands (`public/globe/relief.*`, `colour.*` and each
region's `.relief.png`, `.colour.jpg` and `.surface.json`, baked by tools/globe-surface.mjs). Both
sources were fetched on 2026-11-11 and are kept out of the repository.

| Source | Where | Used for | Licence and limits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| NOAA National Centers for Environmental Information, *ETOPO 2022 15 Arc-Second Global Relief Model*, 2022, [doi:10.25921/fd45-gt74](https://doi.org/10.25921/fd45-gt74): the ice-surface elevation, at 60 arc-seconds (`ETOPO_2022_v1_60s_N90W180_surface.nc`) and in four 15 arc-second tiles for the Cape (`ETOPO_2022_v1_15s_N30W090`, `N30W075`, `N45W090`, `N45W075_surface.nc`) and four for Tyuratam (`N60E045`, `N60E060`, `N45E045`, `N45E060`) | [the 60 s file](https://www.ngdc.noaa.gov/thredds/fileServer/global/ETOPO2022/60s/60s_surface_elev_netcdf/ETOPO_2022_v1_60s_N90W180_surface.nc); the tiles under [15s_surface_elev_netcdf](https://www.ngdc.noaa.gov/thredds/catalog/global/ETOPO2022/15s/15s_surface_elev_netcdf/catalog.html); [product page](https://www.ncei.noaa.gov/products/etopo-global-relief-model) | Relief and the sea's depth: the globe's six faces from the 60 s grid, the Cape's region (20.5 to 31.5 N, 84.5 to 72.5 W) and Tyuratam's (41 to 50 N, 56 to 70 E) from the 15 s tiles, each texel the mean over its area | The DOI's metadata record gives "SPDX License: Creative Commons Zero v1.0 Universal (CC0-1.0)"; a United States Government work. Its use limitation: "Not to be used for navigation." Heights are above mean sea level (EGM2008). Soundings on the Bahama Banks are sparse: at 26.9 N 78.0 W it has 18 m of water where the bank is nearer 5, and on Cay Sal Bank it has a flat some 5 km across standing 20 m out of the sea, which is not there. Only THREDDS serves the files today, as netCDF; no GeoTIFF folder was found |
| NASA Earth Observatory, *Blue Marble: Next Generation*, base map (no relief shading, no bathymetry), May 2004 (Reto Stöckli; MODIS): `world.200405.3x21600x10800.jpg` and the 500 m tile `world.200405.3x21600x21600.B1.jpg` (90 W to 0, 0 to 90 N) | [base map page](https://science.nasa.gov/earth/earth-observatory/blue-marble-next-generation/base-map/); [the global image](https://assets.science.nasa.gov/content/dam/science/esd/eo/images/bmng/bmng-base/may/world.200405.3x21600x10800.jpg); [tile B1](https://assets.science.nasa.gov/content/dam/science/esd/eo/images/bmng/bmng-base/may/world.200405.3x21600x21600.B1.jpg) | The land's colour: the globe's faces from the global image, the Cape's region from the tile. The sea is cut out and filled from the nearest land, since the viewer draws the sea itself | NASA's [images and media guidelines](https://www.nasa.gov/nasa-brand-center/images-and-media/): NASA content is "generally ... not subject to copyright in the United States" and "NASA should be acknowledged as the source of the material". Visible Earth, where these lived (visibleearth.nasa.gov, image 74092; eoimages.gsfc.nasa.gov/images/imagerecords/74000/74092/), now redirects to science.nasa.gov and the old file server did not answer. One month of one year, cloud-free by composition: May's snow lies on the Arctic. JPEG only (no PNG is served), so shores carry a little ringing. Shallows are painted in it, and are taken for sea where they are blue |
| The same, **April** 2004, the 500 m tile `world.200404.3x21600x21600.C1.jpg` (0 to 90 E, 0 to 90 N) | [tile C1, April](https://assets.science.nasa.gov/content/dam/science/esd/eo/images/bmng/bmng-base/april/world.200404.3x21600x21600.C1.jpg) | The land's colour over Tyuratam's region: April, the month of the flights from it, when the steppe has its short green | As the May row. It is 2004's April, not 1961's: the Aral's dry floor (white and grey salt flats) and the irrigated rice of the Syr Darya's valley are in it, as is the later cosmodrome (its sites and the town of Baikonur, grown since 1961, as pale specks at 500 m); late snow lies on the uplands in the region's north-east corner. Where the region's map has the Aral of 1961 the viewer draws water over the dry floor, so of that change none is seen; the rest is listed in site-1.md, "What the region gets wrong" |

How the air, the sea and the clouds seen from height are worked out (`src/viewer/atmosphere.ts`, `globe.ts`,
`weather.ts`). The weather map itself has no source but each flight's own record (its `weather.ts`): the rest of
it is estimated (weather.ts says how).

| Source | Used for | How it was read |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Hillaire, "A Scalable and Production Ready Sky and Atmosphere Rendering Technique", *Computer Graphics Forum* 39(4), 2020 | The method: a transmittance table, a multiple-scattering table, a ray walked with both; the ozone layer as a tent about 25 km | From memory of the paper; the coefficients below are its own |
| Bruneton and Neyret, "Precomputed Atmospheric Scattering", *Computer Graphics Forum* 27(4), 2008 | The transmittance table's parametrisation; Rayleigh 5.802, 13.558 and 33.1 e-6 per metre with an 8 km scale height | From memory |
| Bucholtz, "Rayleigh-scattering calculations for the terrestrial atmosphere", *Applied Optics* 34(15), 1995 | Where the Rayleigh figures come from | Not read: cited by the two above |
| Serdyuchenko and others, "High spectral resolution ozone absorption cross-sections", *Atmos. Meas. Tech.* 7, 2014 | The Chappuis band's shape, from which ozone's absorption is taken as a mean over each screen primary | From memory, not read again: the three figures are estimated |
| Cox and Munk, "Measurement of the roughness of the sea surface from photographs of the Sun's glitter", *J. Opt. Soc. Am.* 44(11), 1954 | The sea's mean square slope for a wind, 0.003 + 0.00512 U: the breadth of the Sun's glint | From memory |
| Jerlov, *Marine Optics*, 1976 (water types); Morel and Prieur, "Analysis of variations in ocean color", *Limnol. Oceanogr.* 22(4), 1977 | The sea's colour by depth: attenuation of clear ocean water in red, green and blue, and the deep water's own reflectance | From memory, rounded: estimated |

The sky's fixed lights (`src/world/stars.ts`, `public/sky/`, baked by tools/stars.mjs; fetched 2026-11-11).

| Source | Where | Used for | Licence and limits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hoffleit and Warren, *The Bright Star Catalogue*, 5th revised edition (preliminary), 1991: CDS catalogue V/50 | [the same file at the SAO Telescope Data Center](http://tdc-www.harvard.edu/catalogs/bsc5.dat.gz) (CDS's ftp did not answer) | Every star's place (J2000), magnitude, B-V and proper motion: 9,096 stars | Public domain. Places to a thousandth of a degree as kept here; proper motions FK5 |
| NASA/Goddard Space Flight Center Scientific Visualization Studio, *Deep Star Maps 2020* (SVS 4851), `milkyway_2020_4k.exr` | [svs.gsfc.nasa.gov/4851](https://svs.gsfc.nasa.gov/4851) | The Milky Way and the sky's other unresolved light, in celestial coordinates (made from Gaia DR2, Hipparcos-2 and Tycho-2) | NASA's, not copyrighted; credit NASA/GSFC SVS. Kept at half size as a JPEG; how bright it is drawn is estimated |
| ERFA 2.0 (the IAU's SOFA routines, BSD) with Hipparcos places | run once through pyerfa: `atci13`, `era00`, `hd2ae` | The seven stars' azimuths and elevations at liftoff that tests/world.test.mjs holds the sky to | An independent check, not an input |

### The front page: the Moon, and the Earth's day

What the front page's picture is made from (`public/front/`, baked by tools/front-moon.mjs and tools/front-earth.mjs;
fetched 2026-11-13, the downloads kept out of the repository) and what its figures rest on (`src/front/`).

| Source | Where | Used for | Licence and limits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| NASA/Goddard Space Flight Center Scientific Visualization Studio, *CGI Moon Kit* (E. Wright, 2019; SVS 4720): `lroc_color_poles_8k.tif` (the Lunar Reconnaissance Orbiter Camera's wide-angle colour mosaic) and `ldem_64_uint.tif`, `ldem_16_uint.tif` (the Lunar Orbiter Laser Altimeter's heights at 64 and 16 texels a degree, half-metres above a radius of 1,727,400 m) | [svs.gsfc.nasa.gov/4720](https://svs.gsfc.nasa.gov/4720); the files under `svs.gsfc.nasa.gov/vis/a000000/a004700/a004720/` | The Moon's colour and relief: the whole Moon at 5 km a texel, the limb the camera looks across at 3.7 km and 481 m (`moon.*`) | NASA's, not copyrighted; credit NASA's Scientific Visualization Studio (the mosaic is NASA/GSFC/Arizona State University's). The mosaic has the light of its own Sun in it (a crater's wall is brighter than its floor), and its poles are filled from the altimeter. Heights are the altimeter's gridded product: nothing under 474 m across is in them |
| ECMWF, *ERA5 hourly data on single levels from 1940 to present* (Hersbach and others, *Q. J. R. Meteorol. Soc.* 146, 2020; [doi:10.24381/cds.adbb2d47](https://doi.org/10.24381/cds.adbb2d47)): total cloud cover, total column cloud liquid water and ice water, sea ice cover and snow depth for 1961-04-12 06:00 UTC, a quarter of a degree | The public copy Google keeps, ARCO-ERA5 (Carver and Merose, 2023): `gs://gcp-public-data-arco-era5/ar/full_37-1h-0p25deg-chunk-1.zarr-v3`, read over https as Zarr chunks | The Earth's cloud, sea ice and snow as they stood at the instant on show (`earth.day.jpg`) | The Copernicus licence: free to use and to share with the notice "Contains modified Copernicus Climate Change Service information [2026]"; neither the European Commission nor ECMWF is responsible for the use made of it. A reanalysis: a weather model run again over the observations there were, which in 1961 were the surface and balloon networks and no satellite, so the northern continents' weather is well held and the southern oceans' is largely the model's own. Derived, not observed, and the cloud is the model's cloud |
| ECMWF, *ERA5 hourly data on pressure levels from 1940 to present* ([doi:10.24381/cds.bd0915c6](https://doi.org/10.24381/cds.bd0915c6)) and on single levels (as above): temperature, geopotential and the wind's two parts at the 37 levels, and the surface pressure, the ground's geopotential, the 2 m temperature and the 10 m wind, at the hour nearest each liftoff and the six either side, between the four cells round each pad | ARCO-ERA5, as above, read by HTTP range a Blosc block at a time (tools/era5.mjs) | Each flight's column of air and surface hours (`aloft.ts`, `pnpm era5:aloft`; `DayWeather.aloft`, `.hours`) | As above: the Copernicus notice, a reanalysis, derived. Heights from geopotential by standard gravity and the 1976 standard's radius (tens of metres at 48 km); humidity left out of the density |
| JPL Horizons, DE441, the Moon from the Earth's centre at 1961-04-12 06:07 UT | as above | What the sky is at the instant: the point of the Moon under the Earth 1.513° E 0.929° N, under the Sun 139.171° W 1.146° S, the Sun 39.22° from the Moon (so the Earth is 88.7 % lit seen from it), 0.00246105 au | The reference `skyAt` is held to |
| The lens: NASA's Apollo 15, 16 and 17 press kits and mission reports (the Hasselblad 500 EL with a 500 mm Zeiss Tele-Tessar f/8, 70 mm film); for Earthrise, AS8-14-2383 (the same camera with the 250 mm Sonnar) | not read for this work | The front page's lens: 500 mm on a 55 mm frame, 6.3° (`app/front/view.ts`: LENS) | From memory: the focal length and the frame are `estimated` until a kit is read |
| The pictures the path passes: *Earthrise*, AS8-14-2383 (Apollo 8, 24 December 1968, from 110 km); Lunar Orbiter 1's frame 1102 (23 August 1966, from about 1,200 km); Kaguya's Earthrise (JAXA/NHK, November 2007, from 100 km); Zond 7's and Zond 8's Earth over the Moon's limb (1969, 1970); Chang'e 5-T1's Moon and Earth from beyond the Moon (28 October 2014); DSCOVR EPIC's far side of the Moon before the Earth (16 July 2015) | NASA, JAXA, CNSA: looked at, not used | What the camera's path is designed to pass through, and what the picture is judged against | A design's references: nothing from them is in the repository. The dates and heights are from memory |
| McEwen, "A precise lunar photometric function", *Lunar and Planetary Science* XXVII, 1996 | not read again | How the Moon's ground sends light back: the lunar-Lambert weight by phase angle, 1 - 0.019 g + 0.000242 g² - 0.00000146 g³ (`viewer/moon.ts`) | From memory: estimated. The dimming with phase (exp(-g / 60°)) is fitted by eye and is not his |

What the clouds seen from height are judged against (nothing from it is in the repository: it is looked at, not used).

| Source | Where | Used for | Licence and limits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| NASA MODIS (Aqua), Corrected Reflectance (True Colour), 9 May 2018, over Florida and the Bahamas, at 250 m | NASA Worldview Snapshots: `https://wvs.earthdata.nasa.gov/api/v1/snapshot?REQUEST=GetSnapshot&TIME=2018-05-09&BBOX=<south,west,north,east>&CRS=EPSG:4326&LAYERS=MODIS_Aqua_CorrectedReflectance_TrueColor&FORMAT=image/jpeg&WIDTH=..&HEIGHT=..` (fetched 2026-11-12) | The reference a fair-weather cumulus field is compared with from 100, 150 and 187 km straight down: streets over land, arcs and clear lanes over the sea, cell sizes, the sea's tone. Crops centred 28.6 N 78.6 W (sea), 27.6 N 81.2 W (land), 28.45 N 80.4 W (the Cape) | NASA's, not copyrighted; acknowledge NASA EOSDIS Worldview. A satellite's picture with the air's scattering taken out ("corrected reflectance"), so its sea is darker than an eye or a camera in orbit sees; a day in 2018, not 1961 |
| NASA astronaut photographs ISS041-E-003579 (Florida, 13 September 2014) and S125-E-007951 (the Bahamas, 16 May 2009) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov) | The oblique look of cloud, banks and limb from orbit | NASA's, not copyrighted |
| WMO, *International Cloud Atlas*, Altocumulus (Ac), definition: "most of the regularly arranged small elements usually have an apparent width between 1° and 5°"; "generally with shading"; "rounded masses, rolls, etc.", "may or may not be merged" | [cloudatlas.wmo.int](https://cloudatlas.wmo.int/en/altocumulus-ac.html) (read 2026-11-12) | The middle deck's cloudlets' size (weather.ts `DECK_CELL`): 60 to 300 m seen from under a deck at 3,600 m | An apparent width from the ground, turned into metres for a deck straight overhead |

Not a mosaic | CC BY-SA 4.0; looked at, not used. Days other than 5 May 1961 |

## Vostok

How each was read matters here, because much of what is online about Vostok is tertiary. **Read in
full** means the text was downloaded and read; **through a summary** means only a fetching tool's
digest of the page was seen, so no wording from it is quoted and no figure rests on it alone.

### Read

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| OKB-1, *Preliminary report on the results of the launch of the third Vostok-3A satellite-ship with a pilot on board*, 3 May 1961 (Korolev, Bushuyev, Tikhonravov; Central Archive of RKK Energia, arkh. 13029), in English translation | [Wilson Center Digital Archive, record 260547](https://digitalarchive.umd.edu/node/104537) | **The authority for Vostok 1**: liftoff 09:06:59, separation 09:18:28, the orbit (181 by 327 km, 64°57′, 89.44 min), the braking engine on and off to a tenth of a second, why the modules did not separate, the landing times and place. Read in full | A translation. Calls the booster "8K72". Its turning rate (6°/s in pitch) is far below Gagarin's 30°/s |
| Gagarin's report to the State Commission, 13 April 1961 (APRF f. 3, op. 47, d. 278), and the questions and answers of the same session | [record 260544](https://digitalarchive.umd.edu/node/104534), [the questions](https://digitalarchive.umd.edu/node/104535) | What he saw and when: the fairing at "second 150", the portholes he names, the pressures he read (150 atm of nitrogen, 320 atm at the engine), the 30°/s turning, separation at 10:35, "beyond 10g", the hatch going at about 7,000 m, the orange canopy, the Vzor's 7° field. Read in full | His clock times are from memory the day after |
| Ustinov and others to the Central Committee, 30 March 1961 | [digitalarchive.umd.edu/node/104531](https://digitalarchive.umd.edu/node/104531) | The plan as proposed: ten days of food and water. Read in full | |
| Siddiqi, *Challenge to Apollo: The Soviet Union and the Space Race, 1945–1974*, NASA SP-2000-4408 | [PDF, whole book](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/06/sp-4408.pdf) | The design (pp. 195 to 197): 2.3 m sphere, 2.43 m and 4.4 m overall, masses, three 1 m hatches, three portholes, the cabin's layout, the jets, the TDU-1, eleven antennas. The launch morning (the thirty screws), the ascent (pp. 273 to 281: T+119, 300, 676 s), the code 1-2-5, the landing claim. Launch log and table of launch vehicles (38.36 m, 287 t). Read in full, as OCR text | Written before the 1961 report was published: its account of the separation failure (cables holding the modules) is superseded. Prints "nitrous oxide" for nitric acid, calls the suit "Sokol", and gives the seat as 800 kg. Its text and its own figure disagree on the fairing's time |
| Siddiqi, "Declassified documents offer a new perspective on Yuri Gagarin's flight", The Space Review, 12 October 2015 | [thespacereview.com/article/2844/1](https://www.thespacereview.com/article/2844/1) | What the declassified documents changed: the separation, the wiring harness, the RD-0109's valve and the high orbit; СССР painted on the helmet that morning; the Granit-5V timer. Read in full | |
| «Космические корабли "Восток"», a Soviet encyclopedia text | [sovkos.ru](https://sovkos.ru/cosmicheskie-apparaty/kosmicheskie-korabli-vostok.html) | The pressure hull's 2.2 m, the hatches "1 m in the clear", three portholes of which two are in hatch covers, the instrument module's shape in words (two cones, a concave spherical end, a recess for the engine), 2.25 m, dry nitrogen, the command link's antennas on the straps. Read in full | Unattributed on the page. It reads like the entry in *Kosmonavtika: Entsiklopediya* (1985), which has not been seen: that is a guess |
| «Космические корабли "Восток"» | [bibliotekar.ru](http://www.bibliotekar.ru/index.files/2/0-40.htm) | Which hatch is where (No. 1 over the head, No. 3 under the feet, No. 2 with the parachute at the left); 14 bottles; the shield's 30 to 180 mm; parachute areas; mass shares. Read in full | Unsourced and tertiary. Gives the sphere as 2,430 mm, which is wrong. Used only where nothing better speaks, and said so |
| Great Soviet Encyclopedia, 3rd ed., «Восток» | [booksite.ru](https://booksite.ru/FullText/1/001/008/006/782.htm) | The numbered captions of its two figures (what the parts are called); 6.17 t and 7.35 m with the last stage. Read in full | The figures themselves were not retrieved. Says he landed at 10:55 |
| RKK Energia's cutaway of the 3KA, with dimensions 5040, Ø2300, Ø2500 | [as reproduced by Drew Ex Machina](https://www.drewexmachina.com/wp-content/uploads/2014/10/Vostok_3KA_diagram.jpg) | **The one dimensioned drawing seen.** Every station of the model that is not a published figure was scaled from it | Its legend (labels 1 to 15) was not on the page. A perspective drawing: the frame scales 4% large against the sphere |
| Zak, RussianSpaceWeb: Vostok 1's launch, orbit and landing; the Vostok launcher; 3KA No. 1 | [russianspaceweb.com/vostok1.html](https://www.russianspaceweb.com/vostok1.html) and its neighbours | The braking burn's shortfall (132 of 136 m/s) and the valve; ejection at 10:42; the planned landing; launcher dimensions; two OKB-1 drawings of the stack (thumbnails). The landing and launcher pages read in full, the rest through a summary | Its table of the report's orbit has slips (372 km, "64.57") against the report itself |
| Wade, Encyclopedia Astronautica: Vostok, Vostok 8K72K, S5.4, RD-0109 | [astronautix.com/v/vostok.html](http://www.astronautix.com/v/vostok.html) | The TDU-1 as "1 + 4" chambers, 1.13 m by 0.95 m; 4,725 kg for Vostok 1; Block E. Through a summary, in part in full | Disagrees with itself between pages (RD-0109's burn and impulse). Says two portholes |
| Photographs of the full-size Vostok at the Tsiolkovsky museum, Kaluga (Stolbovsky, 2022) | Wikimedia Commons: [*Vostok-1 Spacecraft 01*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Vostok-1_Spacecraft_01.jpg), [*02*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Vostok-1_Spacecraft_02.jpg) | The outside as hardware: bottle ring and its gap, support ring, straps, hairpin antennas, cable mast, the forward cone's fittings, shutters | Described on Commons as a replica. Its bottles and antennas are not all as RKK Energia's drawing has them |
| Photograph of the flown descent module at the RKK Energia museum (Errabee, 2007) | Commons: [*Vostok1 descent module*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Vostok1_descent_module.jpg) | Hatch No. 1's opening and screw ring, the socket plate, the lining | After entry: not the look in orbit |
| NASA drawing of the Vostok's seating under its fairing | Commons: [*Vostok cutaway crew seating*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Vostok_cutaway_crew_seating.jpg) | The seat across the sphere, the pilot's pose, the fairing's opening | Its NASA document was not identified |
| Photographs of the cabin's instruments, and of two museum models | Commons: [*Vostok spacecraft- cabin layout*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Vostok_spacecraft-_cabin_layout.jpg), [*Vostok (Memorial Museum of Astronautics)*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Vostok_(Memorial_Museum_of_Astronautics).JPG), [*Vostok spacecraft*](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Vostok_spacecraft.jpg) | The board with the Globus, the black left panel, the hand controller; the general arrangement with and without the third stage | The second is a 1:3 mock-up and the third a model: neither is evidence of a dimension |
| OpenStreetMap, way 172571408 «Гагаринский старт» | [openstreetmap.org/way/172571408](https://www.openstreetmap.org/way/172571408) | Site 1's position, to a few metres | Traced from imagery, not surveyed |
| GeographicLib GeoidEval; SRTM (30 m) through Open Topo Data | [geographiclib.sourceforge.io](https://geographiclib.sourceforge.io/cgi-bin/GeoidEval) | The geoid at Site 1 (EGM96: -33.73 m) and the ground's height (112 m) | The SRTM cell is on the pad's structure |
| K. Thomas, *The Russian Space Suits* (a synopsis of Abramov and Skoog), NASA JSC, 2016 | [ntrs.nasa.gov, 20160009368](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/20160009368/downloads/20160009368.pdf) | **The SK-1 at second hand**: two-layer enclosure (rubber bladder, lavsan restraint), 20 kg, 270 to 300 hPa, the helmet "rigid and irremovable", the double visor that closes by itself. Read by a research pass | Not the book. Gives the maker as "Factory 53"; Russian sources say Plant No. 918 |
| Gagarin's service characteristic of 1960, published by the Russian Ministry of Defence | [*Komsomolskaya Pravda*, 12 April 2024](https://www.kp.ru/daily/27592.5/4917923/) | **His height and mass**: "Рост 165 см, вес 68". Read | A newspaper's quotation of the document. 157 cm, widely printed, has no document behind it that was found |
| Smithsonian NASM, the SK-1 on loan (Gagarin's training suit): case label and photographs | [historicspacecraft.com](https://historicspacecraft.com/Photos/Suit/YG_SK-1_NASM2009RK_1.jpg); [airandspace.si.edu](https://airandspace.si.edu/multimedia-gallery/i19961104001ps05-s-1jpg) | The orange cover, the "gray-checked pressure liner", the sleeve mirror, leather-palmed gloves, laced boots, the inflatable collar; where the fittings are. Read as photographs | A training suit, not the flown one; museum suits differ from one another |
| Wikimedia Commons, photographs of the SK-1: the film still in the bus on 12 April 1961 and suits kept since | [`Юрий Гагарин (Первый рейс к звездам).jpg`](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Юрий_Гагарин_(Первый_рейс_к_звездам).jpg); [`Russian space suit 2.jpg`](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Russian_space_suit_2.jpg); [`SK 1 space suit (MMA 2011) (3).JPG`](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:SK_1_space_suit_(MMA_2011)_(3).JPG) | The loose cord from the collar's seam down the left of the chest (a cord, not a lacing); the plain collar with one snap on the day; the grey yoke under the cover on museum suits. Read as photographs | What the cord is, and whether the yoke is the flotation collar, are not said anywhere read |
| Memorial Museum of Cosmonautics, a teaching note; Gagarin, *Дорога в космос*; 360.ru on the helmet's lettering | [museumday.mgpu.ru](https://museumday.mgpu.ru/uploads/lesson_files/68b728ffa99ad892b8385a39b2b34876_text4teacher2.docx); [the memoir, quoted](https://burevestn1k.livejournal.com/11775.html); [360.ru](https://360.ru/news/obschestvo/fejk-nadpis-sssr-na-shleme-gagarina-nachali-stirat-na-fotografijah/) | The suit's layers and cover; the suit ventilated from the bus (no ventilator carried), the white headset cap; that photographs of the morning exist with and without "СССР" | The memoir is at second hand (a blog's quotation) |

### The cabin of 1961, and the nodes a flight is flown from

Read on 13 November 2026 for Vostok 1's cabin as flown (`missions/ussr/vostok/vehicles/vostok_cabin.py`; the flight's
spacecraft.md, "Cabin"), the seat's straps and the two engines' sizes.

| Source | Where | Used for | Limits |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Yu. A. Tyapchenko, «Системы отображения информации космических кораблей "Восток" и "Восход"» | [astronaut.ru, article 130](https://www.astronaut.ru/bookcase/article/article130.htm), with its figures 1 to 16 | **The authority for what was in the cabin**, by one of the makers (SOKB LII): the set SIS-1-3KA of December 1960 (fig. 1: board, hand controller RU-1A, control panel); the board PD-1-3KA numbered (fig. 12); the control panel PU-1-3KA with every legend (fig. 5), its raked side with the code's six keys (figs 4 and 7); the clock IVK (fig. 15); the Globus as Vostok flew it (fig. 16): no latitude and longitude drum, no orbit counter; the Vzor's ring and ruled screen; the code 125 (Gallay) or 145 (Kamanin) | Small scans. No dimension of anything. Read through a research agent's notes and the figures themselves |
| A. Zak, the flown descent module of Vostok 1 at RKK Energia's museum, through hatch No. 1, labelled | russianspaceweb.com, `images/spacecraft/manned/vostok/sa_interior.jpg` | Where the board, the Vzor, the control panel ("main switchboard"), the hand controller, the seat's guide rails and the parachute container are in the flown cabin; the sizes of the Vzor's bezel and screen, scaled | One photograph, 640 px, square to the long axis: depth along the line of sight is not in it. © Anatoly Zak: looked at, not copied into the repository |
| vostoksupersite (A. K. Collins), "Spacecraft Vostok … Control and Instrument Panels" | vostoksupersite.weebly.com | The board "approximately 40 cm wide and 38 cm tall", its face at 45°, within 1,000 mm of the pilot (quoting the installation drawing); the control panel 47 × 33 × 7.5 cm with its side raked 30°; the signal lamps' words, reconstructed; an uncaptioned period photograph over the pilot's helmet: board, camera and lamp, Vzor | An enthusiast's site, careful, citing RGANTD's «Человек. Корабль. Космос» (2011), which was not seen. Sizes are its estimates |
| The transcript of 12 April 1961 as Kommersant printed it | [kommersant.ru/doc/253987](https://www.kommersant.ru/doc/253987) | What Gagarin read off the board before launch: cabin pressure 1, humidity 65 %, 19°, the module 1.2, manual orientation 155, the TDU 320, automatic orientation 155 and 157; «В окне коррекции цифра 510» | The same record as the Izvestiya TsK KPSS text the flight's data cites, reworded, and wrong where it can be tested: it has the separation at 9:17:07 where the archive's record and the onboard tape have 9:18:07, «магнитный индекс БКРФ» for «подвижный индекс ПКРС», and 155 for the manual system's 175. Its 510 for the Globus's window is not kept: the archive's two prints (Izvestiya TsK KPSS 1991 No. 5; «Первый пилотируемый полёт», 2011, book 1, p. 440) have 710 (Vostok 1's spacecraft.md, "The correction window's figure") |
| Photographs of museum boards and seats | Commons: *Vostokpanel.JPG* (an IMP-3 board, Central Armed Forces Museum), *Vostok-3 control panel.jpg* (Polytechnic Museum), *Vostok ejector seat.jpg* (Kaluga), *Vostok kresls.JPG* (Riga) | The three gauges' scales and words; the board's proportions; the seat as a riveted tub with rollers and two nozzles at its foot (not yet built) | **Later boards than Vostok 1's** (a latitude and longitude drum, an orbit counter): used only for what Tyapchenko's photograph shows they share |
| Titov's report to the State Commission and his answers to its questions, 8 August 1961: «Первый пилотируемый полёт» (2011), book 2, docs 192 and 193 | epizodsspace.airbase.ru/bibl/pervyi-pilotiruemyi/pp_book2.pdf (Internet Archive copy of 17 November 2017), read as its text | **The later Vostoks' cabin lighting**: «Рабочим освещением я пользовался, когда записывал данные в бортжурнал. При дежурном освещении в тени Земли нельзя записывать… Приходилось переключать с дежурного на рабочее» (doc. 192); «Освещение расположено очень удобно, особенно 2-е дежурное, потому что, когда включаешь 2-ое дежурное, подсвечиваются все приборы и весь свет падает на приборную доску. Рабочее освещение хорошо расположено, оно сзади» (doc. 193) | What the lamps and the lighting tumbler in Vostoks 2 to 6 rest on ([vostok-3ka.md](../missions/ussr/vostok/vehicles/vostok-3ka.md), "Cabin lighting"): a working light and a duty one, switched by the pilot. It also says where Vostok 2's were, which the model does not follow: the working light behind him, a second duty light on the board. For Vostok 6 the one line is Tereshkova's «Освещение кабины хорошее» (Kamanin's diary for June 1963, entered below) |
| «Первый пилотируемый полёт» (2011), book 1: doc. 90, the crew's check tables of 22 March 1961 (pp. 351 to 355; ЦА РКК «Энергия», арх. 2237, л. 272–279); doc. 95, the ground command complex's organisation, no later than 30 March 1961 (pp. 373 to 376); doc. 71, the suit's specification of 14 September 1960 (pp. 295 to 296) | an OCR text of book 1, read by search, November 2026 | **What «Спуск III» was for**: table «Спуск III» (p. 353) — the times from the ground, the Globus to the landing place, the lock, manual orientation five minutes before and a coarse orientation, «Включить «Спуск III»», refine the orientation, «В заданное время или по «Глобусу» — включить ТДУ»; doc. 95's order «включить «спуск III» в 21 час 11 мин, включить ТДУ в 21 час 22 мин». The suit's emergency ventilation switched on by itself at 405 mm and by the pilot's hand | What the cycle III did inside the timer is in neither; the 11 minutes of doc. 95 are an example's. No document names the panel's «Кислород» switch |
| «Инструкция космонавту по эксплуатации и управлению космическим кораблем «Восток-3А»», January 1961 (РГАНТД, ф. 1, оп. 3-6, д. 1), as published in scans | gazeta.press, 11 April 2021, the article and its gallery; leaf 112 read as an image | Item 54, the cabin losing its pressure: «включить тумблер аварийной подачи кислорода», the suit then good for five hours | Only thirteen leaves are published; that the panel's «Кислород» is this tumbler is our reading |
| KBKhA's figures for the RD-0109, as English Wikipedia quotes them; Russian Wikipedia; Encyclopedia Astronautica | en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RD-0109; astronautix.com/r/rd-0109.html | One chamber; 1,555 mm long; **733 mm across (KBKhA by English Wikipedia) or 766 mm (Russian Wikipedia; Astronautica 0.76 m)**; area ratio 79.4 | KBKhA's own page is gone. The model takes 733 mm as the nozzle's exit |

Wanted and not found: a dimension of the Vzor and what its "7°" is the field of; the board's size from a document;
which signal lamp says what; how many cabin lamps there were and where; where the tape recorder, the food, the water
and the log were; the TDU-1's nozzle exit and what fed its steering nozzles; the seat's drawing, its mass from a
primary source, and where the parachute, the survival pack and the oxygen sat in it; the harness's colours; a
photograph of a Vostok pilot under his own canopy. Not reached: Sven Grahn's pages (the site is parked), epizodsspace
(Bobkov, *Tekhnika–Molodyozhi* 1988), the Wayback Machine.

### The weather of 12 April 1961

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| NOAA NCEI Integrated Surface Database, stations 35849099999 (Kazaly: Kazalinsk, 45.77° N 62.12° E), 35953099999 (Zhosaly: Dzhusaly, 45.50° N 64.08° E) and 38062099999 (Kzyl-Orda), 12 April 1961 | [noaa-global-hourly-pds](https://noaa-global-hourly-pds.s3.amazonaws.com/1961/35849099999.csv) (the NCEI address redirects there; fetched 2026-11-13) | **The sky either side of the range at liftoff.** Kazaly, 97 km west of Site 1, 06:00 UTC: wind 140° at 2.1 m/s, no ceiling, visibility 20 km, 12.2 °C, dew point 0.0 °C, `GF1 07,99,1,00,1,00,1,99999,9,00,1,02,1` (seven oktas in all; no cloud in the lowest layer; low cloud genus 0, middle 0, high 2: dense cirrus in patches or entangled sheaves). At 03:00: calm, seven oktas, one in the lowest layer, middle genus 4, high 7 (a veil of cirrostratus over the whole sky), a ceiling of 9,000 m by statistics. At 00:00: seven oktas, high genus 1. Zhosaly, 74 km south-east, 03:00 UTC (no report at 06:00): calm, visibility 50 km, 6.1 °C, seven oktas, high genus 2, nothing lower. Kzyl-Orda, 214 km south-east, 06:00: wind 020° at 4.1 m/s, visibility 50 km, 13.9 °C, eight oktas, high genus 7, nothing lower; clear at 00:00 | Synoptic reports of neighbouring stations, decoded by us (codes per the NCEI format document and WMO code table 0509). **None is at the range.** Kazaly's 20 km is the same at every hour and reads as its furthest mark. The distances are ours, from the stations' coordinates and site-1.ts |

### Site 1 (Tyuratam)

Read for [site-1.md](../missions/ussr/places/tyuratam/site-1.md), November 2026. **No Soviet drawing
of the pad was found.** The CIA's reports are the U-2's photographs measured by its interpreters;
their own figures are redacted wherever they would say how good the photographs were.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| CIA, HTA/JR-4/58, *Tyura Tam Missile Test Range*, September 1958 (CIA-RDP02T06408R000900010026-3) | CIA reading room, through archive.org's mirror | Launch Area "A" from the photographs of August 1957: the pit (880 by 550 ft, "oriented along a west-east axis", two terraces, gunite on the west, the road down from the east), the two 110 ft masts at the north-east and south-west corners with an array "at least 5 feet wide and 10 feet high", the 10 ft lip, the 60 ft tower 400 ft west, the five rail spurs, the spoil 800 ft north-east, nine observation positions round the rim, the double security fence 160 ft apart with light poles at 120 ft | Its idea of the structure (a platform "at least 50 feet" thick) was revised by the later reports. Read as OCR text |
| CIA, PIC/JR-2/60, *Tyura Tam*, February 1960 (CIA-RDP02T06408R000900010035-3) | the same | Revised measurements: the platform 135 ft square, the 65 ft dark circle, pylons 30 ft wide, the abutment's 25 ft, the cable conduit 80 ft above the pit base and 60 ft below the platform (so about 140 ft deep), the control bunker 145 by 110 ft and its north-west entrance, the three-bay storage bunker (bays 35 ft square, 35 ft of earth between), the propellant shed 105 ft long, open to the north on six supports, the cascade facility 55 by 40 ft, a support building 70 by 35 ft, ten rail cars | Many figures redacted. Read as OCR text |
| CIA, PIC/JR-2/61, *Launch Area A: a reanalysis of the launching structure*, March 1961 (CIA-RDP78T04751A000100010002-2), with its Figure 1 (plan and front elevation, dimensioned) and Figure 2 (perspective) | the same | The pylons 10 ft thick, 30 and 55 ft, 115 ft over the pair; the fence 15 ft in on three sides; the "servicing tower" 60 ft high on eight struts (the launch system's four supports, seen from above); the "possible umbilical mast" 85 to 95 ft; the 110 ft masts' function "cannot be determined"; the terraces, retaining walls, pit base and 60 ft trenches in elevation. Read as page images and OCR | Figure 1 is a diagram, not to scale; its heights are from shadows |
| U-2 vertical photograph of Tyuratam, 5 August 1957 | Wikimedia Commons | The platform, pit, tracks and spoil as they were | Low resolution in the copy seen |

| Zak, RussianSpaceWeb: Site 1 and the launch of 12 April 1961 | russianspaceweb.com: its Site 1 pages, and [vostok1_launch.html](https://www.russianspaceweb.com/vostok1_launch.html) | "40 by 40 m"; the pit; film stills of the morning. Read again for the pad's service hardware: the lift "went up along the arm of the transporter erector"; "around a dozen steps"; stills of the stair, the lift, the hatch being closed, the tank cars on the deck | Its stills are 200 pixels wide |
| «Звёздный рейс Юрия Гагарина (Документы о первом полёте человека в космос)», *Izvestiya TsK KPSS*, 1991 No. 5: the record of the exchanges between Gagarin and the control points (sent to the Central Committee by Vershinin on 25 April 1961) and his report to the State Commission, in Russian | epizodsspace.airbase.ru/bibl/i_tsk/zv-reis.html, through the Internet Archive's copy | **The morning to the minute**: 08:08 the rail cars leave, 08:15 «опускают площадки обслуживания. На фермах работы все окончены», 08:35 «сейчас будут отводить установщик», 08:37 «стрела установщика отошла нормально», 08:40 «сейчас отводим фермы», 09:06 «отошла кабель-мачта». His report: «слышно, как разводили фермы ... ракета как бы немножко покачивалась». Read in full | Minutes, not seconds. The times are as the record's compilers set them down |

Read in full | A memoir; which of his books the page carries is not stated on it |
| Afanasyev and Lavrenov, *Большой космический клуб*, the chapter «"Семёрка": самая знаменитая ракета в мире», with two drawings from KBOM's archive (the R-7's launch position; its launch system, numbered) and four frames of the film of 15 May 1957 | [astronaut.ru](https://astronaut.ru/bookcase/books/afanasiev3/text/05.htm) | That the launch system had "two lower and one upper cable masts"; the turning ring 18 m across "at the mark -2 m"; the service trusses standing closed round an R-7 with ring platforms, and one being lowered with its half rings on its head («отходят фермы обслуживания»). Read in full; the drawings as images some 420 pixels wide | The drawings are of the 1957 system for the two-stage R-7, small, and their captions' numbers cannot all be matched to the lines |

Read in full | A compilation: its sources are linked, not quoted by page, and were not followed. No dimension of the erector but its mass |
| CIA, PIC/JR-2/61 (entry above), read again for what stood on the platform | | The "servicing tower" of 1959 to 1960 without a rocket: 60 ft to a collar at its top, "two parallel inner sides" north and south with the way open east and west, platforms hinged up 45°; the "possible umbilical mast", 85 to 95 ft, ending in an oblong loop; and the interpreters' own guess that "the boom on the rail car could support servicing platforms for work at levels higher than the top of the servicing tower" | Heights from shadows, "only approximate" |
