| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Jacchia, L. G., *Revised Static Models of the Thermosphere and Exosphere with Empirical Temperature Profiles*, Smithsonian Astrophysical Observatory Special Report 332, 5 May 1971 (J71; CIRA 1972) | [NTRS 19710024820](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19710024820/downloads/19710024820.pdf) | Eq. 14 (Tc = 379 + 3.24 F-bar + 1.3 (F - F-bar), the flux of the day before), eqs. 15 to 17 and their constants (the day's bulge), eq. 18 (28 Kp + 0.03 e^Kp, Kp 6.7 h before), eqs. 21 to 23 (the semiannual term), Table 6 (each 100 K's profile; 1000 to 1400 K on the scan's pp. 88 to 96) and Table 7 (log density in 50 K columns, p. 108); section 15's worked example (F-bar 155, F 136: Tc 856.5 K; semiannual -0.038 at 350 km) | **The scan has lost Table 7's pp. 109 and 110** (1000 to 1450 K below 400 km): Table 6 is read there. Its p. 90 (1100 K) prints the density column a row low. Prefers eq. 20 below 200 km and a smoothed Kp: neither is done here |
| GFZ Potsdam, *Kp_ap_Ap_SN_F107_since_1932.txt* (Matzka, Stolle, Yamazaki, Bronkalla and Morschhauser, "The geomagnetic Kp index and derived indices of geomagnetic activity", *Space Weather* 19, 2021; doi:10.5880/Kp.0001) | [kp.gfz-potsdam.de](https://kp.gfz-potsdam.de/app/files/Kp_ap_Ap_SN_F107_since_1932.txt) | The days flown's eight three-hourly Kp (definitive), Ap, and the observed and adjusted 10.7 cm flux; the 81-day means worked from it | The flux's gaps (1962-06-29 to 07-03 among them) are -1: left out of the means. Checked against CelesTrak's SW-All.csv (the same) and NOAA's DRAO listing (1961-04-12, 89.1) |
| Tapping, K. F., "The 10.7 cm solar radio flux (F10.7)", *Space Weather* 11, 394-406, 2013, doi:10.1002/swe.20064 | the paper | That the Ottawa series of 1947 to 1991 is the measured one, not a reconstruction | |
| Jacchia, L. G., SAO Special Report 375, 1977 (J77) | [NTRS 19770016704](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19770016704/downloads/19770016704.pdf) | Its Table 4 (the smoothed flux by ten days, 1958 to 1976) as a check on the 81-day means: 106.3 for 12 April 1961 and 83.5 for 14 June 1963 | Per-species variations; not carried |

### Fires by what they burn, and where the exhaust goes at Site 1

Read for [viewer/flames.ts](../src/viewer/flames.ts) and trail.ts `pitBlast` (rendering.md, "A fire looks as what it burns").

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Huzel and Huang, *Design of Liquid Propellant Rocket Engines*, NASA SP-125 (1967), tables 1-7 to 1-9, pp. 25-27 | [rrs.org copy](https://rrs.org/wp-content/uploads/2014/01/Huzel-Huang-sp125.pdf) | Chamber flame temperatures at 1,000 psia: oxygen and RP-1 at 2.56, 6,150 °F (3,672 K); oxygen and 95 % alcohol at 1.73, 5,640 °F; nitrogen tetroxide and Aerozine 50 at 2.00, 5,590 °F; IRFNA and UDMH, 3,222 K | Not the engines' own mixtures or pressures: the A-7's (75 % alcohol), the RD-107's and the braking engine's are equilibrium calculations of ours (Cantera, NASA thermodynamic data), checked against these cases to 6 to 20 K, and are `derived` |
| J. D. Clark, *Ignition!* (1972; Rutgers 2017 ed.), p. 96, as quoted at sindark.com (2018) | the quotation only was read | That kerosene burns luminous with free carbon and that the hydrazines and nitrogen tetroxide burn nearly invisible, the shock diamonds showing | Read at second hand |
| NASA film *Gemini: The Twins* (1965), Gemini 3's launch; Periscope Film stock listing xd17414 | [listing](https://stock.periscopefilm.com/xd17414-gemini-the-twins-1965-nasa-film-preparation-and-achievements-of-gemini-3-gemini-4) | The Titan II's flame: peach, seen through | The listing and stills only were seen, not the film frame by frame |
| Photographs of the launch of 12 April 1961 from across the pit (RussianSpaceWeb, Vostok 1); the newsreel *Pervyi reis k zvezdam* (1961) | RussianSpaceWeb's Vostok 1 pages | The R-7's five fires white at the nozzles and opaque, the cloud out of the pit | That the newsreel's launch footage is Vostok 1's, or in colour, is not shown. No colour film of Vostok 1 or of Freedom 7 was found by title and archive: the colour look is judged also on colour film of later launches from the same pads |
| Soyuz CSG User's Manual, Issue 2 (Arianespace, March 2012), §A5.1.2 | [copy](https://courses.edx.org/asset-v1:DelftX+AEASM1x+1T2023+type@asset+block/Soyuz-Users-Manual-March-2012.pdf) | That the RD-107's turbine runs on steam from hydrogen peroxide (so no dark, sooty turbine exhaust) | Where that steam is vented is not said: it is not drawn |

### The strap-ons' separation

Read for orbital.ts's four strap-ons and sim/shed.ts `apart`. The sequence is documented in kind; no figure for the
tail-off, the swing, the vent or the timing between them was found.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Siddiqi, *Challenge to Apollo*, NASA SP-2000-4408, p. ~130 (the R-7's design) | [history.nasa.gov](https://history.nasa.gov/SP-4408pt1.pdf) | The strap-ons "still firing, albeit at much lower thrust", rotating away from the base until "at a certain angle" the apex mountings release; the tension bands loosened by pyrotechnics; a gentle push to move them away | No figures; the R-7 of 1957, not the 8K72K of 1961 |
| Chertok, *Rockets and People*, vol. 2, NASA SP-2006-4110, "Seven Problems of the R-7", pp. 294-295 | [nasa.gov](https://www.nasa.gov/pdf/635963main_RocketsPeopleVolume2-ebook.pdf) | The core carried on the strap-ons' spherical heads: the thrust passes there | |
| Soyuz CSG User's Manual, issue 2 (Arianespace, 2012), §A5.1.2 and Fig. 1.5.1a, §2.3.1 | as above | Ball joint at the cone's top and two rear struts; pyronuts, pushers and a reaction nozzle; Soyuz-2's intermediate thrust at 112 s, separation at 118.1 s | Soyuz-2, not the 8K72K |
| Roscosmos's MS-10 commission findings (November 2018) as RussianSpaceWeb (A. Zak, Soyuz MS-10) and Habr 428530 give them; *Modelist-Konstruktor*, "Raketa, podnyavshaya v kosmos Yu. Gagarina" | web pages | The sensor on the ball freed as it leaves its socket opens the oxidizer tank's pull-away nozzle, on the cone's inner side, its jet at 45° to the axis and forward; pneumatic locks on the lower ties (Modelist-Konstruktor) where Siddiqi has pyrotechnics | Secondary; the vent's thrust and duration are in none of them |
| Interfax on Progress MS-06's strap-ons (2017), about 50 km west of Zhezkazgan | press | The 51.6-degree drop zone: about 350 km from Site 1 (derived, great circle) | Not Vostok's azimuth: its zone was further north-east; RussianSpaceWeb's downrange page names the zones without distances |

### Known, not yet read

| Source | Why it matters |
| --- | --- |
| The range's own weather report for the morning of 12 April 1961 (the State Commission's records; Kamanin's diary; Gagarin's report after the flight), and the launch film looked at for the sky itself | Whether the high cloud the neighbouring stations report stood over the pad: the site's sky is drawn from them, against an earlier reading of the film as clear |
| The scan of Minakov's article in *Modelist-Konstruktor* with its Figure 3; Alway, *Rockets of the World*; Varfolomeyev, "Soviet Rocketry that Conquered Space", *Spaceflight*, 1995–2001 | A dimensioned general arrangement of the 8K72K: every station of the launcher model is estimated for want of one |
| NPO Energomash's own page on the RD-107 and RD-108; KBKhA's on the RD-0109 (neither answered) | Engine figures at first hand: nozzle exit diameters, chamber spacing, burn times |
| The R-7's draft project and technical description (in Vetrov, *S. P. Korolev i ego delo*, and *Tvorcheskoe nasledie akademika S. P. Koroleva*) | Tank stations, the pitch programme, the launch azimuths |
| CIA and DIA assessments of the SS-6 and SL-3 after Paris, 1967 | Measured dimensions of a real vehicle |
| Period colour photographs of 8K72K vehicles in the assembly building, 1960–1961 | The paint, and whether anything was written on it |
| Glushko (ed.), *Kosmonavtika: Entsiklopediya*, Moscow, 1985, pp. 65–66 | The numbered drawing of the Vostok, and the text sovkos.ru appears to carry |
| Semenov (ed.), *Raketno-kosmicheskaya korporatsiya "Energiya" imeni S. P. Koroleva, 1946–1996*: its figures (the text is read: see "The launch vehicle") | The legend to the dimensioned cutaway of the 3KA |
| Raushenbakh (ed.), *Materialy po istorii kosmicheskogo korablya "Vostok"*, Nauka, 1991 | The design documents themselves |
| *Pervyi pilotiruemyi polet* (two volumes of documents, 2011); Uspenskaya (ed.), *Chelovek. Korabl'. Kosmos*, 2011; the report of 9 May 1961 (RGAE f. 298, op. 1, d. 2057) | The Russian originals of the reports read here in translation, and the valve explanation of the braking burn |
| Bobkov, «Космический корабль "Восток"», 1991; «Восток, Восход и другие», *Tekhnika–Molodyozhi* 1988 No. 4 (epizodsspace) | Bottle count, jets, the cabin |
| Abramov and Skoog, *Russian Spacesuits*, Springer-Praxis, 2003; NPP Zvezda's own history pages | The standard reference for the SK-1, and its maker: dimensions, the cover's cloth, the fittings, who painted the helmet. Neither could be reached; what the SK-1's notes give as theirs is at second hand |
| Sven Grahn's pages on Vostok's radio systems | Which antenna is which, and the radio timeline. The site did not answer |
| Petrik and Piunov, «ТДУ-1 А. М. Исаева», *Dvigatel*, 2011 No. 1 | The braking engine's insides: tank, steering nozzles, turbopump |
| Abramov and Skoog, *Russian Spacesuits*, 2003 | The SK-1: needed before a crew model is built |
| Hall and Shayler, *The Rocket Men*, 2001; Pillet, "Le vaisseau Vostok" (kosmonavtika.com) | Hardware detail in English and French |
| NSSDC record 1961-012A; the FAI's record file for the flight | Where 4,725 kg comes from |
| A survey of Site 1 as it stood in April 1961, and the launch azimuth as flown | The pad model and the trajectory |
| Lissov's articles on the flight in *Novosti Kosmonavtiki*; *Pervyi pilotiruemyi polet* (2011), the tracking and ballistics documents | The node, the argument of perigee, where and when the spacecraft was injected, the drop zones: none is in anything read, and the coast's landing along the track hangs on them |
| Anything on the Vostok's solar orientation system that gives the sensor's axis against the braking engine's (Raushenbakh; Chertok vol. 3; Legostaev) | The record says the engine was pointed at the Sun; the Sun was 41° out of the orbit's plane, and the landing says the push was not |
| King-Hele, D. G. and Quinn, E., "Air density at heights of 150-300 km in the years 1962-1964", *Planet. Space Sci.* 13, 693 (1965), doi:10.1016/0032-0633(65)90047-4; King-Hele, *Nature* 203, 959 (1964) | The air of Vostoks 3 to 6's days measured from satellites' decay: the check on world/thermosphere.ts the indices cannot give. Paywalled |
| Cook, G. E., "Satellite drag coefficients", *Planet. Space Sci.* 13(10), 929-946, 1965, doi:10.1016/0032-0633(65)90150-9 | The source of the 2.2 a satellite's drag is flown on (through King-Hele, 1987): its sphere and cylinder figures and the 2.0 to 2.4 range. ScienceDirect, DTIC (AD0464391) and ADS refused it in November 2026 |
| Bailey and Hiatt, "Sphere drag coefficients for a broad range of Mach and Reynolds numbers", *AIAA Journal* 10 (1972); Hoerner, *Fluid-Dynamic Drag* | The sphere's drag curve in `orbital.ts` is set down from memory of them |
| Vallado, *Fundamentals of Astrodynamics and Applications*, 4th ed., ch. 9 (mean elements, J2's secular rates) and its worked examples | A textbook's worked case of osculating to mean elements, to hold `src/world/meanelements.ts` to beside Kozai's own forms; not to hand on 16 November 2026 |

## Voskhod

Read for the [Voskhod programme](../missions/ussr/voskhod/README.md), 13 November 2026. Each
page's text was downloaded and read unless it says otherwise. Entries above under "Vostok" (Siddiqi,
Chertok, Kamanin, McDowell, Minakov, Brügge) are not repeated in full: what is given here is what
each was read for this time.

### The hardware

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| OKB-1, general drawing «ЗКВ 0000-0» ("Изделие"), scale 1:10, signed 17 April 1964; and the same for the 3KD, April 1965 (RGANTD) | as reproduced by Pillet, kosmonavtika.com, "Voskhod: Généralités" (1,123 and 1,440 pixels wide), and by Zak (730 pixels) | **The only drawings of the ship read, and the model's authority for what no text gives**: the reserve engine's stepped outline on the forward pole (1.27 to 1.33 m above the sphere), the hairpin antennas beside it, the hatches a quarter turn apart, the Vostok's instrument module unchanged, the fairing's cylinder and cone (21.5 to 23° from the axis; inside 2.59 to 2.64 m), the folded airlock on the 3KD reaching 1.66 m from the axis | Read at 6.5 and 7.4 mm to a pixel: nothing on them is good to better than a few centimetres. Their parts lists cannot be read. **The 3KV's drawing shows two nozzle-like bodies lying forward along the reserve engine which no text explains.** The whole ship scales to 5.43 to 5.49 m, where the figure in print is "five meters" |
| Siddiqi, *Challenge to Apollo* (entry under "Vostok"), pp. 409 to 412 and 448 to 451, and Table VI, p. 921 | the same PDF, read as text extracted from it; p. 449 as a page image | **The design, from Korolev's draft plans as published in 1980**: what was removed and installed; "a length of five meters and maximum diameter of 2.43 meters"; 2,900 and 5,320 kg; the reserve engine (143 kg, 87 kg of propellant, 12,000 kgf, about two seconds); the landing system (two main parachutes, a 1.2 m probe, 8 to 10 m/s to 0.2); the couches ("side by side in a triangular shape, with the middle one raised forward"; the board and the Vzor "to the left"; 200 to 300 mm of travel); the leak of 180 litres a minute; no escape for "the first twenty-five to forty-four seconds"; the 3KD's changes ("The Volga airlock on hatch no. 3"), the airlock's figures (0.7 and 2.5 m, 1.2 and 1.0 m, a 0.65 m hatchway, 250 kg, forty partitions in three sections, four tanks, four cameras), the Berkut's; the 11A57 as "43.5–45.22" m and 413.3 tf; the RD-0108's 30.4 tf | "Five meters" is round, and the drawings say 5.5. Contradicts itself on the RD-0108's thrust (30.4 on p. 410, 30.0 on p. 469). Woods's drawing on p. 449 is a reconstruction. The launch vehicle's mass is illegible in the copy read |
| Zak, RussianSpaceWeb: "Designing Voskhod", "Voskhod launch vehicle (11A57)", "Design of Voskhod-2", "Third stage of the Soyuz rocket", "RD-0110" | [russianspaceweb.com/voskhod-design.html](https://www.russianspaceweb.com/voskhod-design.html) and its neighbours | "Three seats were rotated 90 degrees around the vertical axis of the spacecraft"; the moved console and "joy stick"; the television camera "on the exterior of the service module"; **the 11A57's "payload assembly ... 16,665 millimeters"**; Block I as it flies today (6.745 m, 2.66 m, tanks, the aft section in three pieces, five 500 mm bottles); the airlock's 740 mm, its two 700 mm hatches opening inward, the Vzor "built into the new hatch"; **his photographs**: the flown Voskhod 1 cabin from its hatch, a 3KD cabin "equivalent" with its couches, test articles of the 3KV, the ship in assembly | Secondary, and its pictures are 200 to 730 pixels wide. What 16,665 mm spans is not stated |
| Pillet, kosmonavtika.com, "Voskhod" (Généralités; Le sas Volga) | [kosmonavtika.com](https://www.kosmonavtika.com) | The two RGANTD drawings at their best resolution; the centre couch "avancée"; the landing sequence (drogue at 5 km, mains at 3.1 km, the PR-30 motors); the airlock at 73 cm folded, 119 and 97.5 cm across | Secondary, in French. Gives the reserve engine's two seconds and twelve tonnes to the main engine |
| Brügge, b14643.eu, table for the 11A57 (entry under "The launch vehicle (8K72K)") | b14643.eu | 44.13 m; Block I "+adapter" 9.05 m by 2.66; fairing 6.74 by 2.58; the engines' thrusts at the ground (3,276.4 kN for four strap-ons, 777.8 kN) | Uncited figure by figure. Names the third stage's engine RD-0110. Its fairing figures are its Vostok's |
| Abramov and Skoog, *Russian Spacesuits* (entry under "Vostok 5 and Vostok 6"), pp. 60 to 74 | Google Books, search-inside snippets joined end to end | The airlock by its makers: "an upper rigid portion with an EVA hatch and a lower assembly ring fixed to the spacecraft flange"; "longitudinal air beams in the form of inflatable rubber cylinders with a cover made of a high-strength fabric"; seven sets made; the beams at 400 to 800 hPa; the Berkut's layers, helmet, pressures, backpack KP-55 and umbilical | **Read as snippets, never as a page.** No dimension came through |
| Altunin, Verba and others, «60 лет внекорабельной деятельности», *Пилотируемые полёты в космос*, 2(55), 2025 | gctc.ru | The airlock's 36 inflatable sections in three groups; the inner hatch's cover taking "up to 30 %" of the cabin | Gives 36 where Siddiqi has 40 |
| McDowell, GCAT launch log (entry under "Vostok 3 and Vostok 4") | planet4589.org | **The launch vehicles' numbers**: R15000-02 (Kosmos 47), -04 (Voskhod), -03 (Kosmos 57, from Site 31), -05 (Voskhod 2) | Tertiary |
| Photographs of the Volga airlock and the Berkut suit at the Memorial Museum of Cosmonautics, Moscow, at Zvezda's museum and elsewhere | Wikimedia Commons (Armael; Kucharek; Laika ac; and others); Google Arts & Culture | The airlock's tan cover in loose folds, its ribbed aluminium upper ring, the four spheres at its base; the suit's white cover, the helmet lettered СССР, the grey harness, the pack | Museum articles: which are flown, which are copies, and what has been repainted is mostly not stated |

### The cabins

Read on 14 November 2026 for the re-laying of both cabins round their seated crews
([voskhod.md](../missions/ussr/voskhod/vehicles/voskhod.md), "What the record fixes in the cabin").

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| OKB-1 (S. P. Korolev), prospectus «Многоместный космический корабль "Восход"», 12 February 1964 | Roscosmos's release for the flight's fiftieth year (roscosmos.ru/26922, read through the Wayback Machine): the scanned typescript, 11 pages | **Фиг. 1, OKB-1's own figure of the three couches: abreast, heads toward the parachute container, the middle one half a trunk toward the feet, the outer two toed in, a harness of four straps to a round buckle**; "three shock-absorbed non-ejecting couches" (p. 4); what came out of a Vostok and what went in (p. 3); **the couches' stroke, "200÷300 mm at the cosmonauts' shoulders"** (p. 5), which is where Siddiqi's figure comes from; the parachute hatch's cover shot off at 5,000 m; Фиг. 3, the container as a crescent against the wall | A proposal five months before the ship was built: the figure is a painting without a dimension, and its Фиг. 3 is titled for the 3KA |
| State Commission, transcript of the crew's report, 22 March 1965 (Roscosmos's release of 2015: `f1_o13-1_d36.pdf`) | as "Voskhod 2", below; read off the page images for these passages | Belyayev: **«Я перевернулся поперек корабля, поперек кресел, т.е. вдоль от "Взора"»** and Leonov «залез почти ко "Взору" и сел вне кресла вниз туда» (typescript pp. 7 to 8): the couches lie across the line to the Vzor; Leonov on the inner hatch: «кулачки отошли, крышка пошла», the lid «лег на свою плиту» (pp. 14 to 15); the cover he took off the Vzor and the pack that caught on it (pp. 14, 16); the camera that might have broken the Vzor (p. 20); the hand wheel (p. 3); the couches fired up under the parachutes (pp. 9, 11) | A stenographer's typescript. It gives no dimension of anything in the cabin |
| Belyayev and Leonov, report on the flight, April 1965 (`1965_otchet.pdf`); OKB-1, technical report, 6 June 1965 (`1965-06-06_teh-otchet.pdf`) | as "Voskhod 2", below. Four pages of the crew's report are missing from the release and were read in mirror image through the paper | Who boarded first (Leonov); Belyayev at hatch No. 1 (he unlocked «люка № 1» and shouldered it); the couches raised after the main parachutes opened, short of their full travel; the monitor's frame that trapped Leonov's right leg; the three cine cameras' brackets beside the inner hatch; "the layout of the equipment does not let the commander control the systems from his own place" | **The technical report has no drawing of the cabin**: its five figures are tables and plots of telemetry. Words read through the paper are sure, single letters are not |
| Feoktistov, K. P., «Траектория жизни» (Moscow: Вагриус, 2000), the chapters on Voskhod | epizodsspace.airbase.ru's text, without page numbers | The man who laid the cabin out, and flew in it: «Ближе к люку находился Володя, он вылез первым, затем Борис и последним я»; he gave Yegorov his middle couch during the flight, by the porthole being cold; "we sat tight in our moulded liners"; hanging head down in the straps after landing | A memoir. Nothing in it on where a couch stood, the angle it was turned through or the container |
| Rabinovich, B. A., a monograph on the safety of a crew at landing impact (the PDF read is dated 2017), sections 2.2, 5.5.2.3 and 6.5 | raapa.ru, 283 pages; figures 2.8 and 6.9 | **The only pictures found of an Elbrus couch out of its ship**: a boat-shaped riveted shell with a dimpled side beam and a head rest, the man's thighs upright and his shins on a raised rest; that it hung on two shock absorbers, at the head and at the legs, tied by an equalising linkage; **stroke 240 mm** | The couch is on a test base, not in a cabin; no dimension of the shell |
| Pillet, N., kosmonavtika.com, "Visite" pages for Voskhod 1, Voskhod 2, Kosmos 110 and the Zvezda trainer | kosmonavtika.com/vaisseaux/voskhod/visite/ (French); photographs at full size | **The flown Voskhod 1 from inside, at RKK Energia's museum** (his fig. 6): the couches' riveted side beam with round stampings, the domed head end, the leg rests, a plate «Изд. ЭЛЬБРУС … VII-64», beams painted red under the couches, the Vzor lit, **the pilot's panel across the cabin just inside hatch No. 1** with its guarded switches (ЗАПУСК ТДУ, СПУСК III, РУЧНАЯ ОРИЕНТАЦИЯ), the hand controller in a recess at its end, the television camera and monitor; which ship is where: both flown balls are at RKK Energia, Korolyov, the first lent to the Science Museum in London in 2015 and 2016 | The flown Voskhod 2 was not photographed inside. **The "Voskhod 2 equivalent" at Zvezda, Tomilino, which every published view inside a 3KD is of, is a ground trainer** (Zvezda's own caption) and its parachute container a painted mock |
| NPP Zvezda and the Science Museum, on Google Arts & Culture | artsandculture.google.com: "Spaceship model Voskhod-2 with the lock chambers Volga" (3,817 by 5,726 pixels) and "Voskhod 1 descent module" (object L2014-4270) | The trainer square on through hatch No. 1: two couches, the airlock's opening on the far wall, the board over it; the flown Voskhod 1 through the same hatch | As above: a trainer. Lengths read off either are good to a quarter |
| RKK Energia, a frame of a film (the 3KV and the 3KD side by side, couches end on); OKB-1, sections of the airlock as proposed on hatch No. 1 and as chosen on hatch No. 3 | Zak, RussianSpaceWeb, "Design of Voskhod-2" (images) | The 3KD's empty place is the one by hatch No. 3; the airlock out at about 22° to the cross plane; the folded ring square to its hatch at 20° | Small reproductions |

### Voskhod 1

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Kamanin, *Skrytyi kosmos*, the diary for 1964, April to 19 October | [militera.lib.ru/db/kamanin_np/64.html](http://militera.lib.ru/db/kamanin_np/64.html) | **The fullest record of the days.** The limits on passengers (3 April, 20 June); the drop test lost (7 September); escape "in fact impossible" to the 27th second (14 September); Kosmos 47 (6 and 7 October: 120, 150, 290 and 523 s; the furrow of 90 mm; the third stage's sag); 9 October at the hatch: «Размещение космонавтов очень тесное»; **12 October**: «Тихое морозное утро, температура — минус 8 градусов ... ветер — 3–4 метра в секунду, видимость — более 20 километров, высокие перистые облака», the bus at 10:15, the grey wool suits and white caps, the order aboard, «Точно в 12:30 по местному времени (в 10:30 по московскому)», «Томительные 523 секунды»; the pulse of 46; 15 to 21 °C; **13 October**: the descent as it was heard, 10:22:30, Mikhaylov's Il-14, «с двумя куполами парашютов» | He keeps the range's clock at the pad (Moscow + 2 h), and says so. Edited for print |
| Siddiqi, *Challenge to Apollo*, pp. 413 to 426, the launch log and Table IB | the same PDF | "3KV no. 3"; "exactly 1030 hours, 1 second Moscow Time"; T+523 s; 177.5 by 408 km at 64.9°; the clothes; the experiments; the request to stay; "312 kilometers northeast of the town of Kustanay"; "one day, seventeen minutes, and three seconds" | Its Table IB prints 64.77° and is jumbled in the copy read. "The first minute or so" without escape against its own 25 to 44 seconds |
| TASS, 12 to 21 October 1964, as printed in *Заря Востока* (Tbilisi), Nos. 241 to 250 | dspace.nplg.gov.ge, scans with a poor text layer; the communiqué of the 12th read off the page image | The announced orbit (178 by 409 km, 90.1 min, "about 65 degrees"); the frequencies; the talk on the third orbit; the bulletins by orbit; 04:28; "10 hours 47 minutes"; Romanov's report from the pad (the suits "of a dark colour", the blue jackets); the Hamlet line as printed; the food | Some passages were read from the text layer and not checked letter by letter against the image |

Space: Airlock of Voskhod-2", *Spaceflight* 36 (August 1994); *Aviation Week* and *Flight* for
October 1964; Pravda and Izvestia themselves.

Its Table 1 carries an unexplained "(25 h 53 min)" beside the duration. Says nothing of a hatch that failed to seal |
| The crew's report (dated 23 April 1965 by Roscosmos), and the extract from the transcript of the State Commission's session of 22 March 1965: Belyayev's report and Leonov's | the same publication | **The two men's own words within days**: the ascent's times (118, 153, 292, 523, 527 s); who boarded first; the airlock's pressures; «Подожди Леша»; what Leonov saw; the cap thrown away; the F-21's release torn off; **«Я доложил, что перехожу на второй режим»**, the pressure let down by plan, **«обе ноги сразу вставил ... кинокамеру»**; the sound of the airlock going; 355 mm of oxygen; 140 to 25 atm; a turn in 20 to 22 s; Belyayev across the couches at the Vzor with his helmet off; «Нажимаю кнопку запуска ТДУ» with both out of their couches; about 39 s; about 7 g; the birch, the snow, the three hours | Scans, with show-through; the morning's times in the crew's report are faint. The transcript is an extract and jumps pages |

**Not reached, or not read**: *Two Sides of the Moon* itself; TASS and Pravda of March 1965 in the
original; Popov's survey of the landing place (2015); the remarks of
8 July 1965 in Roscosmos's set (skimmed); *Flight* and *Aviation Week* for March 1965; the film of the walk,
of which no frame-by-frame account was found.

## Project Gemini

### Gemini-Titan 3

What spacecraft No. 3, GLV-3 and the flight rest on (`missions/usa/gemini/`). The rows added for
the second modelling pass (November 2026) say what that pass's research took from each source. The
flight's record (its times, orbit, landing and weather) was read again on the page or in the text
when it was written into the repo; the hardware rows are as the research reported them, with the
figures the model uses spot-checked against the manuals' text.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Project Gemini Familiarization Manual*, SEDR 300, volume 1, "Long Range and Modified Configurations" (McDonnell, 30 September 1965) | [ibiblio.org, the Virtual AGC project](https://www.ibiblio.org/apollo/Documents/GeminiManualVol1.pdf) (all but Section VIII) | **The authority for the spacecraft's shape**: Figure 2-3, the dimensions (p. 2-5), and Figure 2-4, the Z stations (p. 2-6); the sections and their materials (pp. 2-3 to 2-25); **Figure 2-7, the access doors of spacecraft 3, two sheets (pp. 2-11 and 2-12)**; the hatches' twelve latches, hinges and handles (Figure 2-8, p. 2-17; pp. 2-18 and 2-19), the windows' three panes of Vycor (p. 2-18) and their section (Figure 2-10, p. 2-20); the skins' materials (p. 2-21) and the equipment section (p. 2-23); the cabin and the ejection seat (Figures 3-1 and 3-2; the seats' cant, p. 3-3; the seat, backboard and egress kit, pp. 3-5 and 3-12 to 3-19); **the panels of spacecraft 3, Figure 3-5 sheets 1 and 2, captioned "(S/C 3)" (pp. 3-21 and 3-22)**; stowage, lighting and the static ports (pp. 3-20 and 3-26 to 3-31); the fairings' jettison (p. 4-12), and **when: "The Command Pilot presses the switch after SECO on S/C 3 and 4, and after staging on S/C 7" (p. 4-53)**; **the batteries, spacecraft 3's among them (pp. 5-3 and 5-16)**; the scanner fairing's release (Figures 11-12 and 11-13, pp. 11-28 and 11-29); the retrograde rockets on their beams (Figure 11-32, p. 11-70; p. 11-71); the parachutes (pp. 12-12 and 12-14). It covers spacecraft 3, 4 and 7 and says which | Section VIII (guidance and control, which has the thrusters) is a separate file: the next row. The figures' dimensions are not in the PDF's text layer: read them off the page. Figures 3-1, 3-2 and 3-5 have no dimensions, and the stowage is drawn only for spacecraft 7 (Figure 3-7). Figure 2-10 marks Z136.50 at one end of the window's section without saying which, and gives no angle for the pane. The pressure vessel is described (pp. 2-8 and 2-10; Figure 2-6) and nowhere dimensioned. A printed page is not the PDF's page: the sections are numbered separately |
| The same manual's Section VIII, "Guidance and Control System", which has the propulsion: the confidential supplement of the same date, since released | ibiblio.org, the Virtual AGC project: *GeminiManualVol1Sec2.pdf* | **The thrusters.** The OAMS: Figure 8-72 (p. 8-256), every chamber by number, place and direction, with "TCA 13, 14, 15, & 16 INOPERATIVE ON S/C #3", and "Spacecraft 3 does not have the capability to translate up, down, left or right" (p. 8-258); the chambers of 25, 85 and 100 lb (pp. 8-258 and 8-260; Figures 8-81 to 8-83, pp. 8-276 to 8-278); the tanks (helium 16.20 in outside diameter, fuel 21.13 in, oxidiser 21.12 in: pp. 8-263, 8-271 and 8-273). **The re-entry control system: Figure 8-84 (p. 8-281), sixteen chambers firing along the ship's own axes from four clusters**; 25 lb each, monomethylhydrazine and nitrogen tetroxide (p. 8-284); each ring's tanks (nitrogen 7.25 in; fuel 5.10 by 30.7 in; oxidiser 5.10 by 25.2 in: pp. 8-286 and 8-288). The instruments' sizes (keyboard, readout, velocity indicator, clock, timer: pp. 8-164, 8-172, 8-218 and 8-252) | Figure 8-84 gives each chamber's direction and not its tilt; the clusters' angles round the ship are not dimensioned. Where a tank sits is drawn, not dimensioned |
| P. W. Malik and G. A. Souris, *Project Gemini: A Technical Summary*, NASA CR-1106 (McDonnell Douglas for the Manned Spacecraft Center, June 1968) | [NTRS 19680016105](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19680016105) | **Stations the manual does not name**: Figure 1 (p. 5) has the equipment and retrograde sections dividing at Z68.44, the mating section from Z13.44 to Z15.38, and Z151.62 at what is taken for the hatches' forward edge; its side views place the window's pane, the trough ahead of it and the re-entry thrusters' clusters. Figure 5 (p. 15), the window | Written after the programme, of the type and not of spacecraft 3. Figure 1 is small: a label's place is good to an inch or two, and what Z151.62 marks is read off the drawing. It calls the command pilot's inner pane tempered aluminosilicate glass, where SEDR 300 has all three panes Vycor. Its title page spells the first author Malik |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini IV*, MSC-G-R-65-3 (NASA MSC, June 1965) | [NTRS 19670095754](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19670095754) | **What spacecraft 4 changed from spacecraft 3 (section 3.1 and Table 3-I) and GLV-4 from GLV-3 (section 3.2 and Table 3-IV)**: so what No. 3 and GLV-3 were. Figure 3-1, both vehicles' stations together; Figure 3-12, the re-entry control system's sixteen chambers; Figure 3-13, the OAMS chambers by number; the roll programme's times and rate (Tables 4-I and 5.2-V); remarks on GT-3's heat shield, scanner head and landing attitude | It is the next flight's report. Its Figure 3-1 has the first stage engines at station 1342.31, six inches from the launch systems report's |
| *Gemini Program Launch Systems Final Report: Gemini/Titan Launch Vehicle, Gemini/Agena Target Vehicle, Atlas SLV-3*, Aerospace Corporation, January 1967 | [NTRS 19670076796](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19670076796) | **The launch vehicle's stations: Figure II.C-1 (p. II.C-3)**, with the plan view (conduits, longerons, air scoops, roll nozzle); Figure II.C-2, lengths and thrusts; what made a Titan II a GLV (pp. II.C-1 and II.C-8); **the forward skirt's insulation by vehicle (p. II.C-8)**; the engines (p. II.C-17 on), and of the second stage's that its 49.2:1 chamber "was achieved by use of an ablative extension from 13:1" and that the turbine's exhaust goes out through a swivelled nozzle for roll (p. II.C-23); the first GLV-3 first stage fuel tank failing at 95 per cent of proof (PDF p. 114); pogo and the standpipes; chilled propellants (p. II.D-31); the missions' azimuths and orbits (Table II.D-8); **Table II.D-14, "Flight Test Trajectory Parameters Dispersion" (p. II.D-55): GT-3's first stage cut off 1.70 s early and its second 4.68 s early**, from which the staging time is derived; Air Force serials of GLV-1 and GLV-2 (p. II.E-38) | 597 pages, and the text layer is poor. Figure II.C-1 is small: only what it dimensions is documented. Table II.D-14 gives differences from a nominal it does not print: the nominal is taken from the press kit and SP-121. The text layer has the second stage's extension running "from 13:1 to 47.2:1" in the sentence that opens with 49.2:1. No engine drawing, and no area ratio for the first stage: it mentions only an increase "to 12/1" that was considered (PDF p. 279) |
| NASA release 65-81, "NASA schedules first manned Gemini flight from Cape Kennedy" (the Gemini-Titan 3 press kit), 17 March 1965 | [NTRS 19650009187](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19650009187) | The plan: azimuth, orbits and burns (pp. 7 to 10), among them the burn before retrofire, 93 ft/s to "a perigee of 52 miles" (p. 10); the weather guidelines (p. 9); the count and the ascent's times (pp. 10 and 11): engine start 4 s before liftoff, staging at 2:36, fairings at 3:15, cutoff at 5:38, separation at 5:58; **the three experiments and where each was (pp. 26 to 30)**; the survival kit, 23 lb (p. 35); "radar equipment is not carried for this flight", batteries in the adapter (pp. 37 and 38); sizes and weights in round figures (p. 39); the launch vehicle (pp. 39 and 40); the suit (pp. 41 and 42) | It is what was intended, a week before, and **the ascent was not flown to it**: the fairings went after the second stage's cutoff, and both stages cut off early. Its staging and cutoff times are kept only as the nominal the flown ones are worked from. Its lengths are rounded and its miles are statute |
| *Project Gemini: Technology and Operations, a Chronology*, NASA SP-4002 (1969) | [NTRS 19690027123](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19690027123) | **Appendix 1, Table A: launch time to the second, the launch weight, the orbit at insertion, the flight's highest apogee and lowest perigee, the times of the last burn, retrofire and landing, the landing point and its error, the recovery (p. 264)**; the flight's entry (pp. 189 to 191); spacecraft 3's dates at McDonnell (p. 156); every crew, date and duration of the programme | Table A prints 1 November 1966 for Gemini XII's launch. Its lowest perigee, 85.6 nautical miles, is the orbit after the first burn, not the 45 the last burn left. A printed page is the PDF's page less 20 |
| Barton C. Hacker and James M. Grimwood, *On the Shoulders of Titans: A History of Project Gemini*, NASA SP-4203 (1977) | [NTRS 19780012208](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19780012208) | The flight as a story (pp. 232 to 237): the count and the hold, the sky that morning, the name, the entry, the landing attitude, the sandwich. **Appendix B, "Flight Data Summary" (p. 524): the liftoff to the thousandth, 9:24:00.064 a.m. e.s.t.**, with the highest apogee, lowest perigee, duration, landing point and miss, which agree with SP-4002 | **Its narrative's figures are not followed where they stand alone**: the first orbit as "122 by 175 kilometers" (p. 235), the perigee after the third burn as 72 km (p. 235; 45 statute miles, where SP-138 has 45 nautical), the landing "about 84 kilometers short" (p. 236; 45 nautical miles, SP-138's perigee, where its own appendix has 111.1 km), the third burn as "two and a half" minutes (the transcript: 1 min 51 s). Its "about 20 minutes" and "75 seconds" are round figures for the transcript's 23 min 35 s and 74 s. A printed page is the PDF's page less 20 |
| *Gemini Midprogram Conference*, NASA SP-121 (1966) | [NTRS 19660020336](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19660020336) | The spacecraft in outline (pp. 15 to 21): the OAMS chambers' thrusts, the two-part adapter, ejection to 15,000 ft. **Table 4-III (p. 33), the orbit at insertion: 121.0 by 87.0 nautical miles against 130.1 by 87.1 planned**, with what the spacecraft's two guidance routines made of it (121 by 90; 128 by 78). **Table 4-IV (the same page), insertion: 25,682 ft/s at +0.01° and 353.8 s from liftoff** against 25,697 ft/s and 358.4 s planned | "eight 95-pound thrusters" on p. 16 is a misprint for 25. Insertion in Table 4-IV is 20 s after the second stage's cutoff, by the launch systems report's label for the same instant |
| *Gemini Summary Conference*, NASA SP-138 (Houston, 1 and 2 February 1967) | a NASA special publication; the address it was fetched from was not kept | **"Controlled Reentry", p. 163.** Table 13-I: Gemini III missed by 60 nautical miles with a navigation error of 0.8. The text: the burn before retrofire, finished 12 minutes before it, left a vacuum perigee of 45 nautical miles; the ship made about 35 per cent less lift for its drag than predicted, which cost about 160 nautical miles of reach; on the computer's commands throughout it would have missed by about 3 | One page of it was read for this flight |
| *Composite Air-to-Ground and Onboard Voice Tape Transcription of the GT-3 Mission* ("GT-3 Communications Transcript"), NASA MSC, April 1965 | NASA's mission transcript pages; the address was not kept | **The flight in the crew's and the ground's words, each line with its elapsed time from launch to the second**. The ascent's calls (pp. 2 to 5): roll at 0:12, pitch at 0:25, "there was staging" at 2:35, "SECO" at 5:34, "We are separated" at 6:10, **"There went all fairings" at 6:21**, "87 by 125" at 7:23. The converter's failure (p. 13). The three burns: 1:33:00 to 1:34:14 (pp. 37 and 38), about 2:17 (p. 54), 4:21:23 to 4:23:14 (pp. 90 and 91), each with the velocity indicator's readings and the crew's own timing; the propellant gauge before and after each. Retrofire at 4:33:23 (p. 96), the drogue at 4:46:48 (p. 100), the landing at 4:52:31 (p. 105). The landing area's weather as passed up at 3:08 (p. 77) | A call comes after its event: by a second at staging, by eleven at separation. Typed from tape, with gaps. The text layer misreads digits and "MARK", and the third burn's lines run out of order across pages 90 and 91 (the later ones are under the earlier number): go by the times, not the pages. The burn load read up at 0:52 (139 ft/s, 2 min 39 s) was not the one flown |
| *Gemini GT-3 Inertial Guidance System Evaluation, Trajectory Reconstruction*, supplemental report 4 to the mission report MSC-G-R-65-2 (17 May 1965; NASA TM X-61009) | ibiblio or the JSC History Portal, which both hold the supplements; which this copy came from was not kept | **Second stage cutoff at 334 s and separation at 359 s from liftoff** (section 3.2, PDF p. 18); liftoff 3.452 s after the platform went inertial (the same page); **retrofire at 16,403 s** (PDF p. 79); the spacecraft's place by guidance and by radar | To the second, in passing: it is about the guidance system's errors, not the sequence of events. 102 pages; pages cited by the PDF's count |
| NASA photographs of spacecraft 3: S65-13395 and S65-13394 (at the boresight range, Merritt Island, 6 January 1965, "final configuration"); S65-17337 (being mated to GLV-3 in the white room, February 1965) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/s65-13395) | **The cabin's shingles**: short beads lying round the cone, one after another along it, in cells between rows of fasteners (S65-13395, enlarged: about 1.1 in apart, estimated to a fifth); the window in its hatch, a lower cluster of re-entry thrusters' ports, two by two, the ribbed cover on the forward face; the adapter's foot, its gold cover and two thruster ports, and the Titan's oxidiser dome and tan skirt under it | One side of the ship, from close and at an angle. The first pass read the beads as running along the cone, which was wrong |
| NASA S65-21090: the spacecraft on its launch vehicle from above, in colour, 23 March 1965 | images.nasa.gov, by its number | **The top of the ship on the morning it flew**: both windows in their gold-coloured frames, each at the head of a trough cut into the cone; beaded shingles on the hatches and between them; a white strip along the top centreline from the cabin over the re-entry control section; the two upper clusters of re-entry thrusters, two ports by two; a bronze-coloured fairing on the left of the neck, taken to be the scanners' cover; a white disc near the left trough's end, not identified. The windows' place and width are measured on it | One photograph, steeply from above. Read through a pinhole camera fitted to the ship's documented circles and edges (vehicles/gemini-spacecraft.md), it gives the windows' inboard edges 26° to 28° from the top on both sides, their outboard points 53° to 55°, their aft edges Z141 to Z143, and the thrusters' clusters; the fit leaves about two inches near the top of the ship and cannot be trusted at the picture's edges (the lettering's stations are the liftoff photograph's). The window's straight edge is the inboard one. Where the hatch's forward edge is cannot be told from it |
| NASA S65-13244: spacecraft 3 with both hatches open, 6 January 1965 | images.nasa.gov, by its number | The cabin as built: the command pilot's and pilot's panels, both circuit-breaker panels, the seats' headrests, the hatches' inner faces with the window rings and their bolts, the openings' outline, "S/C 3" on the beam between them | From outside and above; nothing is measured on it |
| NASA S64-04925 and S64-04919: drawings of the re-entry communications experiment (1964) | images.nasa.gov, by number | The right landing gear door as a long tapering strip down the cabin, the plate of three "fluid injection nozzles" near its forward end, an arming receptacle half way along it (S64-04925); the spray into the plasma, before and during (S64-04919) | Drawings without dimensions |
| NASA photographs of the crew in the cabin and suited, February and March 1965: S65-22665 (8 March), S65-20605, S65-19225, S65-23489, S65-24719 and S65-24721 (seated, hatches open); S65-23485 (Young suited on the morning), S65-20601, S65-20604, S65-20994, S65-22670, S65-23486, S65-23488 and KSC-65-4922 | images.nasa.gov, by number | The G3C suit as worn: silvery white on the day, a white helmet with a clear visor on side pivots and a blue neck ring; the men in their seats, the harness, the hatch's inner structure and window bolts | No crew is modelled yet. Colours are a print's. S65-13243, S65-14457, S65-17399, S65-18645, S65-19228, S65-19229 and S65-20641 were fetched with these and nothing was taken from them |
| Smithsonian NASM object records, with their photographs: the G3C suits Grissom and Young wore on the flight (A19680456000 and A19730226000); two Gemini ejection seats (A19710058000 and A19690023001) | airandspace.si.edu, by object number | The suits: David Clark; torso, helmet, boots, gloves and neck seal over a constant-wear garment, with a life vest; materials; about 57 by 27 by 10 in laid flat; in the photographs, the name tag, emblem, connectors, wrist rings, the pressure zip up the back, laced boots. The seats: Weber; 22 by 55 in for the first, which its record calls "a spare that has been installed in the Gemini 3 spacecraft"; 24 wide by 54 high by 27 deep for the second, a training seat, grey; proportions for the model's seats, estimated to a tenth | Museum objects as conserved: the suits are off-white now. The spare's record is read as saying that the seat now in the ship at Mitchell is not one that flew. Neither record gives a seat's dimensions part by part |
| For the two men ([g3c-suit.md](../missions/usa/gemini/crew/g3c-suit.md)), the same Smithsonian photographs and NASA's, read again part by part: NASM2021-04069 (Grissom's suit, front), NASM2021-04126 and NASM2021-04127 (Young's, front and back); S65-23486 and S65-23488 (each man suiting up on the day, harness on), S65-23485 (Young, visor up), S65-22665 (Grissom in spacecraft 3, 8 March 1965). And NASA's biographical data sheet for John W. Young as Kennedy Space Center's site carried it (*YoungJW.txt*) | airandspace.si.edu and images.nasa.gov, by number; the sheet: science.ksc.nasa.gov/persons/astronauts/u-to-z/YoungJW.txt, **not opened** (the archives that hold it refused the request; only a search's report of it was seen) | Where every part of the suit is and what colour: the name tag, the insignia, the tie-down's cable, buckle and blue strap, the oval electrical connector, the two gas connectors (red at his right), the forearm gauge, the wrist rings (red at his right), the arm and shin pockets, the back zip and its two blue straps, the boots' red straps; the helmet's bright visor band and blue neck ring; the personal harness's grey webbing and its quick disconnects. **No flag and no life vest on either man or either suit.** Young "5 feet 9 inches; 165 pounds", by the search's report | Nothing of the suit is dimensioned: every size is scaled on the men. The Smithsonian's record lists silk "(flag)" among the materials and a life vest with the suit; neither is in a photograph. **Neither man's stature was read in a primary source**: Grissom's 5 ft 7 in is the common figure, Young's is hearsay of a NASA sheet until someone opens it |
| For the crew station's fit ([gemini-spacecraft.md](../missions/usa/gemini/vehicles/gemini-spacecraft.md), "Crew station"), read again and measured: SEDR 300's Figure 3-1 (the seats and the vessel's section, looking aft), Figures 2-4 and 2-5 (where the large bulkhead is), pp. 3-12 and 3-13 (the stirrups); Technical Summary Figures 2, 39 and 40 and p. 209 (the seats' cant, 8 degrees 20 minutes); S65-13244 (where the seats and the heads are along the hatch openings); the Smithsonian's photographs of seats A19690023000 (side: the bucket at about 100 degrees to the back), A19690023001 and A19710058000 (front: the tray, the stirrups) | as above | Where the seats stand, the bucket's rake, the seat's depth behind a man's back, the stirrups' drop, the vessel's sides and floor |
| The flown ship as kept at the Grissom Memorial, Spring Mill State Park: *Gemini3 Capsule.jpg* (2011) | Wikimedia Commons, by its file name | One hatch open: the opening's outline and red seal, the hatch's inner face and window ring, a seat's headrest; the forward shingles bare metal; a check on where the hatch's forward edge lies (about Z153, to 5 in) | One view, through glass. The ship has been through an entry and sixty years |
| NOAA NCEI Integrated Surface Database, 1965: station 747940-12868 (Cape Kennedy AFS, the Skid Strip, XMR: 28.483° N 80.550° W) and 747950-12867 (Patrick AFB, COF: 28.233° N 80.600° W), 23 March | NOAA NCEI, the database's yearly station files (`747940-12868-1965`, `747950-12867-1965`); the address was not kept | **The weather GT-3 left under.** The Skid Strip at 14:00 UTC, 24 minutes before liftoff: wind 140° at 5.7 m/s, no ceiling, visibility 16 km, 22.8 °C, dew point 18.9 °C, sea-level pressure 1024.7 hPa, sky cover `GF1 02` (scattered). At 13:00: 130° at 4.6 m/s, 21.6 °C, 1024.0 hPa. At 15:00: 150° at 7.2 m/s, 23.3 °C, 1025.1 hPa, GA1 `02,1,+03300,9,03` (2/8 altocumulus at 3,300 m). At 12:00: GA1 `01,1,+00480,9,08`, GA2 `01,1,+02700,9,03`, GA3 `03,1,+22000,9,02` (1/8 cumulus at 480 m, 1/8 altocumulus at 2,700 m, 3/8 cirrostratus). At 06:00 6/8 with a ceiling at 900 m; at 07:00 overcast (`GF1 08`), ceiling 1,080 m; from 17:00 broken, ceiling 3,000 m. Patrick: ceilings at 900 m at 08:00 and 09:00 (5/8 at 09:00) and at 2,400 m at 11:00; haze at 12:00 (visibility 9.6 km); at 14:00 150° at 6.2 m/s, 21.8 °C | Decoded by us from the fixed-width records (positions and codes per the NCEI format document): the cloud is `derived`. `GF1` takes only 02, 07 and 08 all day at both stations: the airways classes scattered, broken and overcast, not a count of oktas (at 12:00 it is 02 over layers adding to five). The high layer's 22,000 m is the record's limit, not a measured base. 16 km is the station's furthest mark. The Skid Strip is 3 km from Complex 19 and Patrick 30 km. **Not drawn**: the flight has no `weather.ts` until its site is built |
| NASA photographs of the vehicle: S65-21881 (on the stand, erector lowered, black and white); the liftoff in colour (Wikimedia Commons, *Gemini 3 - Liftoff.jpg*) | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/s65-21881); [Commons](https://commons.wikimedia.org/wiki/File:Gemini_3_-_Liftoff.jpg) | **The paint**: UNITED STATES down the first stage, the white and black bands, the roll pattern, the tan skirt; the conduits, the exhaust ports and the holes under them; UNITED STATES and the flag on the spacecraft's left side. Read again for the second pass: the bands stand as modelled; the tank walls show their chemically milled panels as a patchwork of tones, and at liftoff were white with frost, neither of which the model has | Each from one direction. NASA S65-10461, captioned Gemini-Titan 3, is dated November 1964 and shows "12557" on the vehicle: taken as GLV-2 |
| For the flight flown ([mission.md](../missions/usa/gemini/flights/gt-3/mission.md)): the guidance reconstruction (supplemental report 4) read again | as above | **Its input card (p. 75): "BOOSTER ENGINE CUTOFF=156.250, SUSTAINER ENGINE CUTOFF=337.450, TIME BIAS 3.452"**, from going inertial, liftoff 3.452 s after it (section 3.1): the first stage's cutoff at 152.80 s, replacing the 154.3 s worked from the press kit; the guidance's end point 14 nautical miles north and 59 west of the target (section 2.5) | The entry's listings are images, in guidance axes |
| *Gemini Program Mission Planning Report*, Working Paper 5019 (MSC, 6 January 1965; NASA TM X-69194) | [NTRS 19730073399](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19730073399) | Spacecraft 3 planned at 7,098 lb against a capability of 7,601 (Table A-I); the loads planned, OAMS fuel 120 lb and oxidiser 246 lb, re-entry control 72 lb (Table 3-I, p. 3-15); an older plan of the burns (pp. 3-4 and 3-5) | A plan, two months before |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report GT-1* | [NTRS 19670095753](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19670095753) | **GLV-1's engines as measured** (Table 5-II, p. 5-33): the first stage's two chambers 418,530 lb and 257.3 s at 5 s, 443,270 lb at 55 s, 472,770 lb at 140 s, 281.1 s at 155 s; the second stage 100,200 lb and 309.9 s | An unmanned flight's vehicle, taken for GLV-3's (`assumed`) |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini IV*, read again for the flight | [NTRS 19670095754](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19670095754) | **The vehicle's weights (section 3.3, p. 3-10)**: 340,000 lb at ignition, 336,000 at liftoff, 85,000 at first stage burnout, 73,400 at the second stage's steady burn, 13,300 at 20 s after its cutoff; spacecraft 4 at launch 7,879.05 lb, at retrofire 5,432.44, at 0.05 g 4,725.71, at the main 4,415.30, at touchdown 4,305.05 (p. 3-11); the propellant loaded (p. 5-87); **the engines (Tables 5.2-II and III)**, the second stage 102,777 lb and 313.07 s as flown; **GT-3's trim, 8 to 9 degrees at the greatest heating, and its centre of mass 1.43 in off its axis** (p. 5-3, section 5.1.1.3); its own retrofire attitude, 180° yaw and about 30° pitch (5.1.5) | The next flight's figures, taken for GT-3's (`assumed`); Table 5.2-II prints the first stage's flown impulse "361.47", a misprint. The pitch's sign is lost in the text layer |
| *Gemini Midprogram Conference*, NASA SP-121, read again | [NTRS 19660020336](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19660020336) | **Table 4-I (p. 29): the thrusters as tabled**, OAMS 8 × 23 lb at 258 s, 2 × 79 lb and 6 × 95 lb at 273 s; re-entry control 16 × 23 lb; the retrograde rockets 4 × 2,490 lb, 56,800 lb s, 220 lb; Figure 3-3 (p. 17), the retrofire sequence, the equipment section off 30 s before | The type's, not spacecraft 3's. The text's 25, 85 and 100 lb (p. 16) are round figures for the same chambers |
| *Gemini Summary Conference*, NASA SP-138, "Controlled Reentry", read again (pp. 159 to 165) | [NTRS 19680005472](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19680005472) | **"flown with an average lift-to-drag ratio of approximately 0.19"** (p. 159), inverted, the centre of mass toward the pilots' feet; GT-3's "decrease of approximately 35 percent in the lift-to-drag ratio", the target on the footprint's 60 degree contour and south of its centreline, the backup bank angle and then the computer's commands, the footprint shifted 48 nautical miles (p. 163); the retrofire's 320 ft/s and its pitch held by hand within 2 degrees (p. 162) | No figure of GT-3's own lift or its bank angles |
| *Project Gemini: A Technical Summary*, NASA CR-1106, read again | [NTRS 19680016105](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19680016105) | The OAMS chambers, 23.5, 79 and 94.5 lb, the forward-firing pair "canted obliquely" at an angle not given (p. 139); the design centre-of-mass offset of spacecraft 3, 1.35 in (p. 37); the drogue, an 8.3 ft conical ribbon with twelve lines and two 16 s reefing cutters (p. 218); the main, an 84.2 ft ringsail of 72 lines disreefed at 10 s, 31.6 ft/s for a module of 4,400 lb (p. 222) | Of the type |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini IV*, MSC-G-R-65-3, read again for Gemini 3's orbit (November 2026) | [NTRS 19670095754](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19670095754) | **What the orbit's heights are over** (Gemini 3's `orbit.over`, assumed): section 4.3, "The earth model for all trajectories contained geodetic and gravitational constants representing the Fischer Ellipsoid"; section 4.3.1.2, "Preliminary actual orbital elements were measured over a spherical earth, whereas the final actual and planned elements were measured over an oblate earth. The spherical earth elements are approximately 0.8 nautical mile less", and the final elements at insertion "based on the Bermuda vector". **Table 4-III**, the final insertion set 152.2 by 87.6 nautical miles, 32.53°, 88.90 min; **Table 4-II (p. 4-14)**, the separation as finally determined at 365.6 s, 30.97° N, 87.6 nautical miles, path 0.07° (where its perigee stood): tests/periods.test.mjs | Neither section nor table says what kind of period it prints, nor what the spherical earth's radius was |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini V*, MSC-G-R-65-4, read again for Gemini 3's orbit (November 2026) | [ibiblio.org](https://www.ibiblio.org/apollo/Documents/Gemini5MissionReport.pdf) | Section 4.3.1.2: "The actual apogees and perigees were obtained by integrating the best Gemini tracking network vectors throughout the mission to the apogee and perigee that followed ... Preliminary elements are outputs from the real-time computer complex (RTCC) during the mission and are measured over a spherical earth; final elements are measured over an oblate earth"; section 4.3, the Fischer ellipsoid. **Table 4.3-II (p. 4-20), read on the page**: the final insertion set 188.9 by 87.4 nautical miles, 32.59°, 89.59 min (the text layer garbles the apogee; SP-4002 prints 188.9 too); **Table 4.3-I (p. 4-18)**: the separation as finally determined at 356.91 s, 30.89° N, path 0.0° | As Gemini IV's: what kind of period is not said |
| *Project Gemini: Technology and Operations, a Chronology*, NASA SP-4002, Appendix 1, Table A, read on the page (the image at nasa.gov/history/SP-4002/app1a.htm) | as above | Its "At insertion" rows are the mission reports' final elements: Gemini IV's 152.2 by 87.6, 88.90 min, 32.53°; Gemini V's 188.9 by 87.4, 89.59, 32.59°; so Gemini 3's 121.0 by 87.0, 88.3, 32.6° is read as the same reduction | No note on what the heights are over or what the period is |
| SEDR 300 vol. 1, read again | as above | The retrograde rockets "fired at 5.5 second intervals in 1-2-3-4 order" (p. 11-73); the PARA switch at 10,600 ft and the swing to landing attitude (pp. 4-22 and 4-23) | |

Known, not read:

| Source | What it would settle |
| --- | --- |
| How Mercury's and Gemini's published periods were computed (node to node, perigee to perigee, or the two-body period of a vector): **not found**. Searched: the Gemini IV and V reports' section 4.3.1.2 and their orbital-elements tables' notes; SP-4002's Table A; NTRS by the reports' numbers and a web search for Goddard's Mercury orbit computation (November 2026), which found only programs not Mercury's (TN D-1539, TN D-4470) | Which reading of the period each record flies: tests/periods.test.mjs reads it off the flights' own figures instead, and the reading Gemini's sets come nearest, the osculating orbit's at insertion, is not one the simulator flies (docs/agents/world.md) |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report GT-3, Gemini 3*, MSC-G-R-65-2 (April 1965): **looked for three times and not found** (NTRS by title, by report number and through its API; archive.org; ibiblio's Gemini shelves; the JSC History Portal, whose folder cannot be listed; NASA's transcript pages). The Aerospace *Flight Evaluation Report, Gemini/Titan GT-3* and the GLV-3 quick-look report (TOR-469(5126-42)-7), which the launch systems report's bibliography names, are not online either. Only its supplements and the transcripts are online | Every event to the tenth of a second, the first stage's cutoff and the engines' start among them; the weights as flown; the weather at the pad and where it landed; what spacecraft 3 and GLV-3 changed from the vehicles before them; the thrusters' performance |
| Anything giving the adapter batteries' size and place, the radiator's stripes, or what stood in the rendezvous section in the radar's place: not found | Three things the model guesses or leaves out |
| Martin's GLV familiarisation manual and the Aerospace flight evaluation report for GT-3 (TOR-469(5126-42)-9) | The launch vehicle from drawings; GLV-3's weights and performance; where its number was painted |
| Drawings of the Aerojet LR87-AJ-7 and LR91-AJ-7: none found. The first stage's area ratio is in no primary source read, and the one set of engine sizes found is a secondary one that conflicts with others | The bells' exits, which the model sizes from ratios, and the engines' forms |
| A dimensioned drawing of the pressure vessel, its bulkheads, the seats' spacing, the panels' layout or the window's panes: none found in SEDR 300, its Section VIII or the Technical Summary | The cabin's inside, which is fitted round the two seated men, the documented seat and the cone, and held to SEDR 300's Figure 3-1 and S65-13244 where they bear on it; what the window's section means by Z136.50, which the photographs do not bear out as the pane's sunk end |
| The crew's heights and seated dimensions in a primary source. Looked for and not found: the press kit's crew pages; NASA's biographical data sheet for Grissom (Johnson Space Center's); the NASA History Office's "Mercury Seven Astronaut Biographies" (nasa.gov/history/40thmerc7), which gives Shepard's, Glenn's and Schirra's and not Grissom's; the medical papers of SP-121 and SP-138. 5 ft 7 in for Grissom is in secondary sources only; NASA's sheet for Young (5 ft 9 in by a search of it) could not be opened | The figures in the seats, and through them the crew station's sizes |
| A weather observation from the landing area (the *Intrepid*'s, or the mission report's) | The sea, wind and cloud at the landing. Only what the crew were told at 3 h 08 min is known |
| A photograph of the spacecraft's right side on the day | UNITED STATES and the flag there, which are assumed from the left |
| Drawings or a survey of Launch Complex 19 | The stand's azimuth, and with it which way the vehicle faced |
| More of the flown spacecraft at the Grissom Memorial, Mitchell, Indiana, than the one photograph read | The right side's paint, the shingles all round, both hatches |

### Gemini-Titan 4 and Gemini-Titan 5

What spacecraft No. 4 and No. 5, GLV-4 and GLV-5 and the two flights rest on
(`missions/usa/gemini/flights/gt-4/`, `gt-5/`), beside the type's sources above. Read in November
2026, in each PDF's text layer and, for the figures and for any table whose digits the text layer
garbles, on the page.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini IV*, MSC-G-R-65-3 (NASA MSC, June 1965) | [NTRS 19670095754](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19670095754) | **The whole of Gemini-Titan 4.** What spacecraft 4 changed (3.1, Table 3-I) and GLV-4 (3.2, Table 3-IV); **the extravehicular equipment (3.1.2.3.1 to 3.1.2.3.3; Figures 3-7 to 3-10, the last a photograph of the manoeuvring unit)**; the six batteries (Figure 3-11) and four water tanks (Figure 3-6); the panels with the G.m.t. clock (Figure 3-14); **where everything was stowed (Table 3-III and Figure 3-15)**; **weights (3.3)**; the flight in outline (4.1); **every event of the ascent to the hundredth of a second and of the entry to the second (Table 4-I)**; the orbit, revolution by revolution (Table 4-III); the 74 station-keeping burns, each by thruster and length (Table 4-IV); the hatch's latch (5.1.1.4); the OAMS's loading and use (5.1.8.1); the two flashing lights on the second stage (5.2.6); the space walk as the crew told it (7.1); the experiments (section 8) | The times of the space walk are "approximately" and "about". Its summary and its section 4.1 give the landing's longitude three minutes apart. The first stage's thrust as flown is in section 5.2, which was not transcribed |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini V*, MSC-G-R-65-4 (NASA MSC, August 1965) | [ibiblio.org, the Virtual AGC project](https://www.ibiblio.org/apollo/Documents/Gemini5MissionReport.pdf); not found on NTRS by title or number | **The whole of Gemini-Titan 5.** What spacecraft 5 changed (3.1, Table 3.1-I): **the radar (3.1.2.4.2, Figure 3.1-4a), the pod (the same, Figure 3.1-4b), the fuel cell module (3.1.2.6, Figure 3.1-5), the two water tanks (3.1.2.3, Figure 3.1-3)**; GLV-5 (3.2); **weights (3.3)**; the flight (4.1); **the sequence of events (Table 4.2-I)**; the orbit (Table 4.3-II); the pod's ejection (Table 4.3-III); **the oxygen tank's heater and pressures (5.1.7.2)**; the OAMS (5.1.8); the flight controllers' account of the first day (6.1.2.2) | **Its text layer garbles digits throughout** (3 for 8, _ for 5): Tables 4.2-I and 4.3-II were read on the page. None of its figures of hardware has a dimension. Its sections disagree by a few psi on the oxygen pressures. The azimuth is not in what was read |
| NASA release 65-158, "NASA schedules four-day manned Gemini flight from Cape Kennedy" (the Gemini-Titan 4 press kit), May 1965 | [NTRS 19650013875](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19650013875) | Where the three MSC experiments were and what they weighed (pp. 51 to 54); oxygen aboard, 52 lb primary; the launch vehicle's nominal thrusts, used for both flights' engines | The plan, a fortnight before; its text layer is letter-spaced and hard to search |

Known, not read:

| Source | What it would settle |
| --- | --- |
| A drawing or dimensioned figure of the rendezvous evaluation pod, the fuel cell sections, the reactant supply tanks and the rendezvous radar's antennas | Four things on spacecraft 5 whose sizes are scaled from sketches or derived from loads |
| SEDR 300's Section VIII supplement for the rendezvous ships (read since for the OAMS tanks: the entry under Gemini-Titan 7 and 6A. Spacecraft 5 had one tank of each, of the early ships' size) | The radar |
| Close photographs of spacecraft 4 and 5 before launch, and of the two flown ships as kept | Their paint, the forward face of spacecraft 4, whether either had spacecraft 3's white disc |
| The photographs of White in the open hatch, measured (NASA S65-30427 and its neighbours were looked at only as published) | How far the pilot's hatch opened: 100° is by eye |
| The G4C suit: David Clark's drawings, the Smithsonian's records of the suits McDivitt, White, Cooper and Conrad wore | The suit's sizes and the flag's, which crew/g4c-suit.md estimates from photographs |
| Aerospace's flight evaluation reports for GLV-4 and GLV-5; Martin's GLV manual | Their weights and performance in detail, their serial numbers, where GLV-4's flashing lights were |
| The Gemini V press kit (found since, on NTRS as "Gemini 5": the entry under Gemini-Titan 7 and 6A) | It has an artist's view of the pod and no size or weight for it; the fuel cells' dimensions were not looked for in it |
| NASA SP-4002's Appendix 1, Table A, read again for these two flights (it was read for Gemini-Titan 3 and for the programme's table of durations) | A second source for each flight's weight, orbit and landing |

### Gemini-Titan 7 and Gemini-Titan 6A

What spacecraft No. 7 and No. 6, GLV-7 and GLV-6 and the two flights rest on
(`missions/usa/gemini/flights/gt-7/`, `gt-6a/`), beside the type's sources above. Read in November
2026, in each PDF's text layer and, for the figures and for any table whose digits the text layer
garbles, on the page. **The Gemini VI-A mission report of January 1966 was not found**: Gemini-Titan
6A's record is put together from the documents round it, and says so.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini VII*, MSC-G-R-66-1 (NASA MSC, January 1966; NASA TM X-62892; with Change 1 of 12 October 1966 bound in) | [ibiblio.org, the Virtual AGC project](https://www.ibiblio.org/apollo/Documents/Gemini7MissionReport.pdf); not found on NTRS by title or number | **The whole of Gemini-Titan 7.** What spacecraft 7 changed from spacecraft 5 (3.1, Table 3.1-I): **the transponder in the radar's place and the two acquisition lights (3.1.2.4), water tank C (3.1.2.3, Figure 3.1-4), the OAMS reserve fuel tank (3.1.2.7, Figure 3.1-10), the hydrogen tank's insulation (3.1.2.6.2, Figure 3.1-8), the elapsed-time clock in place of the flight plan roller and the range indicator deleted (3.1.2.5, 3.1.2.9.1, Figure 3.1-12), the oxygen crossfeed (Figure 3.1-3), the G5C suit (3.1.2.9.2), the stowage (Table 3.1-II, Figure 3.1-14)**; that the rendezvous evaluation pod's mount on spacecraft 5 was "on the blast shield access door" (3.1.1.2); GLV-7 against GLV-6 and GLV-4, **the four flashing lights (3.2.2.5, Table 3.2-I)**; the weights (3.3). **The record**: liftoff 19:30:03.702 G.m.t. and why it was 3.702 s late (4.1); the sequence of events (Table 4.2-I); the trajectory and landing point (Table 4.3-I); the manoeuvres and the orbit after each (Tables 4.3-II and 4.3-III); the fuel cells (5.1.7), the reactants (5.1.7.3), the OAMS and the failure of thrusters 3 and 4 (5.1.8, Table 5.1.8-I), the primary oxygen (5.1.4.2), the transponder's ground test (5.1.5.1.2), pogo and the 83.6° azimuth (5.2.1.1, 5.2.9.1), the recovery (6.3.2), the crew's account (7.1.2), MSC-2 and MSC-3 (8.12, 8.13). **For Gemini-Titan 6A**: its liftoff, 13:37:26.47 G.m.t., as 258:07:22.77 of this flight (4.1); what was "effective with spacecraft 6" (the hatch latches, 3.1.1.1; the HF antenna switch, 3.1.2.9.1); what spacecraft 5 and 6 had that 7 had not (radar, encoder: 3.1.2.4); GLV-6 against GLV-7 (3.2, Table 3.2-I), the breakwire on its tail plugs after 12 December; the cords on both adapters (5.1.9) | The text layer garbles digits throughout ("85.6°" for 83.6°, "29°25.1′" for 25°25.1′, "i5 :37" for 13:37): Tables 4.2-I, 4.3-I, 4.3-II, 4.3-III, 5.1.8-I, section 3.3 and the azimuth were read on the page. Its figures are sketches without dimensions. It gives no time for the fairings, for equipment section separation or for retrorockets 2 to 4, and none for crew ingress. Table 4.3-III's last period is not legible (90.51 or 90.61) |

| *Launch Vehicle No. 6 Flight Evaluation*, Supplemental Report 2 to MSC-G-R-65-5 and MSC-G-R-66-2 (Martin Company, ER 13227-6, February 1966) | [archive.org, nasa_techdoc_19750069375](https://archive.org/details/nasa_techdoc_19750069375) (its text layer; found through archive.org's full-text search, 17 November 2026) | **GLV-6 on both counts, from its own telemetry.** 15 December, Table II-4, "GT-6A Flight Events Summary": the sequencer's T-0 at 13:37:23.13 G.m.t., the first stage start signal (87FS1) at −3.279 s, liftoff 13:37:26.471, BECO (87FS2) 157.162 s, the second stage's chamber pressure rising at 157.829 s and the stages apart at 157.844 s (II.C: 0.667 s from the signal), guidance SECO 338.717 s and the shutdown signal (91FS2) 338.737 s, spacecraft separation 360.9 s; Summary: stage I burned 160.4 s to oxidiser exhaustion, stage II 181.6 s; the MOCS from T-35 min to "the programmed 25-minute hold at T-3". **Table II-10, "GT-6 Weight Summary":** loaded 346,089 lb, 342,472 lb at liftoff (269,308 + 65,343 + the spacecraft's 7,821), 84,928 lb at BECO, 14,052 lb at SECO + 20 s. **12 December (III.A, III.B):** T-0 at 14:54:03.20 G.m.t., the holdfire C-4 at 14:54:04.40, shutdown at 87FS1 + 1.158 s; the dust cap in subassembly 2's gas generator oxidiser injector inlet; without the plug's fault, a shutdown at 2.2 s by the chamber pressure switch | Read from the text layer, which scrambles some digits ("136.52" for 156.52 in the planned column); the figures used were checked against each other (the burn times against the events). It is the launch vehicle's: no spacecraft event but the separation, and no fairing time |

| Aerospace's and Martin's flight evaluation of GLV-7 | Where round and along the second stage GLV-7's four lights were; the vehicle's paint (GLV-6's sequence of events and weights are in its evaluation, read: above) |
| A dimensioned drawing of water tank C, the acquisition light, the L-band spiral antenna and the mooring latch receptacle (SEDR 300 revisions after November 1965; McDonnell's final report) | Four things sized here from loads or from sketches |
| The other frames each crew took of the other ship (NASA S65-63xxx, S65-64xxx), at full size | Where spacecraft 7's two acquisition lights and its docking light were; the fitting on its retrograde section; both ships' left sides; spacecraft 6's face out of glare |
| The G5C suit: David Clark's drawings, the Smithsonian's records of Borman's and Lovell's suits | The hood's form, which crew/g5c-suit.md takes from the G3C's helmet |
| Photographs of Gemini-Titan 7 and Gemini-Titan 6A on the stand or at liftoff, read for the paint | The vehicles' lettering, roll pattern and bare skirt are assumed as GLV-3's to GLV-5's |

### Gemini-Titan 8 and Gemini-Titan 9A

What spacecraft No. 8 and No. 9, GLV-8 and GLV-9, the two flights and their two targets rest on
(`missions/usa/gemini/flights/gt-8/`, `gt-9a/`; `vehicles/agena_target_vehicle.py`,
`target_docking_adapter.py`, `atda.py`), beside the type's sources above. Read in November 2026, in
each PDF's text layer and, for the figures and for any table whose digits the text layer garbles, on
the page.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini VIII*, MSC-G-R-66-4 (NASA MSC, April 1966) | the Internet Archive's Wayback Machine, its capture of the copy the Apollo Lunar Surface Journal kept (the nasa.gov address now answers 404). Not on NTRS by title or number; ibiblio has only its Supplemental Report 4 | **The whole of Gemini-Titan 8.** What spacecraft 8 changed from spacecraft 6 (3.1, Table 3.1-I; Figures 3.1-1, 3.1-2, 3.1-9, 3.1-10, 3.1-11): the fuel cells as spacecraft 7's without the hydrogen tank's insulation, two 42 lb water tanks, the reserve fuel tank, the handrails and Velcro, the handholds and foot supports, the light pointing aft, the Extravehicular Support Package. GLV-8 from GLV-6 (3.2, Table 3.2-I). GATV-5003 from GATV-5002 (3.4; Figures 3.0-2 and 3.4-2: stations, the antennas, the running lights). The weights (3.3, 3.6). **Table 4.2-I, read on the page:** every time in the flight's timeline. The manoeuvres (Table 4.3-III), the orbits, the thruster (pp. 5-25, 5-85 to 5-90), the recovery (6.3, Table 4.3-I) | An Acrobat "ClearScan" scan: the text layer spaces letters, breaks digits ("thruster 3" for 8) and shuffles lines. Pages 3-29, 3-30, 4-7 and 4-8 are missing (Figure 3.1-7, the panels). It describes the Agena only as changed from the one before |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini IX-A*, MSC-G-R-66-6 (NASA MSC, July 1966), 639 pages | tothemoon.im-ldi.com; not found on NTRS, ibiblio or archive.org | **The whole of Gemini-Titan 9A.** What spacecraft 9 changed from spacecraft 8 (3.1): the window covers, the Astronaut Maneuvering Unit in place of the support package, the stirrups, the hatch's strap, the single OAMS power switch. GLV-9 from GLV-8 (Table 3.2-I). **The ATDA (3.7; 5.8.1.2, pp. 5-161 to 5-164): 146.75 in in orbit, the sections, six running lights, the shroud "pivoted to about 30 degrees", the band clamp 22 in forward of the interface, the lanyards taped down, the paint.** Table 4.2-I and Table 4.3-III: the timeline and the manoeuvres. The walk (7.1.2; p. 5-95: the visor "about 8 minutes after local sunset"). **Figure 8.1-18: the unit on its torque box, Z66.74 to Z56.27, turned 22.5°**; Figure 8.1-19, a photograph of it in the adapter; Figure 3.1-6, the cover. Section 12: the weather at 8:40 a.m. EST | Pages 3-25, 3-26 and 3-47 to 3-50 are missing: with them the report's own drawings of the ATDA (Figures 3.7-1 to 3.7-3). Its text prints 241.25 in where the drawings have 241.52 |
| *Project Gemini Familiarization Manual*, SEDR 300, volume 2 (entry under Gemini-Titan 4 and 5), read again for spacecraft 8 and 9 and the targets | as above | **Section XIII, the docking system on "spacecraft 8 through 12": Figures 13-1, 13-2 and 13-8 (the index bar, the latch receptacles).** Section XIV, the Target Docking Adapter: seven dampers in three sets (pp. 14-3, 14-5), the notch "with a slot at the bottom of the V", the status display (p. 14-7), three discharge fingers (p. 14-14). **Section XV, the ATDA: p. 15-5 (the paint, as the manual has it), p. 15-6, p. 15-11 and Figure 15-1 (the lights: green and red at the top, amber below), p. 15-90 (the shroud's "one long segment (including the nose cone) and one short segment").** Section XVI, Figures 16-2 and 16-9: the handholds, the foot rail, the floodlights. Figure 5-7, "RSS/Fuel Cell Module (S/C 8 & 9)"; p. 5-33 (5.80 lb of hydrogen and 46.0 lb of oxygen); Figure 6-10 sheet 6 (water tank A); p. 12-17 (the parachute container, 22.25 by 21.32 in); p. 2-17 ("The inner adapter surfaces of spacecraft 9 through 12 are gold plated") | Its figures are sketches without dimensions. Its ATDA is "painted black with white longitudinal stripes", which the photographs are not |
| *Project Gemini: A Technical Summary*, NASA CR-1106 (entry under Gemini-Titan 3), read again: pp. 241, 318, 323 to 343; Figures 60, 61, 65 and **66** | [NTRS 19680016105](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19680016105) (PDF page = printed + 7) | **Figure 66, the ATDA dimensioned: 117.00, 44.40, 54.00, 18.00, 17.75, 12.60, 134.15 and 241.52 in along; 65.12, 60, 40.4 and 36.2 in across.** The index bar's travel, 13.82 in (p. 241). The docking light, "4000 candlepower ... centered above and just aft of the heads of the flight crew" (p. 327).
