| The missing pages of the two mission reports (Gemini VIII: 3-29, 3-30, 4-7, 4-8; Gemini IX-A: 3-25, 3-26, 3-47 to 3-50) | Spacecraft 8's panels; the ATDA as its own report draws it |
| The Gemini VIII Technical Debriefing (on the Wayback Machine; the download was refused) | The crew's own account of the roll and of the Agena as they found it |
| McDonnell's drawings of the Extravehicular Support Package and Ling-Temco-Vought's of the Astronaut Maneuvering Unit; the Air Force's report on experiment D-12 | Both packs' outsides, drawn here from small figures |
| The other frames each crew took of its target (NASA S66-257xx, S66-379xx), at full size | The Agena's far side; which half of the ATDA's shroud kept the nose, which side its black band was on, where the wires ran |
| Photographs of Gemini-Titan 8 and Gemini-Titan 9A on the stand, read for the paint | Both vehicles' lettering and roll pattern, assumed as GLV-3's to GLV-7's |
| The countdown records for 16 March and 3 June 1966 | When the hatches were shut and the erector went down on each morning, and how long Gemini 9A's hold at T-3 lasted: the plans are used |
| NASA release 66-52's companion on the experiments (NTRS 19660022876; downloaded, not read) | Where experiments D-14, D-15 and D-16 were on spacecraft 8 |

### The Gemini adapter's equipment section

What the equipment section of every Gemini in the repo is laid out from
(`missions/usa/gemini/vehicles/gemini_spacecraft.py`; `gemini-spacecraft.md`, "Equipment section"), read in
November 2026 on the page: each figure below was rendered from its PDF and looked at, and the two
that carry measurements were measured in pixels. None of McDonnell's figures of the section is
dimensioned beyond three stations.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *Project Gemini: A Technical Summary*, NASA CR-1106 (entry under Gemini-Titan 3), **Figure 4, "Adapter equipment module structure" (p. 12)**, Figure 3, "Adapter structure" (p. 10), and p. 13 | [NTRS 19680016105](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19680016105) | **The arrangement, with the ship's sides named on it (TY, BY, LX, RX):** the blast shield at Z66.74, "aluminum honeycomb" with a "fiberglass facing", made of the modules' support panels and an access door ("their support panels and an access door combine to form a blast shield"); two beams across the section; the OAMS module the segment beyond one, the fuel cell module the segment beyond the other; the electronic module, the access door and the environmental control system module between; the ECS pump module a frame from Z28.61 to Z41.17. **Figure 3: the three retaining straps' places at Z103.44, looking forward: 13° 08' above LX and above RX, and 45° from BY toward RX** | Figure 4 is a perspective sketch. The beams are drawn 23° off square to its own TY-BY line; taken as drawn, because Figure 8-72 (below) agrees. It scales the beams to 14 in and 20 in from the axis; the model has 15 in. p. 13 gives the retrograde section as "30 in. in length", which is not the 35 in of its own Figure 1's Z68.44 |
| SEDR 300 volume 2, Section VIII supplement (entry under Gemini-Titan 7 and 6A), **"Rendezvous Evaluation Pod", pp. 8-289 to 8-299; Figure 8-79 (p. 8-290), the pod; Figure 8-80 (p. 8-292), "Mounting and ejection of cover and rendezvous evaluation pod"** | [ibiblio.org](https://www.ibiblio.org/apollo/Documents/GeminiManualVol2Sec2.pdf), PDF pp. 287 to 297 | **The pod at first hand.** "The transponder, 10 by 10 by 20 inches, is the largest component ... the basic component to which all other components are attached"; the spiral antennas "extend outward approximately two inches from the case"; the dipole "on the end of a two-foot long fixed boom"; two "toroid-shaped, xenon-filled, 25-watt" lights "on opposite sides", 80 flashes a minute; two squib batteries and a boost regulator; "installed in the center of the equipment adapter section"; the cover "an oval, plane structure ... a tubular metal frame over which a thin fiberglass cloth is stretched", silvered 0.00035 in thick, on "four tubular posts, which extend past the REP", two ending in guide pins and two in spring sockets; the cover thrown off at 100 ft/s, the pod 80 ms later at 5 ft/s | The two figures are perspective drawings with no dimensions: which face of the transponder looked aft, where each fitting is on it, and the cover's outline are scaled from them. Figure 8-79 is the same drawing as the Gemini V report's Figure 3.1-4b, larger |
| SEDR 300 volume 1, Section VIII, Figure 8-72 (p. 8-256), and volume 2's Figure 8-94 (p. 8-342), read again for where the tanks stand | as above | The OAMS module's frame with the tanks in a row on it, the valve packages on their manifold along it, the pressurant tank, the reserve tank and the "F" package; water tanks A and B at the row's ends on spacecraft 7. The view is from forward of the adapter looking aft (thruster 16, which pushes the ship down, is at the top, and 13, which pushes it right, is on the right of the picture), and in it the row of tanks is turned 25° from square to the ship's top | A sketch in perspective. It draws the pressurant tank inboard of the row, where the model does not put it. Figure 8-94's leaders for spacecraft 5's tanks do not agree with the Gemini V report's Figure 3.1-3 |
| SEDR 300 volume 1, Figures 5-1 and 5-2 (pp. 5-2 and 5-4), 5-5 (p. 5-8), 6-10 sheets 1, 2, 4 and 5 (pp. 6-19 to 6-23); volume 2, Figures 2-1 (p. 2-2), 5-6 (p. 5-12), 5-9 (p. 5-15) and 6-10 sheet 3 (p. 6-21) | the two manuals, as above | Each module's form: the fuel cell module's beam, the two sections at one end and the two tanks beside them, with their heaters, sensors and shutoff valves; spacecraft 3's one water tank, a drum in two bolted halves; **spacecraft 4's six batteries in two tiers, four such drums in pairs and an oxygen bottle (sheet 4)**; spacecraft 6's three ribbed batteries in a row, its water tank a ball with a bottle of oxygen; the OAMS module's frame, straight inboard and curved to the wall, with four balls in a row (sheet 5) | No dimensions. Each figure's inset blacks in the module's place in the same small drawing of the ship, which is too schematic to take a side from |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini V* (entry under Gemini-Titan 4 and 5), Figures 3.1-2, 3.1-3, 3.1-4b and 3.1-5 | as above | Figure 3.1-2 names the equipment section's modules: "electronic module, OAMS, ECS coolant module, ECS primary O2 module, RSS/fuel cell module, REP". Figure 3.1-3: spacecraft 5's two water tanks at the ends of the OAMS module's row | Sketches |
| *Gemini Program Mission Report, Gemini IV* (entry under Gemini-Titan 3), 8.4.2 | as above | The spectrometer "on a support assembly fixed to the center of the equipment adapter section blast shield door" | |
| NASA S65-63221, spacecraft 7 from behind, taken from spacecraft 6 on 15 December 1965, at its full size (4,115 by 4,134 pixels) | Wikimedia Commons, *S65-63221 - Gemini 6 Views Gemini 7 (Retouched).jpg* | **The temperature control cover, measured.** An ellipse fitted to the gold's rim has semi-axes of 748.7 and 564.9 pixels: the camera was 41° off the ship's axis. The hoop the cloth is gathered to is 270 pixels in half-width and its middle 147 pixels up the axis from the rim's: 44 in across and 18 in forward of the rim. Inside the hoop a flat disc with an opening about 10.6 in wide, an instrument looking out of it and the magnetometer's boom run out through it; the two aft-firing thrusters standing through the cloth at 0.86 to 0.92 of the rim's radius, opposite each other; the cloth gathered in radial pleats | One photograph of one ship, read with a weak-perspective camera and an 80 mm lens assumed: the depth is good to about 3 in and the width to 4 in. A raised oblong under the cloth beside one thruster, and a small fitting at the rim on either side, are not identified |
| NASA S65-17337 (entry under Gemini-Titan 3), looked at again | [images.nasa.gov](https://images.nasa.gov/details/S65-17337) | Spacecraft 3's cover from below as it is lowered onto GLV-3: gathered cloth sloping up into the section from the rim, as spacecraft 7's is, with the Titan's dome standing up under it | Black and white, steeply from below; nothing measured on it |
| NASA release 65-262, the Gemini 5 press kit (entry under Gemini-Titan 7 and 6A), p. 6A | [NTRS 19650020587](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19650020587) | An artist's view of the pod in orbit | Not used: SEDR 300 volume 2's Figure 8-79 is McDonnell's own drawing of it |

Known, not read:

| Source | What it would settle |
| --- | --- |
| A dimensioned drawing of the adapter's equipment section (McDonnell's installation drawings, or the stress report behind the Technical Summary's Figure 4) | How far the beams are from the axis and how far turned from the ship's sides; each tank's place; the access door's size |
| A photograph of spacecraft 5's adapter from behind, in the white room or at mating | How the gold cover was cut round the pod's cover; the pod cover's true outline |
| Photographs of an equipment section with the modules in and the cover off (McDonnell, St. Louis; the white room at Complex 19) | The batteries' and the electronic module's real forms; where the helium tank and the valve packages stand |
| The other frames of 15 December 1965 that show spacecraft 7's foot (NASA S65-63xxx), at full size | The raised oblong under the cover; whether the fittings at the rim are the acquisition lights |

## The Lineup's history

What `missions/history.ts` rests on: the milestones, each flight's story and name, the people and the programmes. Every book here was read as text extracted from its PDF, its pages found by searching that text; a row says where a page was read only through a search engine's summary. Entries under the programmes above, read again for this, say what was taken from them.

### Mercury, Gemini 3 to 7, and the American context

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| *This New Ocean* (entry under Project Mercury), read again for the history | as above, read as text extracted from it | Explorer I (p. 29); the Space Act signed 29 July 1958 (p. 98) and NASA's start on 1 October (p. 113); Mercury named by Glennan and Dryden on 26 November 1958 (p. 132); the Seven introduced on 9 April 1959 (p. 160); Ham's flight (pp. 314 to 316) and MR-BD (p. 324); **Kennedy's message of 25 May 1961, quoted at length (p. 362)**; **the hatch on MR-4: the committee, Schirra's "very remote possibility", "never solved to everyone's satisfaction", the hypotheses, and Grissom the only pilot to blow the hatch without hurting his hand (pp. 376 and 377)**; Enos (pp. 400 to 406); the names: Liberty Bell 7 (p. 368), Friendship 7 "named in a contest by his own family" (p. 420), Aurora 7 and the corner of Aurora and Seventh in Boulder (p. 446), Sigma 7 (p. 470), Faith 7 and the *Washington Post* on it (p. 492) | Printed pages differ from the PDF's by 5 to 11 through the book (plates): read the page's own number. Its p. 333 gives Vostok 1's call sign as "Swallow", which it was not (Кедр); it was written before the Soviet record opened |
| *On the Shoulders of Titans* (entry under Gemini-Titan 3), read again for Gemini 3 to 7 | as above, read as text extracted from it (printed page = PDF page − 20 in the flight chapters) | Gemini named, after Alex Nagy, and approved on 7 December 1961 (pp. 1 to 4 and 73); **Molly Brown, Titanic, and "Later spacecraft were officially referred to by a Roman numeral" (p. 233)**; the sandwich and Congress (p. 237); Houston's control centre first used for Gemini IV (p. 239); Gemini IV had no name or patch, "Little Eva" (p. 245, note); White's "saddest moment" (p. 249); the stationkeeping (pp. 243 and 254); Cooper's covered wagon and "Eight days or bust" (p. 256); the fuel cells (pp. 213 and 257); the pad shutdown of 12 December 1965 and the dust cap (pp. 283 to 285); the rendezvous, 40 m and no relative motion at 2:33 p.m. EST, 0.30 to 90 m for more than three revolutions, and Vostok III and IV's "two bullets" (pp. 287 and 288); the foreword's summary of the programme | Says Liberty Bell 7's hatch was blown by "a faulty circuit" (p. 233), where *This New Ocean* says the cause was never found: the two are given as a dispute. Times are EST |
| NASA, *Astronaut Fact Book* (Johnson Space Center, updated January 2023) | [PDF](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/08/astronaut-fact-book-jan-2023.pdf), read as text extracted from it | Every NASA astronaut's birth date and place, flights and death; the groups (section 3.0); the Congressional Space Medal of Honor citations (section 4.0) | Deaths after January 2023 are not in it (Borman, Stafford, Lovell, Mattingly, Anders). It prints Gordon Cooper's death date under Richard Gordon as well |
| NASA JSC biographical data sheets: Alan B. Shepard, Jr. (September 1998), Virgil I. Grissom (December 1997), Walter M. Schirra, James A. McDivitt (December 1994), Edward H. White, II (December 1997), Charles Conrad, Jr., Thomas P. Stafford, Frank Borman, James A. Lovell | nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2016/01/<surname>_<first name>.pdf (Conrad's: /2019/11/conrad_charles.pdf), read as text extracted from each | Careers before, during and after NASA: Shepard's ear and his return to flight in May 1969; Schirra "the only Astronaut to have flown Mercury, Gemini, and Apollo"; Lovell "the first man to journey twice to the moon" and his record for time in space | Each is as of its date |
| NASA pages on Glenn ("John H. Glenn", nasa.gov/people/john-glenn), Carpenter ("M. Scott Carpenter"), Cooper ("Leroy Gordon Cooper, Jr.") and Young ("Former Astronaut John W. Young") | nasa.gov, by title | Glenn's combat missions, the 1957 record, the Senate and STS-95; Carpenter's SEALAB II; Cooper "the first man to make a second orbital flight"; Young "the first person to fly in space six times" | NASA's Cooper pages also call him the first astronaut to sleep in space, which Titov had done in 1961: not used |
| NASA releases on the deaths of Frank Borman (9 November 2023: 7 November), Thomas Stafford (March 2024: 18 March) and Jim Lovell (August 2025: 7 August) | nasa.gov/news-release, by title | The dates, which the fact book is too early for | Read through the search engine's summary of each page |
| M. Neufeld, "First American In Space: The Flight of Alan B. Shepard", Smithsonian National Air and Space Museum, 5 May 2021 | airandspace.si.edu/stories/editorial/first-american-space-flight-alan-b-shepard | Freedom 7's name, "and added a 7 for the seven astronauts"; Shepard grounded by an inner-ear condition | |
| NASA Science, "About Explorer 1" | science.nasa.gov/mission/explorer-1/about | The launch at 10:48 p.m. EST on 31 January 1958 | Read through the search engine's summary |

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Hacker and Grimwood, *On the Shoulders of Titans*, NASA SP-4203 (1977), read again for Gemini 8 to 12: the foreword; pp. 233 (the names), 304 to 321 (Gemini VIII), 323 to 339 (See and Bassett; Gemini IX-A), 341 to 350 (Gemini X), 354 to 370 (Gemini XI), 372 to 382 (Gemini XII); Appendix B, pp. 526 to 529 | [NTRS 19780012208](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/citations/19780012208), read as text extracted from it | Each flight told in order with its elapsed times; thruster No. 8 and the evaluation team's "probably caused by an electrical short" (p. 321); the backup crew taking over Gemini IX, "the first time in the agency's manned space flight history" (p. 325); the angry alligator (pp. 333, 334); Cernan's fogged faceplate, 128 of 167 minutes (p. 339); the restraints, 9 on IX-A and 44 on XII, and the underwater training (p. 373); "Later spacecraft were officially referred to by a Roman numeral, although a few had nicknames as well" (p. 233); launch times in e.s.t., apogees and durations (Appendix B) | Printed page = PDF page − 20 in the flight chapters. Appendix B gives Gemini X's highest apogee as 753.3 km where p. 345's text has 763 km. Times are e.s.t. (UTC−5); the dates of events after launch are derived from elapsed times. Collins (died 2021) is missing from the deceased list in both editions |
| NASA, "Eugene A. Cernan" | nasa.gov/people/eugene-a-cernan/ | His birth, Gemini 9 as "the second American to walk in space", Apollo 10 and 17, his retirement on 1 July 1976 and after | Gives the month of his death, not the day |

### Apollo

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Richard W. Orloff, *Apollo by the Numbers: A Statistical Reference*, NASA SP-2000-4029 (2000) | [PDF, whole book](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/04/sp-4029.pdf); read as text extracted from it (printed page = PDF page − 10) | **Every Apollo flight's events to the second in GMT**, in a timeline per mission: Apollo 1's fire (pp. 1 to 8, 23:31 GMT); the launches (Range Zero); Apollo 8's translunar injection (p. 48) and lunar orbit insertion (p. 49); Apollo 11's landing at 20:17:39 GMT and first step at 02:56:15 GMT (pp. 94, 107, 108); Apollo 12's lightning (pp. 113, 128) and Surveyor III (pp. 116, 118); Apollo 13's bang at 03:07:53 GMT on 14 April (p. 154), the lifeboat (p. 140), the cause (p. 146), splashdown (p. 143); Apollo 15's rover (p. 207); Apollo 17's liftoff from the Moon at 22:54:37 GMT (p. 263). Each crew member's birth date and place (the chapter openings). **"Call Signs", p. 282: why each command and lunar module was named**, after Lattimer's unpublished *Astronaut Mission Patches and Spacecraft Callsigns* | Two columns run together in the extracted text: read the page whole before quoting. Some death dates differ by a day from NASA JSC's (Eisele 1 December 1987 where JSC has 2 December; Evans 7 April 1990 where JSC has 6 April). Calls Aquarius "the Egyptian god", which it is not. Fra Mauro is called "highlands" (p. 163), which bears on whether Apollo 16 was the first highland landing |
| Courtney G. Brooks, James M. Grimwood and Loyd S. Swenson, Jr., *Chariots for Apollo: A History of Manned Lunar Spacecraft*, NASA SP-4205 (1979) | [PDF, whole book](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2023/03/sp-4205.pdf); read as text extracted from it, printed pages as the book numbers them | The programme's start (p. xiii: "a program, later named Apollo", July 1960) and end (p. xiv: Skylab, Apollo-Soyuz); Kennedy's goal (p. 29); lunar-orbit rendezvous announced on 11 July 1962 (p. 85); Mueller's all-up testing (p. 130); the Apollo 1 test and fire (pp. 213 and 214) and the name Apollo 1 (p. 231 note); Apollo 4 (p. 232); **the end of spacecraft names after Molly Brown and their return on Apollo 9, Gumdrop and Spider (p. 292)**; Schweickart's illness and the EVA kept to one daylight period (pp. 294 to 296) | Ends with Apollo 11. Says officials "turned down" Molly Brown, yet the name was used at launch (*On the Shoulders of Titans*, p. 233) |
| NASA, *Astronaut Fact Book* (Johnson Space Center, updated January 2023) | nasa.gov, *Astronaut Fact Book*; read as text extracted from the PDF | Every NASA astronaut's birth date and place, flights and death date, as of January 2023 | Deaths after January 2023 (Mattingly, Anders) are not in it |
| NASA JSC Biographical Data sheets: Anders, Cunningham, Duke, Eisele, Evans, Haise, Irwin, Mattingly, Mitchell, Roosa, Schmitt, Schweickart, Worden | nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2016/01/<surname>_<first name>.pdf (Mattingly's: /2023/11/mattingly-thomas.pdf); read as text extracted from each | Careers before, during and after NASA, in NASA's words | Written at different dates, some long ago; "present" positions are of then |
| NASA releases on deaths: "Apollo Astronaut Walter Cunningham Dies at 90" (3 January 2023); NASA Science, "Remembering Bill Anders" (June 2024: 7 June 2024); "Family Release Regarding the Passing of Apollo, Skylab Astronaut Alan Bean" (May 2018); "NASA Administrator Remembers Apollo-Era Astronaut Edgar Mitchell" (February 2016); "NASA Administrator Statement on Apollo Astronaut Al Worden" (18 March 2020) | nasa.gov and science.nasa.gov, by title | Death dates and places | Found by search; Cunningham's, Mitchell's, Bean's and Worden's read through the search engine's summary of the page, Anders's on the page |
| NASA, "Earthrise by NASA Astronaut Bill Anders" (image article) | nasa.gov/image-article/earthrise-by-nasa-astronaut-bill-anders/ | That Anders took it, on 24 December 1968 | The frame number, AS08-14-2383, is only in the file's name |

chapters on Sputnik, Vostok, Voskhod, Soyuz 1 to 11, the N1 and Salyut; the rows below are what was new.

| Source | Where | Good for | Watch out for |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |

The extracted text garbles some dates (Nikolayev's birth reads 09-25-29; Spacefacts has 5 September). Moscow time was UTC+3 throughout |
| Siddiqi, *Challenge to Apollo*, pp. 167 to 174, 253, 265 to 283, 292 and 293, 353 to 372, 385, 409 to 460, 525 to 529, 571 to 589, 627 and 628, 659 to 674, 681 to 693, 702 to 728, 770 to 783 | as above | Sputnik and Sputnik 2; Belka and Strelka; Korabl-Sputnik 5; the concealment of Gagarin's landing for the record (p. 283); Vostok 2 timed by Khrushchev before the Berlin Wall (p. 292); Popovich's "thunderstorm" code (p. 360); Mishin on the group flight's myth (p. 361); the women's selection and Kamanin's "a Gagarin in a skirt" (pp. 353 and 362); who ordered Voskhod, disputed (p. 385); Voskhod's design (pp. 409 to 414); Leonov out at 11:34:51 (p. 455); Korolev's death; Luna 9; Kosmos-133; Soyuz 1 and the two accounts of its parachute (pp. 581 to 589); Gagarin's death (pp. 627 and 628); Soyuz 3; Soyuz 4 and 5 (pp. 670 to 674); the N1 failures; Soyuz 6, 7 and 8 and the welding; Soyuz 9; Salyut's name (p. 773); Soyuz 10; Soyuz 11 (pp. 776 to 783) | Written in 2000: Laika's death is put on the fourth day, which Malashenkov's account of 2002 (not read) puts within hours. | Spacefacts (Joachim Becker), cosmonaut biographies: Titov, Nikolayev, Popovich, Bykovsky, Feoktistov, Leonov (leonov_aleksei), Shatalov, Volynov, Yeliseyev, Kubasov, Filipchenko, Gorbatko, Sevastyanov, Rukavishnikov, Tereshkova | spacefacts.de/bios/cosmonauts/english/<name>.htm | Births and places, **deaths after 2000** (Titov 2000, Nikolayev 2004, Popovich and Feoktistov 2009, Sevastyanov 2010, Kubasov 2014, Gorbatko 2017, Bykovsky and Leonov 2019, Shatalov 2021, Filipchenko 2022), each one's later flights | A compiled reference, secondary. No death is listed for Volynov or Yeliseyev (read November 2026), so `died` is left out for them; that is the page's silence, not a confirmation. The site rate-limits quickly |
| Spacefacts, mission pages for Soyuz 1, 3 to 11 | spacefacts.de/mission/english/soyuz-<n>.htm | **The call signs**: Ruby (Рубин), Argon, Amur, Baikal, Antaeus (Антей), Buran, Granit, Sokol, Granit, Yantar; launch, docking and landing times in UTC | The call sign stands under the title unlabelled. Yachmennikova, «"Кедр", "Кречет", "Бурлак": как и какие позывные выбирают себе космонавты», *Российская газета*, 24 August 2025 | [rg.ru](https://rg.ru/2025/08/24/kedr-krechet-burlak-kak-i-kakie-pozyvnye-vybiraiut-sebe-kosmonavty.html) | That the first cosmonauts' call signs were mostly strong birds (Орёл, Сокол, Беркут, Ястреб), Кедр and Чайка the best known; that Soyuz crews still fly (MS-26, MS-28) | A newspaper; it does not say who chose the early call signs, or why |

| --- | --- |
| D. Malashenkov, "Some Unknown Pages of the Living Organism's First Orbital Flight", World Space Congress, Houston, October 2002 | When Laika died: within hours (as reported) or on the fourth day (Siddiqi, 2000) |
| The FAI's record files for Vostok 1 (record 9326 and the two beside it), and its own pages on archive.fai.org (not reached) | What the FAI was told of the landing, and what it ratified |
| Anything on who chose the Vostok and Soyuz call signs, and why | Whether a cosmonaut picked his own. Some stayed with their man (Komarov's Рубин twice, Nikolayev's Сокол twice) and some did not (Shatalov was Амур, then Гранит twice) |

## Apollo 7 — exterior study (November 2026)

| Source | Link | Read / used | Limits |
|---|---|---|---|
| NASA, *Apollo 7 press kit*, release 68-168K, 6 October 1968 | [NASA PDF](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/static/apollo50th/pdf/A07_PressKit.pdf) | pp. 25–33; illustrations 25a–25d and 31a: CSM-101 and SA-205 envelopes, five CM windows, four SM RCS quads, no LM, Saturn IB clustered tanks and eight fins | Preflight, rounded envelopes. Surface stations, panel patterns and nozzle profiles estimated from drawings; no flight physics derived from planned events |
| NASA, *Apollo by the Numbers*, Apollo 7 summary | [NASA](https://www.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/static/history/SP-4029/Apollo_07a_Summary.htm) | Launch at 15:02:45 GMT on 11 October 1968; SA-205 and CSM-101 | Flight timeline not implemented |
| NASA photograph S68-48788, Apollo 7 launch, 11 October 1968 | [NASA Apollo 50th photo gallery](https://www.nasa.gov/specials/apollo50th/photos.html) | Alternating black fuel / white oxygen tanks, black roll markings and instrument-unit band on SA-205 | Paint extents and lettering positions estimated; photograph is not a dimensioned drawing |

| Moseley and Wells, *Wind-Tunnel Investigation of the Aerodynamic Pressures on the Apollo Launch Escape Vehicle Configuration*, NASA TN D-5686 (April 1970) | [NTRS PDF](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19700014969/downloads/19700014969.pdf) | Model description, Table I and Figure 2(g), p. 13: nominal 154-inch maximum diameter, 184.8-inch aft radius, 7.7-inch shoulder radius and 33° cone wall; C2 described as final CM design | Common aerodynamic reference, not an as-built CM-101 drawing. Nose shape not used to invent a forward heat shield or hatch |

SC-101 couch-position sheet identified for further cabin work | Earlier configuration; station references can exclude ablative material. Does not by itself provide every exterior measurement or equipment fit for flight |
| Smithsonian Institution, NASM, CM-107 exterior survey | [Scene document](https://3d-api.si.edu/content/document/3d_package:d8c6457e-4ebc-11ea-b77f-2e728ce88125/document.json), [rights metadata](https://ids.si.edu/ids/media_view?id=3d_package%3Ad8c6457e-4ebc-11ea-b77f-2e728ce88125&format=text) | Four measured shared Block II window aperture outlines, retained in physical centimetres; exact derivative URL and hash in the model's reference JSON | Apollo 11, not Apollo 7. Applied as assumed shared structure, not CM-101 measurements. No photographic textures, post-entry damage or equipment copied. Curved side hatch rejected from planar extraction; forward cover and cabin not reconstructed from this data |

Other plates read for research are not automatically verified flight configuration |

Earlier CM height reference is not used as final outer heat-shield height |

Rounded overview is not used for exit diameter; intermediate bell contour remains provisional |

Later inner-door machining excluded |

Smith, *Apollo Experience Report—Spacecraft Structure Subsystem*, NASA TN D-7780, October 1974 | [NTRS PDF](https://ntrs.nasa.gov/api/citations/19740026226/downloads/19740026226.pdf) | Printed p. 12: unified Block II hatch and SC106+ inner-slab weight-reduction machining | Figure 17 labels top Xc104.5, conflicting with ST-428A's Xc112.25.
